Preface 

THIS

HIS elementary treatise is intended as a non-technical aid to the real estate broker in dealing with his everyday problems and negotiations with his clients. The author has attempted to give to the reader a concise exposition of the broker's rights, duties and responsibilities to those persons whom he represents, and also to owners those corresponding obligations which are due the broker. 

Since the Act of May 1, 1929, P. L. 1216, every person desiring to act as a real estate broker or salesman must be licensed, and it is important that he be familiar with all the terms and provisions of the Statute under which he is operating. Ignorance of the law is, of course, no plea for the broker to escape the punitive provisions of the Act. The author has, therefore, attempted to interpret and explain fully each section of this important legislation in the light of experiences gathered in the administration of the Statute since its enactment.

The book gives an analysis of this law, together with formal opinions of the Attorney General and decisions of the Department of Public Instruction on hearings held by the Department. Much credit is given to the Attorney General, Honorable William 

A. Schnader, Dr. James N. Rule, Superintendent of Public Instruction, and Deputy Attorney General,

Honorable Penrose Hertzler, for their ever-ready assistance in passing upon questions submitted in convii

viii 

Preface nection with the administration and enforcement of the Act.

The main part of the book concerns the law of real estate brokerage and fundamental principles of the law of landlord and tenant. The author believes that students in schools of business administration, pursuing a course in Real Estate Law and Real Estate Practice, will find the book a valuable aid in their study of these subjects. The author does not believe that there now exists in Pennsylvania a treatise devoted exclusively to the subjects of brokerage and leases which is sufficiently concise and clear to enable the average real estate dealer and real estate owner to understand it readily. This work is intended to meet this need.

ROBERT W. SEMENOW

Introduction 

M OST persons engaged in the real estate business do a general business and devote some time and effort to brokerage. Many real estate operators emphasize this phase of real estate activity almost exclusively. Most sub-dividers will do a brokerage business in connection with their major activity, for brokerage constitutes a lucrative source of income to the real estate dealer. Generally, the only prerequisite is a real estate license, which can be obtained upon the payment of nominal charges. It is not strange, therefore, that many individuals who have failed to make the grade successfully in other business pursuits have turned to the real estate business as a means of recouping their lost fortunes. The initial investment and overhead is usually comparatively small. 

Desk space is sometimes all that is needed. These brokers, actuated in the main by motives of monetary gain, with little heed or concern for the interests of the buying and selling clientele, are none too scrupulous in the business methods and ethics which they employ. Sharp practices, fly-by-night operators, and misplaced brokers have tended in the past to place the real estate brokerage business, as well as the other phases of real estate business, in an unsavory light in the minds of the buying public. Organizations of local, state and national real estate boards have done much to force undesirable operators out of the business and to educate brokers and buyers to accepted standards and ethics of the business. The real estate xi

xii 

Introduction license law, more than any other agency, has eliminated these undesirables.

While these organizations are of great force in placing the real estate business upon a high plane, nevertheless, it has not yet reached the standards of the traditional professions, and it is not likely to become really professionalized for some time to come. 

Many individuals, even to-day, refuse to accept an economic justification for the existence of the real estate brokerage business, although trained economists do not subscribe to this view. Prior to the twentieth century many economists complained and argued that the real estate broker was a total loss so far as the community good was concerned. The contention was that the broker was a non-productive element in the organization of society. Carpenters and cobblers were productive people. They produced concrete articles necessary to the comfort of the community which they served. One could visualize their products. They should, therefore, be paid for their work. The real estate broker, apparently, made nothing. Was there any pay due him? It seemed that he was a person who happened to be around when property was sold by one person to another. He received pay in the form of a commission and it was grudgingly given him. Legally he was entitled to it, but there seemed to be no economic justification for it. 

People were not educated to the idea that service, as well as shoes or clothing, had a real, tangible value. 

That the realtor is of real value to his community is no longer doubted by economists. The broker who faithfully discharges his duties and responsibilities to his clients occupies a strategic position as a productive agency of economic goods. He is of tremen-

Introduction xiii dous influence in guiding the development of the community in which he operates. It is incumbent upon him to see that the property which he recommends for purchase to a prospective buyer is suitable for the purchaser's purposes and commensurate with his income. He must first ascertain and then give information as to whether or not it can be readily financed and as to whether or not the payments will be within the periodic income of his buyer. A purchaser who is induced to buy property through the rosy-hued representations and super-salesmanship methods of a broker, and finds, too late, that he has undertaken too much financially, must suffer his property to be taken away from him with the accompanying loss of several years' savings and much attendant worry. 

Such a disappointed purchaser will blame the broker for his misfortunes, and in many cases, with ample justification. He has no further use for the particular broker in the deal, nor, more generally, for any other persons connected with the real estate business. He states his opinions in no uncertain terms. The disappointed individual may not be particularly detrimental to the business of the offending broker or to that of brokers generally, but multiply the isolated case a number of times and the result is very discouraging to those who are exerting every effort to place the real estate business upon the higher business level which it merits. On the other hand, a broker mindful of his purchaser's financial limitations and an authority on the commodity in which he is trafficking will make certain that his client is placed within his means. 

Pride in satisfied home ownership is bound to result.

It is from the wide distribution of home ownership that a firmer and more solidified community will fol-

xiv 

Introduction low. Home ownership, with the consequent participation in taxes, means a more active participation in civic affairs. As individuals more and more directly bear the expenses of government, so do they become more and more interested in an economic and efficient administration of government. Better schools and streets, larger parks and more recreational space, and all of the finer wholesome things that make family life worth while will be given earnest consideration.

The realtor must lend every possible aid to this development. In this development the realtor who has taken an active role will profit directly as well as indirectly. 

To sum up then, a broker must be ever cognizant of his economic place in the community and of his moral responsibilities to that community. It sometimes happens, however, that an unscrupulous client may deprive an honest broker of his justly earned commission. The broker generally learns by his experiences and by those of his fellow brokers, but this proceeding is costly. The writer has, therefore, attempted to indicate in a non-technical way the principles of law supported by cases which control the relations of broker and owner, so that both parties may, in some measure, be aided in the discharge of their duties and responsibilities toward each other.

THE PENNSYLVANIA LAW OF REAL ESTATE BROKERAGE LANDLORD AND TENANT

CHAPTER I 

Duties of Broker to Owner

Duties of Agent 

F a real estate broker is to discharge creditably I the which devolve upon his office, it is necessary that he have a knowledge of certain cardinal and fundamental principles of law which affect his business in his everyday practice. Real estate brokerage represents a combination of the principles of the law of principal and agent and the law of contracts. 

A real estate broker is an agent in the fullest sense of the word, in that he represents another from whom he has derived his authority. The interests of three persons are thereby involved: that of the owner, of the broker, and of the purchaser with whom the broker negotiates. A knowledge of the law of principal and agent is essential in order to determine accurately the respective rights and liabilities of these three parties to the contract. A broker occupies a position of trust and confidence toward his principal, and there are certain important duties that every agent owes to his principal. The first of these is that he must be loyal to his trust. This duty embraces substantially all the others. To be loyal to his trust, an agent cannot so exercise his duties as to garner a profit for himself at the expense of his principal. The courts have decided that in order for a broker to be loyal to his trust he must not sell to himself, purchase for himself, or purchase from himself. Where 1

Duties of Broker to Owner a property is listed with a broker for sale, it is the duty of the broker to determine a fair market value for that property and list it at that price. A broker will not be permitted to purchase a property which has been listed with him for sale where he feels that the list price is below the normal market price and purchases it either in his own name or in the name of another person in trust for himself, and then resells the property at a profit. The reason for this rule is interestingly given in a New Jersey case in which the court said as follows:

Owing to the greed and selfishness of human nature there must, in the great mass of transactions, be a strong antagonism between the interests of the seller and the buyer, and universal experience shows that the average man, when his interests conflict with his employer's, will not look upon his employer's interests as more important and entitled to more consideration than his own.

And so, where a purchaser is given trust funds for the purchase of a particular property and takes title in his own name, he will be considered as a trustee for his principal, and the principal can bring proper court proceedings compelling the broker to convey the legal title to him. It makes no difference that the broker has squandered the trust funds that were entrusted to him and has purchased the property in his own name with funds derived from other sources. He will, nevertheless, be considered as a trustee for his principal.¹ Where a real estate broker has been given funds for the payment of taxes upon property of his principal, and at a sheriff's sale of said property for failure to pay the taxes it is purchased by the broker, such sale will operate to give the equitable title to his 

1 Edwards vs. Dooley, 120 N. Y. 540.

Duties of Broker to Owner 

3 principal who may resort to legal proceedings to reinvest himself of the legal title which he has lost. Another case illustrating the necessity for a real estate broker to observe scrupulously the duty of loyalty which he owes to his principal is that of Wilkinson vs.

McCullough, 196 Pa. 206. In this case, Mrs. McCullough was the owner of a piece of property in the 

City of Pittsburgh. T. C. Jenkins owned adjacent property, and was interested in the property owned by Mrs. McCullough. The broker had a conversation with Jenkins, in which he said he would buy the Mc- Cullough property if he could get it at a fair price. 

Jenkins did not want to appear personally in the matter, and the broker testified that Jenkins instructed his manager "to have me buy the property for him." The sale was presumably to McKean, who was Jenkins's manager. At the time of closing, Mrs. 

McCullough inquired as to whom the property would finally go. She was told that they could not tell her until after the paper was signed because she would jump the price. The broker Wilkinson then sued for his commission. The question involved is whether the broker acted with such good faith towards his principal as is required by law, and without which no claim for commission can be allowed. The court ruled that Wilkinson had violated the first fundamental principle of loyalty which he was bound to observe in the interests of his principal. Acting as agent for 

Mrs. McCullough, it was incumbent upon him to acquaint her with all information at his disposal which would influence her in disposing of her property. 

This he failed to do, and therefore forfeited all claims to commission. A real estate broker employed to find a purchaser for land is bound to disclose to his prin-

Duties of Broker to Owner cipal any known facts material to the transaction, and if he takes part in the negotiations, he is bound to exert his skill for the benefit of his principal. It matters not that there was no fraud meditated and no injury done, the courts have stated that the rule is not intended to be for remedial of actual wrongs, but for prevention of the possibility of such wrong. Good faith forbids the concealment of an arrangement intended for the advantage of the buyer rather than that of the seller whom the agent represents. A broker making a secret profit out of his employment violates his first duty to his principal. Where a principal gives a real estate broker authority to offer $90,- 000 for the purchase of a business property, and the broker subsequently enters into negotiations on his own account at a price of $85,000, and then attempts to sell the property to his principal for $95,000, the principal can compel the broker to convey the property to him at the price paid for it by the broker. The broker here failed to act in that good faith which he was bound to exercise, and attempted to make a secret profit of $10,000, which the court refused to allow. 

Whatever profit was acquired by the agent in acting under his agency authority, flows to his principle and the broker must convey the property to his principal.¹ 

In other words, the law will not permit an agent to take advantage of or to make a secret profit out of his employment. 

Must Obey Instructions

The second fundamental duty that an agent owes to his principal is that he must obey the instructions that are given him by his principal. If a broker un- 

1 Bacharach vs. Fleming, 269 Pa. 350.

Duties of Broker to Owner 5 dertakes to judge that he may depart from the instructions of the owner, and that such variation would not be material, he does so at his own peril, and should any loss result by reason of the agent's deviation from his given instructions, he will be personally liable. For example, where a broker is engaged to sell property on a cash basis but instead accepts notes which are later declared invalid, the agent will be held personally responsible for the resulting loss. The owner, however, must be specific in his instructions. 

In Keys vs. Johnson, 68 Pa. 42, the plaintiff broker gave in evidence a letter from the defendant (owner) in which he says to the plaintiff, "My farm at Penn Square, Montgomery County, I will sell on terms to suit the purchaser as follows: . . . ." The letter then describes the farm and expresses a will to sell as a whole or divided, describing the parts, naming the price for each, and continues, "If one purchaser takes the whole, the price will be $16,000." Hazelton, a prospective purchaser, wished to exchange city property. The broker said that the owner would not exchange as he wanted money. Hazelton then obtained the defendant's address from the plaintiff and called to see the defendant. After negotiating with him about a week, he took the farm at $17,000, giving him in exchange a house in Philadelphia. The question involved in this case is whether the broker departed from the instructions given to him so as to bar his right to commission. The court decided in this case that any variations which took place in the sale from the terms given to the broker were made by the owner himself, and that there was no deviation from instructions by the broker which would operate to prevent him from recovering his commission.

Duties of Broker to Owner 

Must Not Be Negligent

The third duty which every agent owes to his principal is that he must not be negligent in acting in the interests of his principal. An agent should not be dormant, but should actively and aggressively assert his employer's interests. An agent who is grossly negligent in the interests of his principal will be personally liable for any loss which the principal may sustain by reason of such negligence. This is discussed more fully later in connection with an agent's personal liability towards his principal. An attorney failing to disclose an encumbrance of record against a property which his principal is purchasing will be personally responsible to his principal for any damage sustained by the latter.¹ The negligence of the attorney in overlooking the encumbrances was a serious violation of the contract between himself and the pur- chaser.

Account for Money and Property 

Fourth, an agent must account for money and property of his principal. Money and property entrusted to his care should, in all cases, be kept separate and apart from his own funds. If a broker carelessly mingles his employer's funds with his own, and the bank in which the funds are on deposit should fail, the broker will be personally responsible for the loss. The account of the employer's funds should be kept as a trustee account, and so long as the agent exercises due care and caution in selecting a safe depository for such funds, he will not be personally responsible for any later loss. This matter is of par- 

1 Bodine vs. Wayne Title & Trust Co., Appellant, 33 Pa. Sup. Ct. 68.

Duties of Broker to Owner 7 ticular importance because not infrequently a real estate broker, by agreement between the parties, retains the hand money until such time as the deal is consummated. Due care in the handling of these funds is very important in so far as the broker and the contracting parties are concerned. 

Act in Person

Lastly, an agent must perform acts in person. He cannot delegate the authority which he has received from his principal to another. An owner employs a broker because of the confidence that he has in the ability and integrity of that particular person, and so a broker has no right to delegate his authority to another without consent or request of his principal, except as to matters which are of a purely ministerial or mechanical character, and where such delegation does not involve discretion, confidence or skill. A delegation of his authority may, however, be permitted by the usages of the trade. Where a nonresident owner of land employs an agent, also a nonresident, to sell the land, it will be presumed that such agent has authority to appoint a sub-agent in the locality where the land is located in order to facilitate the sale of such land.¹ Where, however, an owner knows that a broker employed by him to sell land has secured the services of a sub-agent by promising the sub-agent half commission from the owner and the owner assents thereto either expressly or by remaining silent when it is his duty to object if he has any objection, the owner is directly liable to the sub-agent for his share of the commission.2 

1 Eastland vs. Maney, 81 S. W. 574. 

2 Allen vs. Roach, 292 S. W. 194.

CHAPTER II Statutory Regulations of Business rules, while 

Tagents generally, are particularly applicable to real estate brokers in Pennsylvania by reason of the fact that the Supreme Court in this State, as early as 1850, established the principle that the real estate business is of such a public nature that it is a fit subject for regulation by the State. An act was passed requiring a broker to obtain a commission from the treasurer of the county wherein he resides and does business, which commission was his authority to exercise the business and occupation of a broker for the term of one year. The act also provided that any individual or co-partnership continuing to exercise the business or occupation of a broker should immediately pay in advance into the treasury of the proper county, for the use of the Commonwealth, the amount stipulated, and receive a new commission. This act was intended and originally applied only to stock exchange and bill brokers, but the legislature, on April 10, 1849, extended all the provisions of the act to real estate and merchandise brokers. The constitutionality of the act was then attacked on the grounds that it represented special class legislation and that it was prohibited by the State Constitution. The issue was decided in the case of Watson vs. Muirhead, 57 Pa. 167, in which case the Supreme Court

Statutory Regulations of Business 

9 sustained the validity of the statute in question, and in an opinion by Chief Justice Sharswood, said:

The business of a conveyancer is one of great importance and responsibility. It requires an acquaintance of the general principles of the law of real property and a large amount of practical knowledge, which can only be derived from experience. In England, it had been pursued by lawyers of the greatest prominence. As our titles become more complex with the increase of wealth and the desire which always accompanies it to continue it in our name and family, as long as the law will permit, it will become more and more necessary that gentlemen, prepared by a course of liberal education and previous study should devote themselves to it. 

There have been and still are such among us. The rule of liability for errors of judgment as applied to them ought to be the same as men engaged in the practice of law or medicine. It is not a mere art, but a science. 

This represents the first attempt to place the real estate business upon a higher business level. Shortly afterwards, the legislature passed an act requiring all brokers, in addition to taking out a license, to pay an annual fee, for the use of the Commonwealth, based on their commissions, discounts, allowances and other sources of income in the transaction of their business.

Mercantile Appraisers Act 

The Act of May 7th, 1907 (P. L. 175), provides that the mercantile appraiser or his duly authorized deputy shall visit the store, office or other place of business of every broker, agent and factor, and, at the time of such visit, require each broker, agent, and factor to make a return, under oath or affirmation, of his, their, or its gross annual receipts from commissions or other earnings in the transaction of

Statutory Regulations of Business his, their or its business during the preceding calendar year; and the appraiser, or any deputy appointed by him, is hereby empowered to administer an oath or affirmation for that purpose. 

The basis of assessment shall be the gross receipts and earnings during the previous calendar year; except where no business was transacted by said broker, agent, or factor during said previous year, when he, they, or it shall be liable for the minimum license tax of ten dollars. If the mercantile appraiser is dissatisfied with the return so made by the said broker, agent, or factor, he shall ascertain and assess the said license tax according to such sum as, in his best judgment, he believes to be correct. He shall also leave a written or printed notice to be prepared and furnished by the auditor general, specifying the amount of the license money to be paid by such person to the Commonwealth, and also when and where an appeal will be held as required by law. Any broker, agent or factor who is dissatisfied with the assessment so made shall have the right of appeal to the mercantile appraiser and county treasurer in all counties, except where there is a board of mercantile appraisers, in which case the board shall hear all appeals. If the broker, agent, or factor is still dissatisfied with the finding of the mercantile appraiser and county treasurer, or board of appraisers, he, they, or it shall have the right of appeal to the court of common pleas of the proper county, within ten days; which appeal shall set forth the date and amount of such assessment, the date of the decision of the appeal board, and the facts in detail relied upon to obtain a reversal of said decision. The appellant shall, within five days after taking said appeal, serve upon the county treasurer

Statutory Regulations of Business 

11 and the mercantile appraiser, or board of appraisers, copies of his said appeal, including the court, term, and number of the case in which the appeal is taken. 

Upon failure to comply with any of the provisions of this section, in whole or in part, said appeal shall, upon motion, be stricken off. The burden of proof upon such appeal shall be upon the appellant. If any person fails to attend the said appeal before the mercantile appraiser and county treasurer, or board of mercantile appraisers, or the court, he shall not thereafter be permitted, in a suit for the recovery of said license tax, to set up a defense either that he is not a broker, agent or factor, or any other ground of defense which might have been heard and determined either by said mercantile appraiser and county treasurer, board of appraisers, or the court of common pleas, on appeal, as aforesaid. 

The rate of assessment is as follows: $10.00 for those whose gross annual receipts are less than $5,000.00; $25.00 for those whose gross annual receipts are from $5,000.00 to $10,000.00; $50.00 for those whose gross annual receipts are from $10,000.00 to $20,000.00; and $100.00 for those whose gross annual receipts are $20,000.00 or upwards.

License Requirements 

Although there have been numerous attempts by real estate organizations in the State of Pennsylvania to have the State Legislature pass a statute requiring persons to pass an examination successfully before engaging in the real estate business, the efforts of these groups to date have not met with success. 

But a person engaged in the real estate business must obtain a license from the mercantile appraiser of the

Statutory Regulations of Business county in which he is operating. This license is in addition to the one required under the act of May 1, 1929 (P. L. 1216).

Failure to obtain a license will prevent him from recovering any commissions. In this connection, it is important to note that he must be licensed at the time that a sale of property is negotiated. For example, A may have been a licensed real estate broker for a number of years. Let us assume that his license fee was paid until May 1, 1928. In June, 1928, he negotiated a sale of real estate. He neglected to pay his license fee for 1928 until August of that year. In an action to recover commission on the above sale, the courts hold in analogous cases that the test is whether or not the broker was licensed at the actual time the sale was made. Under the stated facts, A could not recover.¹ Where a real estate broker is engaged in business under a fictitious or assumed name, it is necessary for him to register with the prothonotary of the county where the business is located, and also with the Secretary of State. Where, for example,

John Steel is engaging in the real estate business under the name of John Steel Company, it would be necessary for this concern to register in compliance with the law and to divulge the identity of the persons making up the firm. However, if the firm name were Steel & Stoner, and the concern consisted of two individuals, John Steel and George Stoner, registration would not be necessary. The concern is not holding itself out to the public as consisting of more persons than the two given in the trade name. Failure to register subjects the concern to a fine, but failure to register in itself will not operate to prevent such a 

1 Luce Appellant vs. Cooke, 227 Pa. 224.

Statutory Regulations of Business 

13 concern from collecting commission in a transaction.

The courts have held, prior to the act of May 1, 1929, that an individual negotiating a sale of real estate in a single, isolated case is not to be considered as a real estate broker engaged in the business, and a license, therefore, is not necessary in order to permit a recovery of commission. The reason advanced is that he has not acted as a general real estate broker, but as a duly authorized agent for the owner in a particular transaction. The question as to the right to recover commission in a single instance was frequently raised during and immediately following the late war. During the real estate "booms" that swept the country, many persons engaged in different lines of work endeavored to supplement their income by selling real estate. This group included house wives, railroad engineers, and even stenographers. The courts uniformly held that, if the person negotiating the sale acted in a single instance, the commission for services could be recovered on the theory that the person negotiating the sale acted as the agent of the owner, rather than as a general real estate broker. Where, however, the person attempted to collect a subsequent commission under similar circumstances, the courts refused a recovery on the theory that the person then became a general real estate broker, and failure to have a license was a fatal bar to recovery. Most of these opportunist brokers have been eliminated by section 2 (a) of the Act of 1929 which specifically provides that one act shall constitute one a real estate broker, and make him amenable to the provisions of the Act. A person employed in the real estate department of a trust company, who

Statutory Regulations of Business personally sells a particular piece of real estate without a license, will not be prevented from recovering a commission for that particular sale. The contract of employment is here again considered a special one, and a license is not necessary to give it validity.¹ 

The apparent exception to the general rule that a broker cannot recover commission unless he has a license at the time the sale takes place is when an owner employs the broker without a license to sell property for him, and in the course of such employment, authorizes the expenditure of such money and time as to make it a real hardship upon the owner to prevent a recovery.

The law will not lend its aid to an unscrupulous employer to defraud his agent out of a just reward if he fails to take out a license. The evidence of the agent's authority and instructions regarding the expenditure of money must be clear and unequivocal in order to permit a recovery, and, in any event, the court would only permit the recovery of money actually expended. 

In the absence of a license, it is exceedingly difficult for an agent ordinarily to present clear and convincing evidence of the acts of his principal of such a nature as to allow him to set up a plea for compensation; and, as a general rule, the agent usually has small chance of being able to collect his commission. 

1 Woods vs. Herron, 229 Pa. 625. Also Webb vs. Rachmil, 75 Pa. 

Sup. Ct. 193. Changed by Act of 1929.

CHAPTER III Recovery of Commission-Contract of 

Employment 

Recovery of Commission

N order that a broker be permitted to recover commission, it is necessary for him to prove two things: (1) that he had a contract of employment, and (2) that in pursuance of that contract of employment he produced a purchaser ready, able and willing to buy the property on the seller's terms, who was acceptable to the seller, and who actually contracted, in writing, at a price satisfactory to the owner.

While it is true that ordinarily contracts for the sale of real estate must be in writing, nevertheless a contract of employment by an owner giving authority to a broker to sell his property need not be in writing. A verbal agreement in this connection is just as binding and of as great legal efficacy as a written contract. For obvious reasons, a written contract is always preferable to an oral one. The duration of human life is at best uncertain. One of the parties to the transaction may die, and it is a rule of evidence that a party in interest to a contract may not testify in a matter to which the other party is deceased. 

Again, so long as the memories of the parties coincide as to what was originally agreed upon, everything goes smoothly, but as soon as their recollections differ—then, in the absence of disinterested wit- 15

nesses—there is no way of conclusively proving what was actually agreed upon. 

Contract of Employment 

The contract of employment must possess all the essential elements of a valid contract. It must be definite; it must be free from fraud, misrepresentation, or undue influence. The object of the contract must be legal and the parties must be competent. In this last connection, it might be well to note that a contract of employment by an owner with an agent who is under twenty-one years of age (an infant at law) is binding upon the owner. On the other hand, a contract of employment by an owner who is a minor may be avoided by him at any time before arriving at his majority, or within a reasonable time thereafter. Minority is a personal defense which can be pleaded only by the infant.

The usual method of effecting a contract of employment is for the owner to leave a description of the premises to be sold with the broker, together with the terms upon which the property is to be sold. 

Where the property is left for sale by the owner himself, the question of employment is seldom raised. 

Where the employment is not by the owner in person but by a third party, ostensibly acting for the owner, it is necessary for the broker to ascertain that such person was authorized by the owner to employ him to perform this particular service. If the broker fails to investigate this presumed agency, he may find that not only has he lost considerable time and money, but in addition he has made himself the defendant in an action brought by the disappointed purchaser, where he signs the agreements for his

alleged principal under his presumed authority. Certainly the agent can maintain an action against the person who acted as the representative of the owner, but if the latter is financially irresponsible or has left for parts unknown, the disappointed broker will be without redress. Where a husband leaves property which is owned by the wife for sale with a broker, the broker may successfully maintain an action against the husband, if the broker can show that he has produced a satisfactory purchaser 'in accordance with the terms of the sale which were left with him. It is well to note here that a broker will not be deprived of his commission where he has procured a satisfactory purchaser and it develops that title to the property is taken in the name of one other than the purchaser produced by the real estate broker. If the grantee takes the property merely as a trustee for a purchaser produced by the broker, or if the purpose of the arrangement is to deprive the agent of his earned commission, the courts will compel the owner to compensate the broker, following the agreed-upon terms. 

Rate of Commission

If, in the contract of employment, nothing is said in regard to the rate of commission, then the broker is entitled to a reasonable rate of return, which rate is the one used generally in the business in the particular locality in which the property is situated. It is assumed here, of course, that the broker is licensed. 

Since an owner entrusts his property for sale to a person whose ordinary business it is to sell real estate on a commission basis, the law presumes, in the absence of any agreement to the contrary, that commission or compensation is to be paid for the services

rendered. On urban properties, this commission is usually three per cent, and on rural properties and vacant property, the rate is five per cent. This rate is not fixed by any legal statute, but is a matter of custom or trade usage. In order to avoid any controversy or future litigation, it is always desirable that the rate of compensation should be agreed upon in advance, at the time of the employment. There is no provision in the Act of 1929 regulating or attempting to regulate the rate of commission to be charged. It is one of voluntary agreement between the parties. 

What Constitutes Employment 

Where a broker performs his contract under an express contract of employment, there is little danger of such broker's being deprived of his commission. Lacking an express contract of employment, however, a broker under certain conditions may prove a valid contract of employment by circumstances amounting to ratification or adoption on the part of the owner of an unauthorized contract of employment. Where the principal, by his action, has taken advantage of or appropriated the benefits of the services of the broker under such circumstances that would render the withholding of a remuneration inequitable, the courts will recognize and enforce the broker's claim to commission. Suppose A, a broker, should meet B, a property owner, upon the street and inquire of him whether he would sell a certain piece of property which he owned. B replied that if A could sell the property so as to net him $20,000.00, he (the agent) could have any excess which he could secure over and above that amount. Some months afterwards, C went to the owner with a letter of introduction from A and pur-

chased the property for $15,500. A brought suit against B for the recovery of his commission on the grounds that he had been employed to sell the property and had furnished the purchaser. The whole case hinges on the question as to whether or not there existed a contract of employment. The facts show that there had been no employment of the broker by the owner, and that the latter had merely authorized the former to sell the property if he could get $20,000 for him. On the other hand, if the owner had actually employed the broker to sell the property, and had even fixed the price at $20,000, below which price the agent could not sell it, and later on the owner sold the property at a lower price to a purchaser produced by the broker, the broker would nevertheless have been entitled to a commission, although a sale took place at a price less than the stipulated minimum of $20,000. The second case differs from the preceding one in that there was an actual contract of employment, which did not exist in the immediately preceding case. 

Minimum Net Sale Price

Where the owner has fixed a minimum net price, below which the agent may not sell the property, the question frequently arises as to whether or not the broker is entitled to any excess realized over and above the net price. In other words, assume that an owner has left property for sale with a broker, with the understanding that the property is not to be sold for less than $10,000, and the broker negotiates a sale at a price of $12,000. The question arises as to the distribution of the $2,000 excess. It must be remembered here that the first duty that an agent owes

to his principal is that he must be loyal to his trust, that he must do everything possible to assert and protect his principal's interest; and so the courts have held that under the circumstances which have just been outlined, the $2,000 belongs to the owner. This is so unless it is expressly stipulated that the broker is to retain the excess as his commission. In Fulton vs. Walters, 216 Pa. 56, Walters asked Fulton to find a purchaser for his land, offering him all in excess of $275 per acre, and an additional fee of $500 for his services. Fulton did subsequently find a purchaser, and the land was transferred. Walters seeks to evade payment of the $500, and wishes to ascertain the exact purchase price paid to Fulton by Martin, the purchaser. The question arose as to whether there was the relation of ordinary principal and agent, or whether there was any relation of trust or confidence that would require Fulton to tell Walters what profit he was actually making on the deal. The Court decided that there was no such relationship between the two parties, whereby Fulton would owe any duty to 

Walters other than what their contract called for.

There is no principle of law that obliges one who has made an honest contract with another to communicate to that other anything subsequently happening that might be an inducement to that other to repudiate his contract.

In order for a broker to recover commission under a contract requiring a net price to the owner, he must procure a purchaser at a price sufficiently in excess of the net price to cover commissions. In Dethloff vs. 

Starbuck, 155 North Eastern 552 (Ohio), an owner of real estate verbally employed a broker to sell real estate at a fixed price of $9,000 net to the owner,

the broker to receive his commission out of the excess of the sale price over $9,000. The broker then brought his prospective purchaser and the owner together for a conference. The first negotiations fell through because the purchaser refused to pay $9,000 for the property and the owner refused to reduce the price. A few days later, the owner requested the broker to see the buyer again and secure another meeting of the parties, nothing being said by either the broker or the owner about commission at this time. The broker then arranged another meeting of the three parties at the home of the broker. The sale price was the main question discussed there. The owner and the buyer finally agreed on a sale price of $8,500, whereupon the buyer asked the owner, in the presence of the broker, if any commission was to be paid to the broker, and the owner replied that he would take care of the commission.

Had the owner then taken the position, not that he would take care of the commission, but that he had taken care of the broker by his original contract, that is to say, that the broker could only get a commission in case the property sold for a sum in excess of $9,000, and that nothing was due the broker as the sale price was only $8,500, the broker would have been obliged then and there to assert his right to a commission, and for him to have remained silent under such circumstances would have amounted to a waiver of his right to collect a commission from either party. Had the owner then taken such a position, that might easily have defeated the sale. The buyer might not have consented to pay the owner $8,500, and then incur the risk of being obliged to pay the broker a commission. When the owner said in the presence of

the broker that he would take care of the commission, that satisfied the buyer so far as commission was concerned. It also satisfied the broker, and it facilitated the closing of the deal between the owner and the buyer, which might otherwise have failed of completion.

It is claimed here, on the part of the owner, that he had a definite contract that the broker was not to have any commission except that which he could procure out of a sale price in excess of $9,000, and that is not disputed by the broker. The contract was entirely definite and certain between the owner and the broker, and, had nothing further taken place between the parties, the broker could not have recovered any commission.

That which took place at the second meeting of the parties clearly amounted to a new contract between the owner and the broker with respect to commission, and that contract was quite as valid as the first one, and quite as plainly sustained by a valid consideration, to wit, the effecting of the sale at the new price at which the owner was willing to sell.

It would be manifestly unjust to hold that the owner might say that which would lead both the buyer and the broker to think that the owner intended thereafter to take care of the commission with the broker, and thus have no question arise that might defeat the sale, and then permit the owner to step back into the position taken in the first contract that he made with the broker by denying the right of the broker to receive any commission at all because the sale price had not exceeded $9,000.¹ 

1 See also Boysen vs. Robertson, 68 S. W. 243. Haven et al. vs. Tartar, 102 S. W. 21.

Employment of Several Brokers 

It has become a common practice for an owner to leave property for sale with a number of real estate brokers. When this is done, the rule of law is that they possess concurrent authority to sell, and that the sale of the property by one of them terminates the agencies of the others by removing the subject matter of the contract. It is advisable for an owner listing a property for sale with a number of real estate brokers to inform each broker that the property has been listed for sale with other concerns, and that upon the sale of the property by one, the employment of the others shall automatically cease. 

In Cathers vs. Patchel, Appellant, 82 Pa. Sup. Ct. 

257, there was an action by a licensed real estate broker to recover commissions on the sale of real estate alleged to have been made through his agency. 

The defendant was the owner of five houses which she placed in the hands of several real estate brokers for sale. The plaintiff, who did not have an exclusive agency, negotiated with D. Centrella, who made an offer of $1,550 for each house, or $7,750 for the entire property. The defendant refused to accept this offer when the plaintiff submitted it to her. The defendant sought to have no further negotiations with Centrella in regard to the property. About a month later, Centrella went to another broker, authorized him to buy the property, and offered $125 more than the previous bid. The defendant agreed to the offer of $7,875, and the sale went through. Could the first broker recover commissions for the sale of the property on the grounds that he produced a buyer, even though the buyer purchased the property through

another agent and for a larger consideration? The 

Court decided that the first broker could not recover his commissions. The broker failed to produce a purchaser who was willing to buy at a price agreed upon or acceptable to the seller; the other broker was considered to have produced such purchaser. In addition, the agency was not exclusive, and the broker was therefore not entitled to commissions unless he himself arranged to sell the property.

This must be so, since the agent can have no further power than the principal has, and the principal, having sold the property to one person, is unable rightfully to sell it to another. It should be noted also in this connection that death of the owner before a sale takes place automatically revokes the agency.

Where claims for commission have been advanced by several brokers, claiming to have produced the purchaser for the owner's property, an owner should hesitate in voluntarily paying such commissions. 

There have been a great number of instances where he has voluntarily paid a commission to one broker, and a court and jury have subsequently decided that the sale resulted from the efforts of a second broker, so that the owner was compelled to pay a second commission. Where the owner is in doubt regarding which broker's efforts produced the sale, he should pay the amount of commission into court and there have the matter settled by a court of law without any further liability to himself.

CHAPTER IV  

Exclusive Listings HE tendency in real estate circles at the present time is away from concurrent authority towards exclusive listings. In other words, a real estate broker generally desires some assurance that he will have the unrestricted right for a certain period of time to negotiate a sale of the property in question, and that his investment in the form of advertising and time will be protected by an exclusive agency during the agreed-upon period of time.

Many exclusive listing contracts contain a provision that, after the expiration of the stipulated term, the listing contract shall continue in force for a similar period of time, unless the owner shall give to the broker so many days' written notice of his intention to cancel the employment. Many owners sign this type of agreement, unaware of the continuing effect of the contract, and find themselves in serious difficulties at a later date, when a sale is attempted by a new broker. In many instances the owner has been obliged to pay a double commission. If the real estate broker has an exclusive contract, the Pennsylvania cases hold that he is entitled to a commission where a sale is consummated by another broker during the term of the agency.

Common forms of exclusive listing contracts are as follows: 25

The undersigned hereby employs John Williams, Inc., as a sole and exclusive agent for the sale of the property described on the reverse hereof for a term of three months, and exclusively thereafter until terminated by written notice as below, and hereby agrees to pay to the said John Williams, Inc., a commission of 3% on the gross consideration upon its sale or exchange by whomsoever the same may be made or effected.

The agent's authority hereunder may be revoked by the owner upon the expiration of the above term, or any time thereafter, when no negotiations for the sale or exchange of the property are pending, but only after having given to 

John Williams, Inc., thirty days' notice, in writing, to that effect. And, if subsequently to such revocation, the property shall be sold or exchanged to any one with whom John Williams, Inc., had theretofore been negotiating, the said commission will be paid to the said John Williams, Inc. 

The said John Williams, Inc., is authorized to expend for the owner's account $ for advertising the property during the next months. 

Witness my hand and seal this 193.. ... .... day of (Seal) Witness: Owner .Address ... ...

Sale by Owner

An important question arises as to whether a broker is entitled to his commission where an owner sells the property directly without the intervention of another agent during the specified period. The Colorado

rule holds that where a broker is given a definite time in which to procure a purchaser, he has an exclusive agency, and even though the owner could make a sale himself, the broker is entitled to his commission if he procures a purchaser within the stated time. In other jurisdictions, the cases hold that a real estate broker is entitled to his commission if a sale takes place during the duration of this contract of employment, and it is unnecessary to inquire whether the broker has procured a purchaser during the specified time. The theory appears that, since the property has been conveyed and thus the subject matter of the contract has been removed, the broker now has nothing to sell, and the owner's action in recognizing or in making the other sale amounts to a breach of his contract with the broker, and the measure of damages is the amount of the commission which the broker would have earned had he been permitted to negotiate a sale during the entire period of his contract. 

In Murphy vs. Sawyer, 153 S. W. 991, the appellant, Murphy, in November, 1911, entered into a written contract with the appellees, Sawyer & Warford, who were real estate agents, placing with them his farm for sale. The contract contained the following provision: 

This contract is to remain in force for a period of twelve months from this date. Said property is valued by me at $17,500, cash trade, and I agree to accept dollars dollars for same, payable as follows: cash, and the balance in and years in equal instalments. I am giving the above named firm the exclusive privilege of selling or trading said property, and agree to pay a commission of 22% on the purchase price, said commission to be taken from first cash payment. ...... ... •-•

In 1912, Sawyer & Warford brought this suit against Murphy, alleging that, after making the contract, they advertised the land extensively and were making every effort to sell it, when Murphy, in March, 1912, sold the property to H. J. Chaney for $17,500, and they sought to recover from Murphy the commission specified in the contract on this sum, amounting to $438.75. For answer to this suit, Murphy, after admitting the contract and the sale by him of the land to Chaney, averred that it was a part of the contract between him and Chaney that in the event Sawyer & Warford could find a purchaser for the farm at the contract price, Chaney would consent that Murphy might sell and convey the land to such purchaser, and averred that both he and Chaney were willing and ready to do this if Sawyer & Warford found a purchaser. Sawyer & Warford testified as to the execution of the contract and the sale of the land by Murphy to Chaney, and said it ended their efforts and opportunity to make a sale. Murphy said on his examination that, although he had sold the land by written contract to Chaney, it was verbally agreed between himself and Chaney that Chaney would surrender his right to take the farm if Sawyer & Warford found a purchaser for it within the year, and that he so notified Sawyer & Warford, and in this evidence he was corroborated by Chaney. At the conclusion of all the evidence, the court instructed the jury to find a verdict in favor of Sawyer & Warford, which they did, and judgment was entered accordingly. 

On this appeal, counsel for Murphy insisted that the case should have been submitted to the jury under instructions advising them, in substance, that if the verbal arrangement before mentioned was made

between Chaney and Murphy, and Chaney was willing that the land might be conveyed to any purchaser secured by Sawyer & Warford, they should find for Murphy; the argument in support of this was that the verbal agreement between Chaney and Murphy was enforceable, as they both expressed their willingness to carry it out. 

If Murphy had only given Sawyer & Warford the exclusive agency to sell the land, the court stated this would not have deprived him of the right to find a purchaser and make a sale, but only have denied him the right to make a sale through another agent. 

But he did more than give them an exclusive agency. 

He gave them the exclusive privilege, and this condition took it out of his power to sell the land himself or through other agents, without accounting to 

Sawyer & Warford for the stipulated commission. 

Of course, when Murphy sold the land within a few months after he made the contract with Sawyer &

Warford, he deprived them of the privilege of selling the land within the time mentioned in the contract, and did not give them the opportunity to find a purchaser, as they might have done before the contract expired. This conduct on the part of Murphy constituted a breach of his contract with Sawyer & Warford, and gave them as a matter of law the right to recover in damages the commission stipulated in the contract. The judgment is affirmed.

It is true in some of our Pennsylvania cases,¹ where the parties had executed a contract in which it was expressly covenanted that the broker should be paid a stipulated commission in the event of a sale within the time specified, no matter whether it was effected 

1 Turner vs. Baker, 225 Pa. 359.

by the broker, or by the principal, or by any other person, it was held that the commission could be recovered when the sale was made and that it was immaterial who made it. These cases announce no new rule of law. They are simply declaratory of a fundamental maxim, which is that the parties are bound by the terms of their own contract. If an owner of real estate chooses to make a contract with a broker, in which it is stipulated that the broker shall have the exclusive right to sell the property within a specified time, and that he shall be entitled to receive a certain commission if the sale be made within the time designated, no matter who makes it, he is bound by its terms and cannot be relieved from a bad bargain because his agreement may have been foolish or improvident. Our cases have gone thus far and no farther.

Exclusive Agency and Exclusive Right to Sell Dis tinguished In this connection, it might be well to mark a difference between a broker's exclusive agency and an exclusive right to sell. In Martin Realty Company vs. 

Fletcher, 136 Atlantic 498 N. J., the suit was instituted to recover commissions upon an alleged sale of property belonging to the defendant. The defendant, on April 8, 1924, signed and delivered to the plaintiff the following contract:

I hereby authorize exclusively the Martin Realty Company to sell or exchange the property described on the reverse side of this card, and in the event of said property being sold or exchanged, agree to pay them a commission of 

32% for improved property, and 5% for unimproved property.

At the time of signing, the defendant borrowed of the plaintiff $400 to finish the house mentioned in the agreement. The defendant repaid to the plaintiff the full amount of this loan with interest. During September, 1924, the defendant conveyed the property to his partner in part settlement of a partnership debt. The house was taken at an agreed value of $8,- 

500. Suit was then instituted for commissions based on the above writing. Judgment for the plaintiff in the sum of $297.50 was given. Motions to non-suit and direct a verdict for the defendant were denied by the trial court. Exceptions are the grounds of appeal.

The plaintiff procured no purchaser. It advertised the property for sale. It procured no customer. No sale was made by reason of any advertising which the plaintiff did. If the writing set forth gave the plaintiff an exclusive right to sell the property, the judgment was properly rendered. Where an exclusive right to sell property is given to a real estate broker, the broker is entitled to a commission of any sale thereof made by the principal within the time specified in the contract. (9 Corpus Juris 622.)

Where, however, the broker is given merely an exclusive agency, the contract merely precludes the principal from employing another broker, and does not prohibit the principal from making a sale himself without the broker's aid. In such a case, he will not be liable to the broker for commissions unless there is a special contract to this effect, or unless he sells through another broker. (9 C. J. 622 and 623.)

In the present case, we construe the writing in question as an exclusive agency, and not as an exclusive right to sell the property. The respondent, on the question of consideration, urges that the loan

of $400 created a consideration for the agreement and changed its character in the respect mentioned. We do not think that the making of this loan, which was paid by the defendant, puts the agreement upon any higher plane than the advertising of the defendant's property by the plaintiff below. The judgment is reversed, with costs.

CHAPTER V 

Agent for Both Parties in Same Transaction 

Acting as Broker for Buyer and Seller

IT T IS a generally accepted rule that a broker cannot act as the agent of both parties in the same transaction. Each is entitled to his undivided efforts and to the unimpaired use of his skill, knowledge, and experience. It is not possible for a broker to fulfill these requirements if he is at the same time giving an equivalent service to the other party to the contract. The interests of buyer and seller are diametrically opposed to each other. The seller is interested in getting as high a price for his property as he possibly can, while the purchaser is naturally interested in obtaining the property at as low a price as possible, and so it is impossible for an agent to represent justly these conflicting interests. The agent must not, in other words, occupy the position of a judge, impartially weighing the merits of both sides. He has been engaged by a principal to present in as convincing a manner as he can the claims of his principal, and it is therefore imperative that the broker do all in his power to secure the most favorable settlement possible under the circumstances. He is neither interested in nor must he take any heed of the wishes of the other party in the transaction to the prejudice of his principal. In other words, his employment by one is incompatible with his employment by the other. This is so whether the sale is for cash, or whether there is an exchange of 36

Agent for Both Parties in Same Transaction 37 property involved. This general rule has been followed strictly in most states, and it is the rule in Pennsylvania.

Under Certain Circumstances Broker May Recover 

Under certain extenuating circumstances, however, there has been a divergence from this principle of law. The presentation of the facts and decisions in other cases will probably be the best means of illustrating this point. F employed S to sell a certain piece of land, his commission to be all that he could secure above $125 per acre. Subsequently, B agreed in writing to pay S $500 for his services for assisting to negotiate the purchase of the land belonging to F. 

S brought B and F together, and a contract was made for the sale of the land in question at $150 per acre. 

B and F subsequently consummated the sale without the assistance or advice of S. B refused to pay the $500 to S, claiming that the latter was acting for both parties without their consent. S sued him for that amount, but there could be no recovery on the grounds that have just been outlined. The other case presents the opposite point of view. A knew that B was an agent for C for making investments in real estate. A employed B to sell certain property at a specified price. Subsequently, B proceeded to make the sale to C at a price exceeding that specified by A, and paid to the latter a specified amount, less the stipulated commission, and retained for himself the excess as the price of his services rendered to C in making the investment. A brought suit against B for the excess, claiming that he had no right to it since he was acting for both parties. The court, however, held that under the circumstances B might law-

Agent for Both Parties in Same Transaction fully recover compensation from A and C, since A knew that B was already acting as agent for C in the transaction.

Even though the transaction is an exchange, the broker cannot recover commissions if he is entrusted with any discretion and has an agreement to receive any commission from the other side, without the knowledge of both parties. The rule, however, does not apply to an exchange of property where the broker has no discretion, but is simply to bring the parties together. In such case, the broker is merely a middleman. Where the broker is a mere middleman in the transaction, he is not bound to inform his principal of his employment by the other side. It may be said that an owner might reasonably assume that in an exchange of property, a broker receives commissions from both sides. In Alford vs. Cooke, 175 Mass. 

120, where the contract provides that each of the parties is to pay the broker a commission, it would appear from the contract that they would know he was acting for both sides, and therefore cannot refuse his compensation. It is not unlawful per se for one to act as broker for buyer and seller without disclosing the fact, providing the broker acts as a mere go-between or middleman. It is only when his employment is that of an agent with discretionary authority from his principal in the matter of such employment, that he cannot accept payment from another whose interests conflict with those of the first principal. 

In McClure vs. Luke, 154 Fed. Rep. 647, the defendant entered into a written contract with plaintiff's intestate, by which he agreed, in case he should purchase certain mining property at a stated price

Agent for Both Parties in Same Transaction 39 with the assistance of plaintiff, to pay the latter a commission. Three days after the death of the decedent, a contract was executed by which the defendant purchased the property with other property, for slightly more than the price named. A witness also testified that on the day before the decedent's death, defendant told him of the contemplated purchase, and asked him to ascertain if the decedent would not accept a sum in cash in lieu of an interest in the property which he was to receive under the commission contract. Held that the contract of sale and such testimony were sufficient, prima facie, to establish that the decedent had performed the service that entitled him to the commission. Where a broker, although acting as agent for both the seller and purchaser of property, is given no discretionary power to negotiate the sale, but his employment consists merely in bringing the principals together and in keeping them informed as to the condition of the property, the dual employment is neither inconsistent with nor contrary to public policy, and he may receive payment from both principals.

However, a broker who is vested with discretion, or upon whom his employer has a right to rely for the benefit of his skill or judgment, loses his right to compensation if he agrees to act in a similar capacity for the other party. A broker who is employed to sell property, and whose duty it is not only to find a purchaser but to negotiate the sale, cannot accept any compensation from any other than his employer, and if he does make an agreement to be paid by the purchaser, or if he assumes a position with reference to the transaction where his duty and interest might clash, he loses all right to his commission from

Agent for Both Parties in Same Transaction his employer, but as previously stated, when he is employed to find a purchaser upon the terms fixed by his employer, his duty is performed by bringing to the seller one who is willing to purchase upon such terms. He has no discretion to exercise, and there is no reason why he should not be permitted to take from the purchaser such compensation as the latter may see fit to give for the benefit he has received by being informed of the fact that he would be able to make such a purchase. 

In Hannan vs. Prentis, 83 N. W. 102 or 19 Cyc. 

279, there was an action to recover a commission for procuring a purchaser of a vacant lot in the city of Detroit. The plaintiff acted through employees of his office. Walter C. Wooley, a real estate broker employed in the plaintiff's office, testified that the defendant's brother, Browse T. Prentis, listed the property with him for sale at the price of $6,000, or for trade with improved property. He testified: "We had no further talk with reference to this property, except that I would try to secure a customer for him." 

Eugene Reynolds, another broker in the plaintiff's employ, testified: "I saw this property-the property on Lafayette Avenue was listed on our book for exchange for improved store property. We had the house of Mr. A. C. Peoples, on Lincoln Avenue, just about completed at the time. We had it for sale or exchange, and I went in to see Mr. B. T. Prentis about it, and worked on him until I got his written proposition on the exchange of his property for the 

Lincoln Avenue property." B. T. Prentis testified that he had no knowledge that Reynolds was to get a commission from Peoples, and never knew that he was acting for Peoples. The testimony offered by the

Agent for Both Parties in Same Transaction 41 plaintiff tended to show that the trade fell through by reason of a defect in the title of defendant to the vacant lot. The defendant's testimony tended to show that Browse T. Prentis had no authority to make the agreement either with defendant or Peoples. The jury found these issues for the plaintiff. 

The defendant raises a number of questions which are based on the statute of frauds, but as a contract of employment need not be in writing, and as the commission' is earned when a customer is procured ready to purchase at the price agreed, or to make an exchange on terms satisfactory to the employer, it is not conceived that the statute of frauds is an obstacle. There is, however, an impediment to plaintiff's recovery, arising out of his dual employment.

By the terms of the employment, the duty of plaintiff appears to have been to secure the best trade for the defendant that he could. In case of an exchange, the value of the property received was necessarily uncertain, and clearly Mr. Prentis would not be expected to be "worked on" by one having an adverse interest. If the employment had been simply to find a customer at a stated price, the fact that the plaintiff expected a commission from the customer would not have barred recovery.¹ But the exchange of property involves, of necessity, negotiations. The price of property to be received in exchange was not fixed, and the duty to find a customer to make exchange involves an obligation to find the best customer obtainable. Under the terms of such an employment, the employer has a right to assume that the agent is acting solely in his interest, until apprised to the contrary, and the onus probandi 

1 Montross vs. Eddy, 53 N. W. 916.

Agent for Both Parties in Same Transaction rests with the agent to show that he was so apprised.¹

The better doctrine, perhaps, is that which recognizes the difference between the case where confidence is reposed in the agent by the owner, and the case where no confidence is reposed. In summing up the law in Pennsylvania, it may be stated that a broker may recover commission from both parties to the same transaction when (1) he merely brings the parties together; (2) nothing is left to his discretion; (3) no special confidence reposed in him; and (4) the fact that he is acting in a dual capacity is known to both parties. 

1 Humphrey vs. Transportation Co., 65 N. W. 13; see also Campbell vs. Baxter, 60 N. W. 91; Rice vs. Wood, 113 Mass. 133.

CHAPTER VI 

Efficient and Procuring Cause of Sale-Duration of Employment-Terms of Sale 

Must Be Efficient and Procuring Cause of Sale

o determine which of several brokers is entitled to the commission, where each claims to have found the same purchaser, presents a question in many cases of no little difficulty. Where all the brokers are employed independently, at least, it would seem that the ordinary rule applicable to the case of the employment of a single broker would apply, i.e., that the broker who was the efficient procuring cause of the sale is entitled to the commission, and that this right cannot be affected because the principal in person, or by another agent, takes into his own hands and completes the transaction which the broker has inaugurated. 

Where two or more brokers are employed, there is no implied contract to pay more than one commission, and it therefore becomes necessary to lay down a rule for determining which one of different possible claimants is entitled to be paid.¹ A similar rule exists in the law of insurance, stated thus in Phillips on 

Insurance (5th ed.), p. 1132: "In case of the concurrence of different causes, to one of which it is necessary to attribute the loss, it is to be attributed to the efficient predominating peril, whether it is or is not in activity at the consummation of the disaster." 

1 Whitcomb vs. Bacon, 49 N. E. 742. 43

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 

So where several brokers have each endeavored to bring about a sale, which is finally consummated, it may happen that each has contributed something without which the result would not have been reached. 

One may have found the customer, who otherwise would not have been found, and yet the customer may refuse to conclude the bargain through his agency; and another broker may succeed where the first has failed. In such a case, in the absence of any express contract, the one only is entitled to a commission who can show that his services were the really effective means of bringing about the sale, or, to use the language of Phillips, "the predominating efficient cause."

Where several brokers are openly and avowedly employed, so that each can be said to have undertaken the employment on that basis, it is held in many cases that the entire duty of the principal is performed by remaining neutral between them, and that he has a right to sell to the buyer who is first produced by any of them, without being called upon to decide which of the several brokers was the primary cause of the sale.

Other cases state the rule somewhat less broadly, and it is everywhere agreed that, in order to be entitled to the benefit of it, the principal must in fact have remained neutral, and he certainly must not knowingly permit, much less aid in or connive at, the appropriation by one man of the rewards of what was really the result of another man's efforts. 

In either class of cases, it is quite possible that the principal may, in fact, be held to more than one payment if different juries differ as to who was the procuring cause. It is not ordinarily a case in which in-

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 45 

terpleader is possible, but the principal must defend himself at law. For this purpose it is, of course, competent for him to show that the plaintiff was not the procuring cause, but that some other man was. 

Abandonment of Effort

The same rule in regard to the abandonment of effort applies here also. Thus if one of several brokers gives notice to his principal that he cannot effect a sale, he will not be entitled to commissions because another broker, who is informed by the first that the property is for sale, succeeds in finding a purchaser. 

So, if two brokers are employed, and one of them enters into negotiations with a purchaser which fail and are abandoned, he will not be entitled to commissions because another broker subsequently succeeds, wholly through his own efforts, in making a sale to the same person, and upon substantially the same terms as those proposed by the first broker. The same result will follow where one broker has not been able within a reasonable time to effect a sale, and another broker afterwards succeeds in selling to a purchaser first approached by the former broker.

The principal, acting in good faith, and with no intention of defeating the broker's claim, may revoke his authority, while his efforts are yet unsuccessful, even though the principal in person or through another broker subsequently sells to a purchaser to whom the first broker endeavored to sell.

Duration of Employment 

An agency to sell real estate is presumed to continue until a sale is effected, and the burden is on the owner to disprove such presumption. In Hartford

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale vs. McGillicuddy, 68 Atlantic 860, the defendant in 

1896 placed in the hands of the plaintiff, a real estate agent, certain real estate to be sold at a given price, and for selling the same the plaintiff was to have a commission. The defendant never withdrew the property from the hands of the plaintiff, and there was no express revocation of the contract by the defendant, and no revocation by implication or by law. In 1906, after having made several unsuccessful efforts to sell the same, the plaintiff effected the sale of the property on the authorized terms, but the defendant refused to make the conveyance. The plaintiff then brought suit to recover his commission. The verdict was for the plaintiff. It was also held that the relation between the parties was that of principal and agent, and, while no definite period of time was expressly agreed upon during which the agency was to continue, yet the agency being established for a particular purpose, to wit, to sell the real estate, it was presumed to continue until the sale was effected, and the burden was on the defendant to rebut this presumption. 

Cases involving the question of reasonable time within which an offer of reward is held to continue are not analogous. The special findings by the jury that the defendant in 1896 authorized the plaintiff to sell the land in question for $2,800, and that he procured a purchaser for the land at that price, as well as the general verdict, are sustained by the evidence. The fact that a partner of the would-be purchaser had attempted to buy directly from the owner and the owner had refused to sell, should not deprive the plaintiff of his commission, as he had no knowledge of that fact and acted in good faith. Contracts of agency may be terminated by operation of law, but

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 

47 such cases fall within one of three classes: a change in the law, making the required acts illegal; a change in the subject matter of the contract, as the destruction of the property by fire; or a change in the condition of the parties, as by death or insanity. But the case at bar falls within none of these classes.

Owner's Right to Terminate Agency 

However, where a broker is employed to sell lands for his principal and there is no stipulation in the contract as to the duration of the broker's employment, the courts have held that the principal may terminate the agency at any time and discharge the broker, subject to the rule, however, that the purpose of the revocation cannot be to deprive the broker of an earned commission. In other words, it must be in good faith. It may be in writing, oral, or implied from the circumstances. A employed B to sell real estate for him. No time was fixed for the duration of 

B's employment. Before B had an opportunity to sell the land, A revoked his employment and took the sale of the land out of B's hands. B then sued A for breach of contract. The court decided that the owner was within his rights in terminating the employment, and B was without any legal redress.¹ An agency once terminated is not revived by subsequent acts. 

It is also indispensable that the purchaser should be found within the time limited, for if the broker's exertions do not produce the buyer until after that time has expired, it is not enough, even though that buyer may subsequently become the purchaser, unless the principal has caused the delay, or unless he waives it. (Mechem on Agency.) In Chapin vs. 

1 Coffin vs. Landis, 46 Pa. 426.

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 

Bridges, 116 Mass. 105, the broker was to receive a commission "if it is sold to any party within a year from this date, or at any time thereafter, before I have given you thirty days' notice." Held, the clause relative to withdrawal of the property only applied to the time after the expiration of the year. 

But if the purchaser is found within the time limited, it is immaterial that the actual sale was not fully consummated until afterwards. Where no time has been fixed, performance within a reasonable time will be sufficient, unless the offer to the broker has been withdrawn earlier.
