Where no time is fixed, a sale made within a reasonable time is sufficient.¹ A lapse of ten years does not necessarily terminate the broker's authority, but the authority continues until revoked, and the lapse of time is merely one fact to be considered by the jury in determining whether the authority had been revoked.2 When an owner of land agrees to pay commissions to a broker for the sale of it, and the latter prepares articles of sale, inserting the name of an alleged purchaser and providing that the purchaser should have a right to decline the property within two months, and the owner signs the agreement, without the alleged purchaser's having any knowledge whatever of it, the broker cannot recover his commission after the death of the owner and after the expiration of the two months, because the purchaser named, on discovering the transaction, but without having any extension from the owner, bought the land under a new contract from the executor.3

A broker may recover any expenses incurred in 

1 Morgan vs. Keller, 92 S.W. 75. 

2 Hartford vs. McGillicuddy, 68 Atl. 860. 

3 Enyeart vs. Figard, 38 Pa. Sup. Ct. 488.

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 49 

connection with the agency, previous to the revocation of his authority. In an action in assumpsit by A against B to recover damages for a breach of contract, B employed A to sell real estate. It appeared that A had agreed to sell a tract of land belonging to B, which had been laid out in 449 building lots, for which A was to receive as compensation $10 for the sale of each lot. A erected a temporary office upon the land, and incurred expenses amounting to $230. 

After A had sold two of the lots, it was found that B's wife would not join in the deeds, and thereupon B notified A that he was unable to carry out the agreement. The court directed a verdict for A for the amount of his expenses, and this was held no error by the Supreme Court.¹ 

Death of Owner

It has been stated previously in other connections that employment of a broker is revoked by the death of his principal. A was employed by B to sell land. 

The contract did not fix the duration of A's employment, but there was a stipulation that A was to receive a share of the proceeds on the land as his commission. B died, and A sold the land to C subsequent to B's death. There was no stipulation in the contract of A's employment that it should be extended to B's heirs and executors. B's heirs refused to convey to C, whereupon C filed a petition in the Orphans' Court, praying that the heirs be directed to convey the land to him. The petition was dismissed by the court.2 

Where a principal fixes a time within which a sale must be executed, and the broker does not sell within 

1 Hill vs. Jones, 152 Pa. 143. 

2 Shisler's Estate, 2 District Rep. 588. 1

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale that time, the principal may take the property out of the broker's hands and off the market and subsequently negotiate its sale himself. A, who was not a broker, solicited from B the privilege of offering for sale an oil property owned by B. B agreed to the request, and a price was fixed upon the property. 

Failing to find a purchaser, A requested that the price be reduced. This was agreed to by B, but upon the express condition that the property should be sold on that day, otherwise it would be taken off the market.

Eleven days afterwards, B sold the property to a person from whom A had obtained an offer slightly less than that for which the property was sold. The court refused to charge that if the jury believed that B withdrew the property from the market in good faith, and that no sale of the property was made by A before that time, then A could not recover. The jury returned a verdict for A, but upon appeal the verdict was reversed, and the case decided against the broker.¹

Under ordinary conditions, a principal has the unquestionable right to cancel an agency at any time, providing it is not an exclusive contract, unless he attempts to terminate the contract while negotiations are pending, for the mere purpose of depriving an agent of his commission. Even under these conditions, a principal as a rule can terminate the agency, but in doing so, he opens himself to a suit by the agent for commission on the sale which he has missed. The courts have held in a number of cases that an owner must give the agent reasonable notice before terminating the agreement, the purpose being to give to the broker a chance to take advantage of whatever 

1 Kelly vs. Marshall, 172 Pa. 396.

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 51 

labor he had expended in attempting to secure a purchaser. The question of what is a reasonable length of time under such circumstances, together with the question of what amount the owner owed the agent for the services which he has rendered to him during the life of the agency, is a matter for the jury to decide. There is considerable controversy on this point, and there are a great many instances where a broker has introduced a prospective purchaser of property to the owner before his agency has been cancelled, and sales have taken place by the owner to such prospective purchasers at a later date. Whether the broker is entitled to commission is, of course, a question of paramount importance to the broker. The question is: "Did the broker set in motion a series of circumstances, which, without interruption, culminated in a sale?" If the introduction did that, the broker is considered the efficient and procuring cause of the sale, and he can recover.

The rule which prevails in most jurisdictions is that a duly employed broker is the procuring cause and entitled to commissions either when he secures a contract of sale or when he produces a purchaser ready, able, and willing to purchase on his principal's terms.

Purchaser Must Be Financially Able 

It is also incumbent upon the broker who contends that he has found a purchaser to show that the purchaser produced was ready or able financially to complete the purchase. Pecuniary responsibility may be implied in many cases, but in cases of this nature the broker, by the weight of authority, must be prepared to prove that the purchaser found by

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale him was financially able to pay the purchase price agreed upon. He certainly cannot satisfy his undertaking by the production of a mere "man of straw." 

The principal may, of course, accept the purchaser produced in such a way as to foreclose his subsequent reliance upon this objection, even though the purchaser proves to be unable to carry out his contract. It is also possible for the principal by his conduct-as, for example, by expressly placing his rejection of the purchaser upon other grounds-to estop himself from raising this one at a later time. 

What constitutes financial readiness or ability in these cases cannot be determined by any hard and fast rule. It is, obviously, a very different question from that of solvency. Much must depend upon the terms prescribed in each case, and a man must be deemed pecuniarily able and ready when he has made the preparations and arrangements which are in accordance with safe and conservative dealing. The purchaser, for example, is not financially ready and able if he has to procure the deed of the lands in question in order that he may then go out and raise the purchase price. He would doubtless be deemed to be financially able if, where cash is to be paid, he is ready to turn over part himself and produces a responsible lender who is ready to lend and pay over the residue upon receiving a mortgage contemporaneously with the delivery of the deed. Where only part of the purchase price is required to be paid down with security upon the land, he is "ready" if he has the necessary cash payment and is prepared and offers to execute and deliver the necessary securities for the residue. (Mechem on Agency.) "Financially able," in this connection, must, of

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 53 

course, have a reasonable construction. It cannot mean that the proposed purchaser must necessarily have all the money in his pocketbook or to his credit at the bank, but it does mean that he is able to command the necessary money to close the deal on reasonable notice, or within the time limited by the vendor, if a time be limited.¹

The courts are not harmonious as to just when the broker may claim to be the procuring cause of a sale. In some jurisdictions, the broker is not regarded to have been the effectual cause of the sale until he procures an enforceable contract of sale. 

Where that rule prevails, the commissions are not due until such contract has been procured. As the broker's obligation under the usual employment does not require him to see that the contract is carried out, he may, under the rule just stated, claim his commission upon the execution of the contract. 

Other jurisdictions hold to a somewhat different view as to when the broker is the procuring cause of the sale. This rule, which is by far the more generally accepted, is that the broker is the procuring cause of the sale and his commissions therefore become due and payable upon the production of a purchaser ready, willing, and able to purchase the property upon the seller's terms, irrespective of whether a sale is actually consummated at a later date between the parties. Where a contract of sale is executed between the owner and the purchaser, the right of the broker to commission ordinarily does not depend upon the performance of such agreement of sale. If, from a defect in the title of the vendor, or upon the refusal of the seller to consummate the contract entered into, 1 McCabe vs. Jones, 124 N. W. 925.

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale or for any reason in no way attributable to the broker, the sale falls through, the broker is nevertheless entitled to his commission, for the reason that he has performed his part of the contract. Where a purchaser refuses to accept property by reason of the fact that the survey shows the property to be over the building line, the failure of the negotiations is no defense in a broker's action against the owner for commission in connection with the sale.

Commission Dependent Upon Transfer of Title 

A broker may, by special agreement with his principal, contract to make his compensation depend upon the actual signing of the contract, or upon the actual passing of title, or other contingencies. Even under these conditions, a broker may recover his commission at the time fixed in the contract of sale if it develops that the negotiations fall through by reason of some defect in the title of the seller, or upon the arbitrary refusal of the seller to go through with the deal. The owner is not permitted to plead that the failure to consummate the transaction will operate to deprive the broker of his commission.

Where the default or failure is on the part of the seller, the courts have uniformly held that the broker may recover the agreed-upon commission, and where the failure or default is attributable to the purchaser, the rule is different. Ordinarily, the signing of an agreement of sale for the property in question by the owner and prospective purchaser gives to the broker in the transaction a right to his commission. 

The execution of the agreement of sale by the owner is an admission on his part of the acceptability of the prospective purchaser produced by the broker,

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 55 

and ordinarily will entitle the broker to his commission.

In all the cases, under varying forms of expression, the fundamental doctrine is that the duty assumed by the broker is to bring the minds of the buyer and seller to an agreement on a sale, and on the price and terms upon which it is to be made, and that until that is done his right to commissions does not accrue. "A broker is not entitled to commissions when the customer, through no fault of the seller, refuses to complete the contract; but it is different when the customer has entered into a contract binding upon both parties, or into an agreement to pay a stipulated sum as damages in case of refusal to complete the contract." (Leete vs. Norton, 43 

Conn. 219; Coleman's Ex'r vs. Meade, 13 Bush, 358; 

Veazie vs. Parker, 72 Me. 443; Pearson vs. Mason, 120 Mass. 53; Rice vs. Mayo, 107 Mass. 550; Ward vs. Cobb, 148 Mass. 518, 20 N. E. 174.) When the vendor accepts the purchaser and enters into a contract with him, the solvency of the purchaser is to be presumed, in the absence of proof. This is the principle of law generally followed.

A broker who has fully earned his commission is generally not bound by any subsequent agreement that no commission is to be paid until the deed passes, for such an agreement is without consideration and cannot affect the obligation of the owner to the broker, and the agreement is not more binding when it recites a nominal consideration or good and valuable consideration, when in fact none passed. The fact that the seller refused to make the contract unless the broker agreed to wait for his commission until the deal was closed has been said not to furnish suffi-

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale cient consideration. But in another case, where the proposed vendor refused to execute an agreement of sale unless the broker consented to the incorporation of the following provision: "The vendor agrees that

O. E. Larsen is the broker who has brought about this sale, and agrees to pay said broker his commission therefor, namely, 3 per cent, when balance of cash payment to be paid is made," the courts have held that the broker is not entitled to his commission until actual transfer of title takes place, and that if the negotiations fail to materialize in the form of an actual transfer of title, the broker is not entitled to his commission, unless the owner has acted arbitrarily.

The duty assumed by the broker is to bring the minds of the buyer and seller to an agreement for a sale on the price and terms under which it is to be made, and until this is done, his right to commission does not accrue, but in order to entitle a broker to commission under this rule, it is necessary that a contract of sale should actually have been executed or that some note or memorandum of the sale should be made in writing. Naturally, it will strengthen the broker's case if he can produce signed agreements by the purchaser. He is entitled to his commission, however, upon the production of a purchaser, ready, willing, and able to purchase the property upon the terms fixed by the seller. The application of this rule to the facts of a given case ordinarily is not difficult, for when it is shown that the agent was instrumental in bringing the buyer and seller together, the fact that the agent was the procuring cause of the sale which was afterwards consummated is sufficiently established.

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 57 Introducing Purchaser to Owner May Be Sufficient 

Mere introduction of the purchaser to the owner by the broker may be sufficient performance of the broker's contract of employment with the owner, depending, of course, upon the facts in the particular case. If negotiations are taken up from the point of introduction by the buyer and seller, without the aid or intervention of the broker, and a sale results, the broker is entitled to his commission. A typical case is that of Kiernan vs. Bloom (91 Appellate Division 

429-N. Y. 1904). Here it was agreed that the broker should advertise the owner's place in the broker's name, that the inquiries should be attended to by the broker, and that the owner would pay a commission if a sale resulted. A purchaser had his attention drawn to the advertisement and wrote the broker, asking for details. The broker arranged a meeting between his representative and the purchaser, and within a day or two after this interview, in which the broker's representative described the property and advised the purchaser to buy, the purchaser opened negotiations personally with the owner, which, after some time, resulted in his purchasing the property. The matter of the purchase and the sale had not been discussed before between the owner and the purchaser, and no other agency at any time intervened to induce the purchaser to open negotiations. In passing upon this case, the court said that the sale was effected through the broker's agency as the procuring cause. His communications with the purchaser were the cause and means of bringing him and the defendant (owner) together, and a sale resulted in consequence of his efforts. The broker is therefore entitled to his com-

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale mission. It is of no consequence that the plaintiff did not negotiate the sale in person and was not present at the closing, nor that the purchaser was not introduced to the owner by the plaintiff direct. In full compliance with his agreement, he procured a purchaser who took the property on the owner's terms. 

His commissions were therefore earned. 

Ordinarily, however, a real estate broker must do more than merely introduce the buyer and seller in order to earn his commission. In Griffith vs. Cowan (57 Pa. Superior Court 625), the plaintiff had introduced the defendant to Mrs. McEko and the latter was informed that the defendant's three lots were for sale. She was acting for her husband as well as for herself. Mr. and Mrs. McEko each bought a lot, as did George Vargo, whom Mrs. McEko had informed that the lots in question were for sale. The 

Lower Court awarded a judgment of $150 to the plaintiff as commission for the sale of all three of the lots. The defendant appealed. The question involved was whether a broker can recover commission for the sale of real estate in which he was not actually concerned as to bringing the two parties together; whether the broker was entitled to the commission on the sale of the lot to Vargo. From the evidence, it appeared that Mrs. McEko was acting for her husband and herself, and that therefore the plaintiff was entitled to commission on the sale of the two lots to these two persons. Independent of the broker, however, Mrs. McEko had informed Vargo that the lots were for sale, and he subsequently bought one direct from the owner. The plaintiff was in no way connected with this transaction directly. The law deals only with proximate and not remote causes, and the

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 59 

plaintiff could therefore claim no commission on this sale. In another case, a plaintiff broker worked upon one P and induced him to look at the property. P finally decided not to buy himself, but upon P's advice, P's brother J bought directly of the owner. 

The plaintiff was held not the procuring cause of the sale to J, and the court was upheld in directing a verdict for the defendant.¹

But again the law regards only proximate, and not remote, causes; hence, if, after the broker's services have failed to accomplish a sale, and after the proposed purchaser has decided not to buy, other persons induce him to do so, the broker is not entitled to commission.2

The general rule of law is, that where there has been no direct communication between the broker and the purchaser, it must be shown affirmatively that the latter was induced to enter into the negotiations which resulted in the purchase through the means employed by the broker for that purpose. If the broker employed other persons to aid him, whether under pay or not, or if he put up maps, signs, notices, or otherwise advertised the property, and if by means of these measures, a person was induced to open negotiations with the owner which resulted in his buying the property, the sale may be said to have been effected through the broker's instrumentality. But it must be made to appear that what the broker did was the immediate and efficient cause of such negotiations. If the broker merely talked about the property with different persons, and one of them, on his own accord, and not acting in behalf of the broker, 1 Johnson vs. Seidell, 150 Pa. 396. 

2 Earp vs. Cummins, 54 Pa. 394.

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale mentioned to another that the property was for sale, and such last mentioned person thereupon looked into the matter and finally became the purchaser, the agency of the broker in inducing the sale was not sufficiently direct to entitle him to a commission.¹ 

A broker had been given, at his request, information concerning rentals of stores in defendant's building, in order that he might perhaps interest one Selznick in leasing one of them. He secured an offer from Selznick which the defendant would not accept. 

Later, Selznick himself made another offer to the defendant directly. That offer was not accepted, but as a result of the negotiations, Selznick formed a syndicate and was active in securing leases to the syndicate of all the stores in the building, and then he himself took one of them under the syndicate upon terms practically the same as his last offer. The upper court reversed a judgment for the plaintiff broker because the lower court had refused to instruct the jury: “If plaintiff introduced Mr. Selznick to defendant as a prospective tenant for one store, and Mr. Selznick afterwards applied to defendant, either on his own behalf or for himself and others, for a lease for a number of stores, this would not give the plaintiff any claim for commissions," and because this subsequent lease was not in accordance with Selznick's "original intention, or of the same nature, or in consummation of the original negotiations." 2 

Variation in Terms

It is not essential, to entitle a broker to his commission, that he should have procured a purchaser 

1 Gleason vs. Nelson, 38 N. E. 497. 

2 Meyer vs. Holding Co., 137 App. Div. (N.Y.) 691.

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 61 

upon the precise terms first named by the principal at the time of employment. If, through the instrumentality of the broker, the buyer and seller meet and negotiations are thus opened up between them, which, continuing without withdrawal of either party therefrom, culminate in a sale thereof for a less sum than originally named, the broker is entitled to his commission.

It is indispensable that the purchaser produced by the broker as one with whom the principal should deal should be one ready, willing and able to purchase upon the terms specified, for if he be willing to buy only on different terms, or at a different price, or upon other conditions, the broker will not be entitled to his commission, unless the variance in terms be waived by the principal.

This requirement as to terms, however, is subject to some qualifications where a sale has actually been made to a buyer produced by the broker. Thus, it could not be contended that the principal might, by slightly reducing his terms, deprive the broker of a well-earned commission. Otherwise he would be availing himself of the broker's efforts with impunity. 

Where the buyer produced by the broker is not willing to buy upon the terms first fixed by the principal, still, if the principal, by some modification of his terms and as a part of the same negotiation, actually accepts the buyer upon the terms originally fixed, the facts are held not to defeat the broker's right to his commissions. And under the same circumstances, if, though the seller does not lower his price, the buyer raises his offer during the negotiations, and buys at the seller's price, which is more than the broker had originally induced him to offer, the broker

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale ought still to be regarded as the procuring cause of the sale.¹ In Hubacheck vs. Hazzard, 83 Minn. 437, 86 N. W. 426, the principal's price, as given to the broker, was $8,250; the broker sent to the principal a proposed buyer who induced the principal to sell to him for $8,000. The principal knew at the time that the buyer had been sent by the broker, but contended that because the sale was not at the price fixed, he was not obliged to pay commissions. But the court said: 

It is immaterial whether the owner sold at the same price or at a lower figure than that given to the agent, for the agent might still remain the efficient cause of bringing the parties together. The case would present an entirely different aspect if the owner had refused to deal with the purchaser at the time in question, had dropped all proceedings connected with it, and afterwards, through some other source or from some other cause, negotiations had been opened anew. In such case, the agent would not be the procuring cause or instrumentality in bringing the parties together. 

The fact that a person is the first broker who calls the attention of the purchaser to the property in itself gives no claim to commissions. The right to commissions depends entirely upon the performance of the broker's obligation to bring the buyer and seller to an agreement. Where an owner does not know that the purchaser produced by the second broker is the same purchaser with whom the first broker is working, the owner, in the absence of bad faith or of such facts as should have prompted inquiry, is not liable to the first broker. Although the parties are originally brought together by the broker but no terms are agreed upon, and some weeks later the matter is taken up by a second broker, who finally consum- 

1 Mechem on Agency.

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 63 

mates the sale by bringing the parties to terms, the first broker is not entitled to commissions. To earn his commission, the broker must do something more than get authority from the owner to negotiate the sale. The fact that a sale or exchange of property is finally brought about by the efforts of the principal himself or by another broker with a person with whom the first broker had previously negotiated without success, will not furnish a legal basis for a claim for commission by the first broker. This is especially so when it appears that the first broker has for a long time ceased negotiations with the purchaser and abandoned all efforts to induce him to take the property on the proposed terms. 

An interesting case is that of Hovey vs. Aaron, 113 

S. W. 718, in which a broker who was authorized to get a purchaser at $30,000 interested a man in the land and took him to look at it. This purchaser saw on the land the sign of another broker, and by going to him secured a contract for $29,000, which the owner accepted, in ignorance of the fact that the purchaser had had any dealings with the first broker. Yet upon the ground that this first broker was the moving cause in securing this purchaser, the court held him entitled to a commission from the seller. In Vreeland vs. Vetterlein, 33 N. J. L., Chief Justice Beasley says:

It appears to be equally obvious that another principle must be applied to cases in which several agents are avowedly employed by the owner. Under such circumstances, it would be impracticable to resort to the same rule as when a monopoly to sell is given to one. In the latter case, the implied understanding is, that the seller will not take advantage of the endeavors of the agent, and that no other per-

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale son is authorized to do so. But in the instance of a number of agents, the agreement of non-interference is not so wide, for it extends to the act of the seller only. Where the property is openly put in the hands of more than one broker, each of such agents is aware that he is subject to the arts and chances of competition. If he finds a person who is likely to buy and quits him without having effected a sale, he is aware that he runs the risk of such person falling under the influence of his competitor-and in such case, he may lose his labor. This is a part of the inevitable risk of the business he has undertaken. On the other hand, if fortune should be propitious, a bidder for the property on sale who has been solicited by his rival, may come to him, and by his means effect the bargain. Now, in this competition, the vendor of the property is to remain neutral; he is interested only in the result. But when either of the agents thus employed brings a purchaser to him, and a bargain is struck at the required price, on what ground can he refuse to complete the bargain? Can he say to the successful competitor, this purchaser was first approached by your rival, and you should have refused to treat with him on the subject? There is no legal principle upon which such a position could rest. It is contrary to the usages of everyday commerce. Every advertisement of a stock of goods for sale has a tendency to carry off the customers of rival dealers. And if, therefore, it should be known to the vendor of the property that the agent who introduces a purchaser to him has, by the usual arts of competition, taken such purchaser out of the hands of his rival, I am not aware of anything in the law which would justify such vendor in a refusal to complete the contract. The task would be difficult and the risk great if vendors were called upon to decide between the claims of contestants. How would it be possible for such vendor to say whose influence it was that produced the sale, where the purchaser has been solicited by both agents? It would be at variance with all practical rules to require the party selling to pronounce, under the penalty of paying double commissions, upon the metaphysical question, which agent, under such circumstances, was the efficient cause of the sale. In the absence of all collusion on the part of the vendor, the

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale 65 

agent through whose instrumentality the sale is carried to completion is entitled to the commissions. 

While the statement of the law is plain, it is not always free from difficulty to determine which broker's efforts were the inducing cause of the sale. The general rule is that a real estate agent having a contract authorizing him to effect a sale is entitled to the commissions agreed upon where he procures a buyer who consummates the purchase of the property on terms satisfactory to the owner. Ordinarily, the application of this rule to the facts of a given case is not difficult, for when it is shown that the agent was instrumental in bringing the buyer and seller together, and a sale results, it is apparent that the broker's right to commission is sufficiently established; but when each of two or more brokers, within the knowledge of the other, has a contract authorizing him to effect a sale of the same property, the fact that one was instrumental in bringing the parties together cannot fairly be made the test of the liability of the owner of the property for commissions claimed. 

The owner has a right to authorize more than one broker, each independently of the other, to effect a sale of his property, and so long as he remains neutral, he should be permitted, without incurring liability for commissions to more than one of them, to close the sale of the property through the one who first produces a person ready to buy it, whether the agent producing the purchaser is the one who first brought him and the buyer together or not.¹ In Hogan vs. Slade, 71 S. W. 1104, it was held that evidence in an action by a real estate agent for commissions 

1 Edwards vs. Pike, 107 S. W. 586.

Broker's Employment-Terms of Sale for sale of land was sufficient to sustain findings that plaintiff was, at the time of the sale, defendant's agent to make the sale, and was the procuring cause thereof, and so entitled to commissions, though the sale was closed through another agent. 

Test to Be Employed

The test which ought to control in fixing the liability of the property owner on the facts of a case like this is: Within the knowledge of the owner at the time, was the sale consummated on terms agreed upon between the buyer and the broker who brought the parties together, or was it consummated on other terms as the result of negotiations between another broker and the buyer and after the latter had abandoned the contract made by him with the other broker? In the absence of special circumstances which would make it proper so to charge him, the owner ought not to be held liable for commissions to more than one broker, and after actually selling his property to a purchaser produced by one broker, on terms negotiated by such broker and not by another, he ought not, before paying him the commission, be required to determine whether some other broker was not in fact the procuring cause of the sale. In Pennsylvania, however, where an owner pays commission to a broker, the owner cannot plead this as a ground for avoiding liability on the claim of a second broker, where it is proved that the second broker was the procuring cause of the sale.

CHAPTER VII Broker's Liability for Representations Made Care in Making Representations broker is authorized to sell property for his prin- I cipal, as well as to initiate negotiations, it is very important that the broker be circumspect in the representations which he makes to prospective purchasers concerning the property for sale. Representations include not only actual statements made by the broker, but also any impression or belief that his conduct is calculated to produce in the mind of the other party as to the facts. The law holds the broker responsible for his representations in almost the same manner as if he were acting for himself. Not only will the broker lose his right to commission where he has been guilty of misrepresentation, but in addition, he may find himself the defendant in an action brought by the disappointed purchaser for any damages or expenses incurred by said disappointed purchaser, such as a fruitless action against the principal in the contract. A misrepresentation may be defined as an innocent misstatement of a material fact which induces the contract, or it may be the concealment of a material fact where there is a duty upon the broker to disclose such a fact. Under misrepresentation, a disappointed purchaser may recover from the broker only damages actually sustained by him, whereas under fraud he may recover damages in the nature of punitive damages. 67

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 

In this connection, it should be noted that there is a very vital difference between an expression of opinion and a misstatement of fact. Should a broker state that a property is worth $20,000 when as a matter of fact it is worth but $15,000, it is doubtful whether such a statement could be construed as misrepresentation, as it represents merely an expression of opinion. On the other hand, if the broker had stated that the property cost $20,000 when it really cost but $15,000, this is misrepresentation. Similarly, if a broker represents that a property is located fifteen minutes' walk from the railroad station, when as a matter of fact it takes the prospective purchaser thirty minutes to negotiate the distance, this would probably be construed as an expression of opinion, whereas if the broker had stated that the property is located one mile from the railroad station and the distance proves to be two miles, that would be misrepresentation. Where the purchaser does not rely upon the broker's statements, but examines the property for himself before the deal is closed, he cannot subsequently hold the broker responsible for any misrepresentations, as such misrepresentations cannot then be considered as inducing the contract. A purchaser of land could rely upon the representations of the seller's agent as to the direction and location of the land, such representations being within the scope of real estate brokers. "Puffing of Goods"

There is also a doctrine of law to be noted here known as "puffing of goods." Where the broker makes extraordinary and extravagant statements regarding the property for sale, as, "It is the most beautiful

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 69 

spot in the world" and, "The sun shines daily," and where there is no serious intent to include in the contract for sale that the property possesses all the magical powers and charms claimed for it, the deluded victim has only his pains for his trouble and no remedy at law. A principal selling property is presumed to know whether the representations he makes concerning it are true or false, and if he knows them to be false, then he commits a positive fraud, but if he does not know whether they are true or false, then his actions constitute gross negligence, and in contemplation of law, a representation founded on a mistake resulting from such negligence is fraud. The purchaser confides in the information furnished him by the owner, upon the assumption that an owner knows his own property, and it is consequently immaterial to the purchaser whether the misrepresentation proceeded from a mistake or fraud. The injury to him in both cases is the same, whatever may have been the motive of the seller. 

The law imposes the same obligations upon an agent acting for the owner of the property. He must be just as scrupulous in the statements which he makes concerning it as the principal would be were he conducting the negotiations personally. If an agent's misrepresentations have been made within the actual scope of his authority under his contract of employment, or even under the apparent scope or color of his authority, the representations and declarations made by the agent will bind the principal, since in both cases the representations and declarations of the agent are treated as though they had actually been made by the owner himself. A principal is so far liable for the acts of his agent that he cannot take

Broker's Liability for Representations Made any benefit from any misrepresentation fraudulently made by his agent, even though the principal was ignorant of the fraud at the time it was committed. 

The law, as it is construed, holds that if a party dealing with an agent suffers loss from such fraud, the principal is bound to make him a sufficient compensation for the injury, provided, however, that the agent acted in the matter as the agent of the principal and within the scope of the authority which he was held out to the purchaser to have. 

It has already been pointed out in another connection that a real estate agent occupies a position of trust separate and apart from that of the ordinary business man. 

Not Liable for Honest Mistakes

In other words, a real estate broker is not personally responsible for an error or mistake which he honestly makes, unless he has been careless, grossly negligent, or has gone contrary to his honest convictions and beliefs. It should be remembered here that the death of the principal automatically terminates the agency, and any representations or statements made by a broker after the death of his principal which induce a contract will give no rights of action for commission in favor of the agent.

It sometimes happens that a broker acts for an undisclosed principal, and the prospective purchaser deals exclusively with the agent as owner of the property. In such case, the agent is as liable as if he were the principal. It is the duty of the agent, if he desires to avoid personal liability on the contract, not only to disclose the fact that he is acting in a representative capacity, but also to disclose the identity of

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 71 

his principal. If he fails to do so, it must be taken that he assumes to and intends to bind himself.¹ 

Acting for Undisclosed Principal

In cases where the principal has desired to remain entirely undisclosed and has required the agent to make the sale in his own name, even though it is the business of the principal, fairness and equity to the third party requires that the third party be allowed to enforce the contract as he originally intended it. 

The fact that it is actually the business of the principal gives the principal the right to hold the third party. Nevertheless, it cannot take away the agent's liability resulting from the agreement by which he originally assumed to act as one of the contracting parties.

Where the principal is known to exist, but is not identified at the time of contracting, a different situation arises. In cases of this nature, considerable attention must be given to the form of the contract itself. If the agreement so states, it is possible for the third party to look only to the principal, whom he expects to discover at a later date, for the fulfillment of the terms of the contract. On the other hand, the agent may agree to assume liability even though his principal's existence and identity have been completely revealed. In a case where the agent contracts in his own name, even though he discloses the fact of the existence but not the identity of the principal, almost all the courts hold the agent liable on the contract. It is the intermediate situation where the contract is not clear as to the intention of the parties that causes the most difficulty, and is the most dan- 

1 Heath vs. Goslin, 60 Missouri 310.

Broker's Liability for Representations Made gerous from the point of view of the agent. The courts usually take the position that the agent is personally liable on all contracts which he has signed, unless he has disclosed both the existence and the identity of his principal. It is, therefore, essential that an agent in executing a contract be certain that it clearly discloses both the existence and the identity of his principal; otherwise he may find himself held personally liable on an agreement where he has failed to make clear to the third party and the court that he was contracting only in behalf of his principal.

In determining the intention of the parties, the contract must be read as a whole. After it has been so read, if it is clear that the intention was to hold the principal and not the agent, the form in which the agent may have signed the instrument has no bearing on his liability. But if the contract is ambiguous as to the question of who are the contracting parties, the form of the agent's signature becomes very important in determining his liability to the third party. 

The safest ways for an agent to sign to indicate clearly that it is his principal's contract and not his own are as follows: "Price & Co., by Adams, Agent," or “Adams, as agent for Price & Co." Where the agent has signed in an ambiguous manner such as "Theodore B. Tyson, agent," the courts often allow the third party to hold the agent liable on the contract. This is especially true in the case of negotiable instruments, where the party bringing the suit was not one of those present at the time of contracting. 

Broker's Personal Liability 

Where the agent has acted beyond his power to bind his principal on the contract, we have seen that

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 73 

the third party was without remedy against the principal. In such a case, fairness requires that the third party be allowed some right against the wrong-doing agent. If the agent has executed the contract in such a form that he is liable as one of the contracting parties, the third party can, of course, hold him to full performance of its terms. But in instances where the agent has contracted wholly in the name of his principal, a different situation arises. It is clear that there was no intention on the part of either party that the agent was to be bound by the agreement, and as we have already seen, the agent personally acquires no rights or duties under a contract which he makes entirely in the name of his principal. The third party can, therefore, not enforce performance of the contract against either the principal or the agent. He is, however, not entirely without remedy against the agent.

It is clear that the agent in making such an agreement in the name of his purported principal is misleading the third party. And whether the agent intended it or not, the third party has been damaged by relying upon the agent's expressed or implied representation that his principal actually exists, and that he has given the agent power to contract for him. The third party, although he cannot hold the agent to the performance of the contract, is given the right to sue the agent for the damages that have resulted from the fact that the agent did not have the power that he claimed.

There are two other conditions under which an agent may be held personally responsible under the contract. The first is found when the agent makes a fraudulent representation concerning his authority

Broker's Liability for Representations Made to act, with the intent of deceiving the other party. 

In these cases, he is personally liable in an action of deceit for the false representation, not only for the amount of the contract, but also for the costs incurred by the injured party in an unsuccessful action against the supposed principal. This liability of the agent to the third party is called the agent's warrant of authority, and it exists in all cases where the third party has suffered loss due to the fact that the agent acted without authority. 

Jackson, purporting to act as the agent of the owners of the Miles Building, Philadelphia, Pa., executed a five year lease of the building in favor of Harper. 

Harper, pursuant to the agreement, moved into the building and remained there for two years, during which time the owners accepted the rent. At the end of two years, the owners ejected Harper, naming as their reason the fact that Jackson had no authority to make the lease. Upon inquiry, it developed that at the time of leasing the building, Jackson had no authority, but he produced a written acceptance of the lease signed by the owners of the building. The question arises: may Harper hold Jackson for the damages resulting from his eviction? The court in this case decided that in an action in assumpsit to recover damages alleged to have been suffered by the plaintiff as a result of his enforced removal from a property leased to him by the defendant, binding instructions for the defendant are proper, where it appears that the defendant, a real estate broker purporting to act as agent for the several owners of the building, leased the fourth floor thereof to the plaintiff for a term of five years; that the written lease was signed by the defendant as agent for the owners,

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 75 

naming them, and the lessee went into possession; that after about two years the owners notified the plaintiff that the defendant had no written authority to execute the lease, and demanded that the lessee should vacate the premises by a certain day. The plaintiff did not remove on such day, but two months later entered into a contract with the owners, whereby he agreed, in consideration of a certain sum, to vacate by a month later, it further appearing that there was sufficient evidence to sustain a finding that one owner had expressly ratified the broker's action in executing the lease and that the defendant had acted in good faith. In such case, the fact that the plaintiff, in his agreement to vacate the premises, reserved the right to hold the defendant responsible for alleged damages sustained by him is immaterial. In other words, where a tenant, voluntarily and not under compulsion, abandons the premises, there is no eviction. The parties to the subsequent arrangement, namely, the plaintiff and the tenant, could not by the language used alter the essential character of the contract or thereby preserve a right against the defendant, who was not a party to their agreement, nor could the plaintiff in this way affect the force of his subsequent act in vacating the property at the time agreed upon.¹

Second, where a broker has no authority and knows it, and nevertheless makes the contract as having such authority, as where, without having actual authority, he assumes to act for his principal, presuming that the latter will recognize and adopt the act as his own. 

In such a case, there need be no fraudulent intent, but, having no authority in fact, although he acts 

1 Harper & Bro. Co. vs. Jackson, 112 Pa. 475.

Broker's Liability for Representations Made bona fide, he does a wrong to the other party by misleading him. It is a principle of equity that where one of two innocent persons must suffer, he ought to bear the loss who has been the sole means of producing it, and the fact that the agent was innocent of any wrong, or actually thought that he was acting properly, is no excuse, because in either case the third party is injured by relying on the actions of the agent. Of course the agent is under no liability if the third party knew the facts. The agent, in contracting in the name of his principal, warrants only to make the sale; the principal gave the agent power to execute the contract in question, and the contract is as binding upon the principal as if he had actually executed it.

In certain states, there is a third situation under which the agent may be held personally liable under a contract which he has negotiated for his principal. 

Where an agent in fact does not have authority to make a contract and he nevertheless does so, under the honest belief that such authority is vested in him, he may or may not be held personally liable depending upon the state in which he is operating. A good illustration of this is furnished by a case in which an agent might sell property under a Power of Attorney which he believes to be genuine, but which, as a matter of fact, is forged, and therefore invalid. 

In the states of Pennsylvania, Illinois, and Massachusetts, where the agent is placed in a class of professional men, who, so long as they act without negligence and in accordance with their honest beliefs, cannot be held personally liable for their acts, the agent could not be held for anything which he had done under the circumstances which have just been

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 77 

outlined. In Minnesota, however, an entirely different doctrine prevails. In that state, the real estate broker occupies the same position as any other business man. 

He has not been placed in a special class, nor does he enjoy the immunity ordinarily given to a professional man for honest mistakes. As a result, the agent is always held to a strict liability and to the contract which he has made, no matter whether or not he has made it under a false conception of his powers or of his situation. The same rule would pertain where an agent sold property under a Power of Attorney which had expired due to the death of the principal, which fact was unknown to the agent at the time the sale took place.

An agent acting in excess of his authorized powers will be personally responsible to a third party for any damages sustained by the latter by reason of the agent's representations. 

The general rule in Pennsylvania was clearly expressed by the Supreme Court in the case of Kroger vs. Pitcairn (101 Pa. 216). In this case, Kroger insured his merchandise with a company represented by Pitcairn. Pitcairn, the insurance.broker, told him that the policy would not be voided if he stored a barrel of carbon oil on the premises, although the policy contained an express clause providing against the storage of such material. After a loss occurred, the policy was in fact voided on this ground, and Kroger was unable to obtain damages from the Insurance Company. He thereupon sued the broker Pitcairn upon the ground of his false representation. The court permitted a recovery for the damage sustained by Kroger on the theory that an agent was responsible for any unjustified assumption of authority to

Broker's Liability for Representations Made act for his principal. In other words, an agent who knowingly, negligently, or mistakenly holds himself out, either by express or implied methods, as having authority to act for his principal in a given transaction, when as a matter of fact he has no such authority, is liable to the third party, who deals with him on the strength of such representation, for any damages the third party may suffer as a result. 

While it was held in this connection that an action could be maintained against the agent for the damage caused by his unauthorized act, nevertheless the injured person could not maintain an action against the Insurance Company because of the fact that the act complained of was beyond the agent's authority. 

The insurance policy in regard to the storage of combustibles upon the premises was explicit, and created the law by which the insured and the insurer were governed.

Again, where a real estate broker, in order to make a sale, represented that an assessment for a sewer in process of construction near the property had been paid, the owner was not liable therefor in an action in deceit, in the absence of evidence that he gave the broker authority to make such representations, it not being within the ordinary scope of his employment.¹

An innocent vendor, in other words, cannot be sued in tort for fraud of his agent in effecting a sale. 

In such a case, the vendee may rescind the contract and reclaim the money paid, and if not repaid, may sue the vendor in assumpsit (that is, on the contract), or he may sue the agent for the deceit.2 The license

law takes cognizance of these situations and a broker's license may be suspended or revoked for such misconduct.

Where an agent bids more for a property at a Sheriff's sale than he has been authorized by his principal to offer, he would be considered the purchaser of the property and personally responsible, in case his principal declined to pay the amount which the agent bid for it. 

In Holman vs. Lipcomb (103 S. E. 513 S. C.), the plaintiff was a real estate agent and the defendant was the owner of a tract of land in Cherokee County. 

The plaintiff asked the defendant to allow him to sell her plantation for her. The defendant was not anxious to sell, and there were several interviews between the parties regarding the sale. The plaintiff claims that the defendant agreed to pay commission of 5 per cent on the selling price. The defendant claims that the price of $12,000, for which he sold the place, was to be net to her, and she finally consented to the sale at that price. The plaintiff then had an agreement prepared and signed by the defendant as 

State of South Carolina 

County of Cherokee

RECEIVED of A. L. Holman the sum of $500, as part payment of the purchase price of $12,000 of my farm in Cherokee County, containing 104 acres, more or less (then follows a description of the premises), and upon the payment of the balance of said sum of $12,000 on February 1, 1918,

I do hereby agree to execute and deliver to the said A. L. 

Holman a deed of conveyance in fee simple, of the above described premises, free of encumbrances. 

WITNESS my hand and seal January 11, 1918. 

Mrs. Emma G. Lipcomb.

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 

In pursuance of this contract, the plaintiff prepared and the defendant executed a conveyance of land to those to whom the plaintiff assigned the contract. After the sale, the plaintiff demanded commission, which the defendant refused to pay. The plaintiff brought suit in view of the verbal contract. The plaintiff admitted the written contract. The defendant objected to all testimony in reference to any evidence of the parol contract. The plaintiff asked the presiding judge to charge that if there was fraud, it would vitiate the written contract. The testimony upon which this request to charge was based was that on the morning of January 11, 1918, the defendant told him that she had had an offer of $12,600 for the land, and he found that the statement was not true. 

The record does not show that the plaintiff discovered the falsity of the statement, if it was false, until after the contract was made, and it was then too late for him to repudiate the contract. The plaintiff stated that he induced the defendant to sign the contract by his promise to surrender his commissions. The court decided that where a broker abandoned his position as agent to sell on commission by taking a contract for the sale of the land directly to himself, he cannot recover his commission, since the positions of agent to seller and purchaser are conflicting, and the plaintiff could not occupy both positions. The plaintiff, in other words, procured the conveyance under his contract as purchaser.

On the other hand, where a real estate broker is in no way guilty of bad faith towards his principal, a recovery of commission may be had.

An intentional concealment of important and material facts from the knowledge of his principal by a

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 81 

broker employed to sell real estate will deprive him of his right to commissions for procuring a buyer. 

In Pratt vs. Patterson's Executors (112 Pa. 475), it appeared that in 1886 the plaintiff was a real estate broker. In March of that year, George Patterson employed him to find a buyer for his real estate at the southwest corner of Eighth and Chestnut Streets, Philadelphia, Pa. The plaintiff found a purchaser in the person of Mr. W. W. Harding, and the price agreed upon was $140,000, which was the price fixed by Mr. Patterson. Of this sum, $30,000 was to be paid in cash and the remainder secured by the purchaser's bond and mortgage on the premises. Mr. 

Harding desired to purchase also the adjoining property, and being afraid that if his intention was known, the owners of the property would advance the price, he made an agreement with the plaintiff by which a clerk in his employ, Mr. Thomas Nott, was put forward as the ostensible purchaser. Mr. Nott was a young man in Harding's employ at a small salary, and was altogether irresponsible from a financial point of view. By an arrangement made between Mr. 

Harding and the plaintiff, Mr. Nott was reported to the owner of the property as the prospective purchaser and his name was put in the written contract of purchase as a buyer. The name of the real purchaser was never communicated to Mr. Patterson.

Patterson had represented the property as containing a depth of about 10 per cent more than it really contained, and when this was discovered, the purchaser declined to pay more than $130,000 for it. The plaintiff replied that it was useless to make a reduced offer, as the owner would not take less than $140,000, and that he, the plaintiff, would not com-

Broker's Liability for Representations Made municate with him unless the purchaser agreed to give that sum for the property. The purchaser then stated that he would give the $140,000 only on condition that the plaintiff should offer first respectively $135,- 000 and $137,500, and they being declined, then to close the transaction at $140,000. The plaintiff replied that he knew the smaller offers would be declined and would not submit them unless Nott would sign the agreement to purchase at $140,000, if Patterson should decline them. Nott then signed the agreement to purchase at $140,000. With this agreement, the plaintiff called on Patterson and the smaller offers were declined. The plaintiff explained to Patterson why he had made them. Patterson accepted Nott as the purchaser, but stated that he would not sign the agreement until he had seen his lawyer, as he had gotten into trouble before by signing papers, but he said the sale was made and that he would produce the deed the next day to make the conveyance. He called on the plaintiff the next morning but without the deed, and said again that he would consummate the sale. This, however, he finally refused to do and broke off all negotiations. 

This case has been tried several times, the first time in 1870, resulting in a verdict for the plaintiff. This trial was set aside because, as the District Court then interpreted the law, the agent could not recover unless the vendor actually made a conveyance to the parties agreeing to purchase. Chief Justice Gordon delivered the opinion of the court, in which he stated: "It is certainly a correct rule of law, as held in Clendenon vs. Pancost (75 Penna. 213), that a real estate broker's commissions are earned whenever he has procured a buyer who will comply with the con-

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 83 

ditions fixed by his principal for the property proposed to be sold, but it is to be understood that this rule depends not only on the fact that the broker is to be regarded as the agent for the seller, but that as such agent he acts with the utmost good faith towards his principal, and if he does not so act, he is entitled to nothing." In the case in hand, however, were we to consult the evidence alone, giving no attention to the allegations of the plaintiff, we might be compelled to regard him as the agent of the vendee, rather than of the vendor. George Patterson, the defendant's testator, had a property to sell, for which he agreed to take $140,000, $30,000 in cash and the balance to be secured by bonds and mortgage. Under these conditions, the matter was placed in the hands of the plaintiff as Patterson's broker. He found a purchaser, and that purchaser was W. W. Harding, a perfectly responsible party, but through fear of competition, the negotiations, so far as Patterson was concerned, were conducted in the name of Nott, Harding's clerk. 

The latter was to be the ostensible buyer, Harding to advance the hand money, and Nott was to make an assignment of the deed after it had been executed by Patterson. Now it is hard to conceive why the name of Harding, the real vendee, should have been concealed from Patterson, unless the intention was to conceal the purposes of the former. It is obvious that whether the sale was made in the name of a real or fictitious purchaser, it could make no difference to the seller. In either case, he would get his hand money, and though admittedly the bonds in the latter event would be worthless, yet as the property in itself was sufficiently valuable to secure the payment of the mortgage, the bonds were of no consequence.

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 

This argument, if such it may be called, is met by the pertinent inquiry, "By what authority did the agent in this matter assume to judge for his principal?" It does not require the discernment of a very acute casuist to perceive that it was Pratt's duty to submit this whole matter to Patterson and allow him to determine whether he would prefer as a purchaser the responsible or irresponsible party, and whether he would accept the bonds and mortgage of Nott, rather than those of Harding. Good faith forbade the concealment of an arrangement intended for the advantage of the buyer rather than that of the seller. 

When we come to that part of the transaction in which, at the instance of Harding, the plaintiff carried to Patterson a bid of $135,000 for the property, when in fact he, the plaintiff, and Harding had as between themselves closed the sale at $140,000, we can no longer regard Pratt as the agent of Patterson.

This conduct can only be excused on the ground of ignorance, an ignorance which caused himself, unwillingly perhaps, to seem willing to tamper with the interests of the principal in favor of the person with whom, as a professed agent of that principal, he was dealing. But none the less, had Patterson, on the discovery of this fact, the right to dissolve as he did the relation between himself and Pratt and treat the whole transaction as fraudulent and void? The court accordingly decided against the broker.

A real estate broker who received authority from the owner to procure offers for real estate had negotiations with the defendant, who thereby learned the price and terms. The defendant, after ceasing relations with the broker, had negotiations with the owner, and represented to him that he was an agent

Broker's Liability for Representations Made 85 

of a third person, who desired to purchase the property. The defendant, intending to be the real purchaser, made the representation so as to procure a commission from the owner, who conveyed the property to the third person, who in turn immediately conveyed it to the defendant. The owner paid the defendant commission. The court held that the defendant obtained through fraud, money which in equity belonged to the broker, who could maintain an action, therefore, for money had and received.¹ 

¹Gormley vs. Dangel, 110 N.E. Rep. 1084 Mass.

CHAPTER VIII Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 

VENDOR of land who has been compelled by a decree of court to repay the commissions paid directly to his agent by the purchaser as part of the consideration, can recover them from the agent, the payment being compulsory and not voluntary. 

Responsibility for Down Payment

This raises the question as to the authority and responsibility of a real estate broker in accepting cash payments from a prospective purchaser as hand money in the transaction. In practice, many real estate brokers accept and retain hand money payments. 

Since a real estate broker acts in a representative capacity as agent for the owner, any monies entrusted to him in the course of his employment properly belong to the owner, and should be turned over to the owner upon request. Many Agreement of Sale contracts are so drawn that custody of down payments is entrusted to the broker. Retention of this money may place the broker in an embarrassing position at a later date, and in one which he did not anticipate. 

In other words, if the negotiations fail to materialize, the burden and responsibility are then placed upon the broker to decide as to the merits of the contracting parties and dispose of the funds entrusted to him accordingly. Either way he decides, he may find himself the defendant in a damage suit by the other party 86

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 87 

to the contract. In order to protect himself in his claim for commission, it is advisable for him to insert a clause in his contract with the owner or in the Agreement of Sale, to be signed by both parties, to the effect that the deposit money shall first be applied towards payment of commission of the agent of the first party, and the balance shall be paid to the first party.

The situation is oftentimes complicated in practice by the fact that a prospective purchaser deals with a real estate broker on the strength of the confidence he has in such a broker's integrity and experience. 

If the negotiations fail through the fault of the seller, the disappointed purchaser naturally looks to the broker for the return of his hand money. It may be that the principal is entirely unknown to such purchaser. Legally, the disappointed purchaser would be obliged to confine his action for the return of the down payment to one against the principal. Where it is stipulated that the broker is to retain his commission out of the hand money, the broker would be protected in transferring to his principal the balance over and above his commission. 

In Messer Real Estate & Insurance Company vs. 

Ruff, 64 S. W. 50, J. J. W. Cooke, of Maben, Miss., owned a lot in the city of Birmingham, on which was situated the "Bonita Theater." The Messer Real 

Estate & Insurance Company, as the agents of Cooke, rented out this property, looked after its taxes, and collected the rents. On or about the 11th day of February, 1911, Henry Ruff informed the Messer Real 

Estate & Insurance Company that he desired to buy the property. Thereupon, the Messer Real Estate & 

Insurance Company wrote to Cooke, informing him 1

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money that a party desired to buy the property, and asked Cooke to name his figures, and requested the authority to sell the property for him as his agent. Cooke thereupon, on the 18th day of February, 1911, wrote to the Messer Real Estate & Insurance Company the following letter: 

Maben, Miss., Feb. 18, 1911. 

Mr. M. P. Messer, Birmingham, Ala.-Dear Sir: In re your letter of the 11th inst., asking for a price on my Third

Avenue property will say that I have always refused to price this particular piece of dirt but if your man will pay me $40,000.00 net, carry out my lease to the moving picture people, assume the indebtedness against same, paying me the difference between the $40,000.00 and the amount now held against the property, which is approximately $13,- 000.00, in cash, I will make him a deed to the property and will transfer to him the insurance now in force and paid up for this year. This offer will hold good not later than 

March 1, 1911. Yours hurriedly, J. W. Cooke. This is a 

5% loan.

Thereupon the Messer Real Estate & Insurance Company offered the property to the said Ruff at $42,500, and Ruff and the Messer Real Estate Company entered into the following agreement: 

Birmingham, Ala., Feb. 22, 1911. This is to certify that we have this day sold to Henry Ruff the north one-half of the east one-half of lot 4, block 86, Birmingham, Ala., for $29,500.00 cash, and the assumption by him of a certain mortgage on the above described property, payable to the Penn Mutual Life Insurance Company in the sum of $13,- 000.00, at the rate of 5% per annum, interest payable semiannually, and we herewith acknowledge the receipt of $1,000.00, the same being part of the purchase money to bind the sale, said sale being subject to the approval of J. W.

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 89 

Cooke, owner, and an abstract of good title conveying the above described property unencumbered, except as stated above. The purchaser agrees to assume the lease contract now in existence, which expires September 20, 1912. Signed in duplicate this 22d day of February, 1911. Messer Real 

Estate & Ins. Co., Agts., By M. P. Messer, Prest. I hereby accept the above contract of purchase. Henry Ruff.

The $1,000 referred to in the above contract was represented by a certified check, payable to the Messer 

Real Estate & Insurance Company, dated February 22, 1911, which check was held by said Messer Real 

Estate & Insurance Company until March 20, 1911, when it was by said Company presented to and paid by the bank upon which it was drawn, and the proceeds were by said Company credited on the account of J. W. Cooke under the circumstances hereinafter stated.

On the 9th of March, 1911, a suit in ejectment was brought against said Cooke for said lands by third parties, and Ruff refused to accept deed unless Cooke would indemnify him against loss on account of said ejectment suit. This Cooke refused to do. After various negotiations to which it is not necessary to refer, the agreement of purchase was cancelled, and Ruff leased the property from Cooke. 

The Messer Real Estate & Insurance Company claims that Cooke owes it $2,500 on account of the above transaction. Ruff agreed to pay $42,500 for the land, and if the sale had been consummated, said Real Estate Company would have received $2,500 out of the proceeds of the sale as its commission for negotiating the sale; and on March 20, 1911, this

Real Estate Company charged Cooke on its books with said $2,500, as of February 22, 1911, and on

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money said day had the bank on which the above described check was drawn to pay the check, and this money the Real Estate Company retained, and credited the same on the said Cooke's account. On the subject of this charge of $2,500 and this credit of $1,000 on the books of the Real Estate Company, the bookkeeper testified: "That entry of February 22d was made on March 20th. It was held off until that split occurred on the 9th of March; the whole matter was held off the books until it was determined whether or not the trade would go through." In other words, after it became apparent that the agreement between Cooke and Ruff as to the purchase of the property was not going to be consummated, then the Messer 

Real Estate Company charged Cooke with $2,500 on its books as commission for making the sale, and then proceeded to the bank and had the check for $1,000, which Ruff had given said Company as "part of the purchase money to bind the sale," cashed, retained the money, and credited the Cooke account with the proceeds. The theory of the Messer Real 

Estate Company was and is that J. W. Cooke owed said Company $2,500, as above stated; that Ruff owed Cooke the purchase price of the land; that the $1,000 was part of the purchase price of the land, and that therefore this money was Cooke's money, and that as the Real Estate & Insurance Company came lawfully into the possession of the money, it had a right so to credit it on the amount which, according to its theory, Cooke owed said Company. 

We may as well say, before we conclude this statement of fact, that when Cooke and Ruff, by mutual agreement, rescinded the executory agreement of sale, Cooke told Ruff that he had never laid any claim to

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 91 

the $1,000 in the hands of the Real Estate Company, and that Ruff could demand and receive that money of said Company.

The agreement of sale between Ruff and Cooke was, by mutual agreement, rescinded. No deed was ever delivered. It is not contended that this rescission was done for a fraudulent purpose, i.e., for the purpose, participated in by Ruff, of defeating the Messer Real Estate Company of its commissions. On the contrary, we gather from this record that Cooke is solvent and can be held to the payment of any money which he may owe said Real Estate Company.

Under our law, parties who are sui juris possess the utmost liberty of contracting with reference to all matters not forbidden by law; and it is a familiar principle that parties to an executory contract may, at pleasure, rescind any such contract, without any new consideration therefor, the mutual assent of the parties to the rescission furnishing a sufficient legal consideration for such rescission.

When an agreement is made between two parties relative to the sale and purchase of a tract of land, the contract of sale, although it may be enforceable by a proceeding in equity, remains unexecuted until there has been a deed, conveying the title to the land, executed and delivered by the grantor to the grantee. At any time before the actual delivery of the deed-no rights of third parties intervening-the parties to such agreement may, by mutual assent, rescind it, and the rescission, unless there is some agreement to the contrary, places the parties in the same attitude as if they had never made such an agreement.

It not infrequently happens that, although the

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money recorded deeds may show a perfect title to real estate in a certain person, that person has not, by reason of some fact not shown by the recorded deeds, any title whatever to such lands. Some other person, in spite of the recorded deeds, may have a perfect title to the land.

The agreement between the Messer Real Estate Company, as agents of Cooke and Ruff, dated February 22, 1911, which we have above set out, clearly contemplated that the contract should not be binding upon Ruff unless Cooke could convey to Ruff a perfect title, subject to the mortgage for $13,000, to the land. It further contemplated that the abstract should show this perfect title, and that it should show this title when the deeds were delivered. No time was fixed by the parties for the execution of the contract, and the law therefore gave them a reasonable time within which to complete it. When the time for the delivery of the deed arrived, a suit by third parties was pending in Jefferson County against Cooke for this land. 

The agreement, fairly construed, shows that it was not the intention of Cooke to sell to Ruff, and not the intention of Ruff to buy a lawsuit from Cooke, and while nothing was said in the contract with reference to the lawsuit, nevertheless, under the principles above announced, so long as the contract remained executory, the parties to that contract had the right, if they mutually agreed to do so, to rescind that contract on account of such lawsuit, or for any other reason, and place each other in statu quo. The trial judge therefore clearly charged the jury the law when he said in his oral charge: 

The contract of February 22, 1911, as you have doubtless observed, is not a completed contract for the sale of the

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 93 

lot therein mentioned. The proposed sale is by the terms of that contract made subject to contingencies, one contingency being that the agreement entered into by the defendant realty company and Ruff was to be approved by the owner of the land, Mr. Cooke. Another one of the contingencies is that an abstract of title is to be furnished showing title to be in Cooke, excepting the existence of a mortgage given to the Penn Mutual Company for about $13,000. 

The contract being in that condition, the minds of the parties to the sale, the principals to the transaction, not having up to that time ever come together completely, the completion of the contract being held up to that time in abeyance, it was competent for the principals to the contemplated sale to carry on the negotiations further, and in such negotiations it does not appear that the real estate company had any control of either one of the principals in the transaction. It was competent for Cooke on the one part and Ruff on the other part to conduct their negotiations, and if it became impracticable for them to agree further upon the terms of the sale, then they were competent to abandon it without the consent of the defendant real estate company.

This record discloses that at all times Ruff was willing to accept from Cooke, in spite of the pendency of the lawsuit, a conveyance with general covenants of warranty, but that he was unwilling to accept a deed with covenants of warranty limited to the acts of Cooke unless Cooke would give him an agreement indemnifying him against loss by reason of the above described lawsuit. Cooke refused to sign and deliver a deed with general covenants of warranty, and was only willing to give a deed with warranty against his own acts. He was unwilling and refused to give Ruff an indemnity against loss on account of the lawsuit. 

This being the situation of the evidence, the trial judge properly charged the jury as follows: "If, by

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money reason of the existence of suits against Cooke for the property, or if, by reason of the limited character of the warranty expressed in the deed made by Cooke, there was a failure on the part of Cooke and Ruff to complete the contract of sale, then, without any writing to that effect, it was competent for them to abandon it and for Cooke to abandon all right as between himself and Ruff to the payment which Ruff had made to the real estate company. If you find that there was such an abandonment of the contract for such causes, and that by the agreement so abandoning the transaction Mr. Cooke abandoned all right to the $1,000 payment, and that it had not been up to that time paid over to him in some form other than by a credit of his account upon the books of the real estate company, then the plaintiff Ruff had a right to recover the amount of the payment so made to him." 

Undoubtedly, there was evidence tending to show that Cooke, by his acts, ratified the contract—if, indeed, it needed ratification-which was made by the 

Messer Real Estate Company with Ruff on February 22, 1911, so as to make that contract, executory in its nature, binding upon him. 

The check which was turned over to the Real Estate Company "to bind the trade" was, in our opinion, under the terms of the contract itself, a check which was to become the property ex equo et bono.
