Under the law, his contract required him to do this. 

That check belonged to Ruff until Cooke carried out his contract. The parties never did get together and complete the trade. By mutual agreement they rescinded it. This mutual agreement-which the parties were certainly competent in good faith to makeforecloses any consideration as to whether Cooke or

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 95 

Ruff was in the wrong pending the negotiations which finally resulted in a complete rescission of the contract. The Messer Real Estate Company acquired no greater rights to the check than did their principal, Cooke. In this transaction, the rights of the Real 

Estate Company to the $1,000 must be worked out through Cooke; and Cooke, having rescinded the contract, is certainly in no position to claim the check or its proceeds. 

Cooke may owe the Messer Real Estate Company more than the $1,000 involved in this suit, because of Cooke's failure to complete a trade which said Real Estate Company made for him. If this be true, such fact in no way authorizes the said Real Estate Company to retain from Ruff and apply on the Cooke debt money which ex equo et bono belongs to Ruff and to which Cooke is not entitled. The agent is not superior to his principal, and under the facts of this case, the rights of the agent must be measured by the rights of the man in whose shoes, at all times during this matter, he has stood. 

Where money was paid to an agent under an agreement with his principal for the sale of real estate, upon the promise of the agent to return it in case it should turn out that the principal had not a good title, and the sale fell through because the principal had no title in fee to the property, the court held that an action would lie against the agent for the money thus deposited.¹

Where a person purchases a house of an agent, knowing him not to be the owner, and afterwards claims the right to rescind the contract because a mortgage on the premises has not been discharged as 

1 Read vs. Riddle, 7 Atl. Rep. 487.

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money promised, he cannot recover the purchase money in an action against the agent, who has remitted it to his principal. He must therefor sue the principal. 

C, as agent of H, delivered to B, to whom he had sold a house, the following receipt: "Received, Lansing, Mich., December 9, 1884, sixty dollars, to apply on purchase of lot No. 4, in block No. 131, Lansing,

Mich., at the price of $1,100; the balance to be paid in 15 days from this date, provided the title to said lot is proved good, and on delivery of good and sufficient warranty deed. Possession of said premises is this day surrendered to said B." Held, in an action to rescind the sale, and recover the purchase money, that the receipt was not a complete written contract of sale, and that it was competent to show that it had been agreed that a mortgage on the land should be paid out of the purchase money, and that the defendant had procured a discharge to be prepared, as agreed.¹

Where a real estate agent, employed by the vendor in a sale of land, holds the receipt given the purchaser by his principal for money paid thereon, and afterwards the sale is abandoned by the vendor, and the money returned to the agent, who delivers up the receipt, he is so far acting as the agent of the purchaser in the receipt of the money that the latter may maintain an action against him to recover it. (Phelps vs. 

Brown et al., 30 Pac. 774.)

This is an action by the vendee in a contract for the purchase and sale of lands to recover back the sum of $500, paid at the date of the contract as a forfeit. The decision of the superior court was against the plaintiff, and she appeals from the judgment. 

1 Bailey vs. Cornell, 33 Mich. 50.

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 97 

The following are the material facts disclosed by the record: "In June, 1887, one Norton and wife owned a tract of land in Santa Clara County, which was incumbered by a mortgage for $9,000, and the plaintiff, a married woman, owned a house and lot in the city of San Jose. The Nortons wished to exchange their land for the lot of the plaintiff, and to negotiate the exchange they employed the defendants, who were real estate agents doing business in San Jose as partners under the firm name of Brown & Ensign, and orally agreed to pay them $500, as a commission, if the exchange should be made. The proposition of the 

Nortons was that they would convey their land to the plaintiff for $20,000, and that in payment thereof she should assume and pay the mortgage on the land, and should convey her lot to them for $6,500, and pay to them the balance of $4,500 in cash, when the deeds should be executed. This proposition was put in writing and given to the defendants, and they delivered it to the plaintiff. She was willing to accept the proposition and make the trade if she could realize $6,750 for her lot, and not otherwise. The defendants then agreed to pay her $250 out of their commissions when the trade should be consummated. This arrangement was satisfactory, and she thereupon drew her check upon a local bank for $500, payable to the Nortons, and handed the same to the defendants, as a deposit or first payment. The defendants on the same day gave the check to the Nortons, who executed a receipt therefor, closing with the words, 'trade to be finished within two weeks from date, or this deposit to be forfeited without recourse. Title to prove good or no sale, and this deposit to be returned.' A few days later, the defendants handed back to the plain- 2

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money tiff her check, and she thereupon gave to them, in place of the check, $500 in money, which they at once paid over to the Nortons. Subsequently, it appeared from the abstract of title furnished by the plaintiff, that an attachment, issued in an action against her husband, had been levied on her property; and on learning this, the Nortons refused to accept her deed, or to carry out the proposed exchange, unless she would have the attachment removed. She offered to give them a warranty deed, but refused to procure the discharge of the attachment. The Nortons were ready and willing to complete the trade, and tendered a deed of their property to the plaintiff, but she never offered to convey to them an unencumbered title to her property, and never tendered or offered to pay the balance of the purchase money. Thereupon the Nortons abandoned the trade, and without the knowledge of the plaintiff, and without any directions as to the disposition to be made of the money, returned the $500 to the defendants, and the latter returned to them their receipt, with an indorsement thereon as follows: 'Money returned and their receipt is cancelled. Brown & Ensign. July 25, 1887. We agree to release the signers of this receipt from any expense, legal or otherwise. Brown & Ensign.'

Upon these facts two questions arise: First, was the plaintiff entitled to receive her money back when the Nortons abandoned the trade? Second, if so, can she recover it back from the brokers? It is assumed throughout the argument, and we shall so assume without deciding, that the failure or refusal of plaintiff to clear her land of an attachment levied upon it in a suit against her husband was a failure or refusal

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 99 

to do something which her contract bound her to do, and that she thereby placed herself in default exactly as if she had refused to pay an installment of the purchase price when due. But assuming this to be so, and that the Nortons were not in default when they abandoned the trade and put an end to the contract, the plaintiff nevertheless immediately became entitled to receive back the $500 which she had paid, less the actual damage caused by the breach of her contract. 

When a contract of sale and purchase of lands is abandoned or rescinded by the parties, the vendee, though in default, may recover back installments of the purchase money, less the actual damage to the vendor occasioned by his breach of contract. This principle has never been reversed or modified. 

Down Payment Clauses

Great care should be exercised by the broker in drawing the down payment or hand money clause in the Agreements of Sale, in order to protect adequately the interests of his principal. The usual type of clause employed for these purposes reads as follows: "and the party of the second part (purchaser), agrees to pay therefor the sum of $10,000, as follows, to wit: $500 upon the signing of these agreements, receipt of which is hereby acknowledged, and the balance upon the delivery of deed." Should the purchaser default in the performance of his contract, and fail or refuse to go through with the deal, the broker cannot, as he generally believes, retain the $500 by reason of such default. He may legally retain out of the $500 only the damages and expenses which he actually incurred by reason of the default, and should

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money the amount of such expenses be less than the down payment, he would be in duty bound to return to the purchaser the difference. Expenses ordinarily would include broker's commission and the cost of preparing the papers. Another type of clause commonly used reads as follows: "and the party of the second part agrees to pay therefor the sum of $10,- 

000, as follows, to wit: $500 upon the signing of these agreements, the receipt of which is hereby acknowledged, and which sum is to be retained as liquidated damages by the party of the first part (seller), in event of the breach of any of these conditions by the said party of the second part, and all other rights to be at an end." Should the purchaser default, the seller would be permitted to retain the $500, and this would be the sole and exclusive remedy which he would have under the aforementioned clause. A comprehensive type of clause would read as follows: "the said party of the second part agrees to pay therefor the sum of $10,000, as follows: Five hundred ($500)

Dollars upon the signing of these agreements, receipt of which is hereby acknowledged, which deposit it is hereby agreed and understood may, at the option of the party of the first part (seller), be retained by said party of the first part as liquidated damages in case of default by the said party of the second part in the performance of these agreements, and the balance of the purchase money to be paid at the time of delivery of deed." Under the law, if the vendee fails or refuses to make settlement, the vendor has a choice of remedies,-first, either to tender deed, and sue for the whole purchase price, or make a bona fide private or public resale and sue the vendee for the difference between the original purchase price and that of the

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 101 

resale. Sometimes, however, the vendor would prefer to do neither, and would rather retain the deposit money and call the sale off, especially if he believes that he can sell more advantageously later on. The words "at the option of the said party of the first part" must not be omitted, else the Court might take the view that the parties had agreed on the forfeiture of the deposit money as the sole remedy in the case of a breach.

Seller's Liability Upon Default 

Should the seller be the party at fault, the purchaser would have a right of action against the vendor, and the amount of his recovery would depend largely upon whether the defaulting seller had been guilty of fraud. If the breach is unaccompanied by fraud, the buyer could recover only the expenses which he actually incurred. Should the breach of the seller be accompanied by fraud, the buyer could recover the value of his bargain. For example, if a purchaser had agreed to buy property for $10,000 and could prove that he had a bona fide purchaser at a price of $12,000, the $2,000 difference represents the value of his bargain. 

Where real estate brokers, on making a sale, give the vendee a receipt for the first payment, signed by themselves, as agents, in which it is stated that "it is agreed that in case the title proves to be not good, this $1,000 will be refunded by us," they are personally liable to the vendee in case of failure of title, even though the contract of sale signed by the vendor contains a similar provision. 

In Mead et al. vs. Altgeld, 26 N.E. Rep. 388, Julia 

M. Ray, being the owner of lots 4, 5, 6 and 7, block

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 

23, in Canal Trustee's Addition, etc., in the city of 

Chicago, entered into a contract, in writing, with John P. Altgeld to sell and convey said lots to him for $80,000, of which $1,000 was to be paid in cash, and the residue on terms stated in the contract. Aaron 

B. Mead and Albert L. Coe, co-partners, doing business as real estate brokers under the firm name of Mead & Coe, negotiated the sale of said lots to Altgeld as agents for Mrs. Ray. At the time said contract was entered into, Mead & Coe delivered to Altgeld an abstract of the title to said lots, and Altgeld paid to them the sum of $1,000 and received from them the following instrument: "Chicago, March 29, 1886. Received of John P. Altgeld one thousand dollars, 1st payment on contract for purchase of certain lots. It is understood and agreed that, in case the title proves to be not good, this one thousand dollars will be refunded by us to Mr. Altgeld. $1000. Mead & Coe, Agents. Per Westworth." Altgeld submitted said abstract to his attorney, who, after giving it an examination, advised him that Mrs. Ray's title to said lots, as shown by said abstract, was not a good and marketable title; and thereupon Altgeld demanded of 

Mead & Coe the return of the $1,000 paid, and upon their refusal to comply with such demand, this suit was brought against Mead & Coe to recover said money from them.

The contract of the defendants being to refund to the plaintiff the $1,000 paid if the title should prove not good, we are of the opinion that the defect under consideration was sufficiently material to justify the plaintiff in refusing to perform the contract of sale, and demanding back the money paid. He was entitled to a good title, which should at least be a mar-

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money 103 ketable title; that is, a title having no defects which would materially impair its marketable quality. 

Whether the title was good, that is, free from material defects, is a question of law. That question was decided by the trial court in the negative, and our concurrence in that decision is sufficiently indicated by what we have already said. Whether it was a marketable title may perhaps, to some extent, involve questions of fact, but, so far as it does so, the judgment of the appellate court in favor of the plaintiff is conclusive here.

But the defendants contend that, if there is any liability to refund to the plaintiff the $1,000 paid, such liability is upon Mrs. Ray, and not upon them, and that she, and not they, should have been sued. 

The contract between Mrs. Ray and the plaintiff, by which she agreed to sell and convey said lots to him, is in writing, and contains substantially the same provisions in relation to refunding to the plaintiff the $1,000 paid in case the title should prove not good, that is also contained in the defendant's receipt above set forth. The contention is that the two instruments should be construed together as one instrument, and that by such construction both should be held to be contracts of Mrs. Ray, the principal, and not of Mead & Coe, the agents. We are of the opinion that this contention cannot be sustained. The construction of written contracts is a matter of law for the court, and by applying to said receipt the recognized rules of construction, we are of the opinion that it amounted to a personal undertaking on the part of Mead & Coe to refund the $1,000 in case the title should have proven not good. This conclusion need be in no way affected by the fact that Mrs. Ray, in her contract

Broker's Liability for Down Payment Money with the plaintiff, inserted a similar stipulation. The two instruments, though executed at the same time and in relation to the same subject matter, are not necessarily one contract. It was entirely competent for Mead & Coe, if they saw fit to do so, to become personally obligated to the plaintiff to refund him his money if the title should prove defective, and whether they did so or not must depend upon the construction to be given to the instrument to which they had affixed their signatures. "It is undoubtedly competent," says 

Mr. Mechem, "for an agent, although fully authorized to bind his principal, to pledge instead his own personal responsibility, if he so prefers. The presumption is that the agent intends to bind his principal, but where he expressly charges himself personally, he will be held. Such a personal undertaking is not necessarily inconsistent with his character as agent, and where he has so promised personally, the mere addition of the word 'agent,' 'trustee,' etc., to a written promise, will ordinarily be regarded as mere descriptio persona." The instrument sought to be enforced in this suit is signed by Mead & Coe, and the undertaking is "that, in case the title proves to be not good, this $1,000 will be refunded by us to Mr. 

Altgeld." The promise is manifestly their own personal promise, and the addition to their signature of the word "agents" must be regarded then as a mere descriptio personarum.

Where an agent receipts for money in the name of his principal, the purchaser must look to the latter to account for it, and cannot recover from the agent, whether the latter has delivered it to his principal or not.1 

1 McCubbin vs. Graham, 4 Kan. 340,

CHAPTER IX The Pennsylvania Real Estate Broker's License 

Act of May 1, 1929 FO OR ten years previous to 1929, the organized real estate men of Pennsylvania have endeavored to obtain a real estate license law. For one reason or another, their efforts failed until the waning days of the 1929 session of the Legislature. The close coöperation of the local real estate boards and the Pennsylvania Real Estate Association was finally successful in obtaining this important legislation. 

Concessions were made in the provisions of the Act to satisfy different groups in the State and Legislature who opposed it. The Act was not perfect, but it was generally satisfactory to real estate men throughout the State who had sponsored it for a decade. If for anything, it was criticized for not being sufficiently drastic. It may be gratifying to the legislators and to real estate men to know that the Act has been very highly commended by brokers in other states where a similar license law has been in operation for several years prior to the enactment in Pennsylvania. 

It is my purpose, in this chapter, to interpret the Act section by section and to point out where, in a year of administration, it has been found wanting and where it has proved defective. Much credit must be given to the Attorney General for the Commonwealth, Hon. William A. Schnader, and to Hon.

Penrose Hertzler, of the Department of Justice, for 105

their careful study, interpretations, and opinions on questions and problems which have confronted the Department of Public Instruction in its administration of the Act.

Section 1-Name of Act 

Section 1 merely gives the Act its name and reads: "Be it enacted, &c., That this act shall be known, and may be cited, as the Real Estate Brokers' License 

Act of one thousand, nine-hundred and twenty-nine."

Section 2-Real Estate Broker and Salesman De fined

Section 2 is a very important provision, since it defines the terms "real estate broker" and "real estate salesman." Numerous questions have already arisen as to whether a certain person's activities are such as are embraced in the definition of a real estate broker under Section 2 (a). This provision, in substance, divides real estate activity into three divisions: selling, purchasing, and exchanging, which are treated as one division; leasing; and loans on real estate. Each division is then divided into three groups: the actual selling, for example; the negotiating; and the attempt to negotiate. There are two limitations on any person's activities in any of these directions: first, the act must be for another; and, second, it must be for a fee, commission, or other valuable consideration. 

Where the Department attempts to hold a person responsible under the Act, it is necessary to prove both of these limitations. This is sometimes extremely difficult to do, since important witnesses are reluctant to testify and subterfuge is often resorted to for the purpose of defeating the Act. This is true in spite of

a provision in this Section to the effect that the term, "real estate broker," shall include all persons who shall hold themselves out as engaged in the real estate business. It has been contended that a sign with the words, "real estate," was sufficient to constitute one a real estate broker under the terms of the Act and to require a real estate broker's license. For example a sign 

JOHN STEELE REAL ESTATE 

was holding John Steele out to the public to be generally engaged in the real estate business. In one case the Grand Jury ignored a bill of indictment because it believed that it was necessary for the Department to prove that the broker had acted for another and for a fee, that the sign itself was insufficient to warrant a prosecution. This point, therefore, has not been passed upon finally by the courts. 

The second division we have adopted in our classification of real estate brokers includes persons who collect, or offer or attempt to collect, rentals. A distinction should be noted between persons charged with the execution of leases and entrusted with the rent collections and those persons employed by the collecting agency for the ministerial service of going from tenant to tenant, receiving the rent due and receipting for the same. The former requires a license; whereas the latter, whose duties are purely of a routine, perfunctory nature, are exempted from obtaining a license. Apartment house superintendents and jani-

tors who collect rent from the tenants in their charge are similarly exempted for the reasons just stated. 

The third division in our classification includes persons who shall negotiate, or offer or attempt to negotiate, a loan, secured or to be secured by mortgage or other encumbrance upon or transfer of any such real estate. The provision contemplates a loan based upon security. Persons who make loans upon household goods or automobiles are, of course, not included. Many mortgage brokers mistakenly believe that no real estate broker's license is required because they are not engaged in the real estate brokerage business. This is due in large part to the name of the Act-Real Estate Brokers' License Law. The language clearly indicates that a license is necessary for mortgage brokers.

The next provision in Section 2 (a) definitely settles the question of whether or not persons employed by builders or owners of property require a license. 

This clause states that the term, "real estate broker," shall include any person employed by an owner upon a salary, commission, or salary and commission basis, to sell or exchange, or offer or attempt to negotiate the sale or exchange of such real estate. A real estate broker's license is required by such employee or salesman. For such a person to use a real estate salesman's license, it would be necessary for the owner to obtain a broker's license. This is so for the reason that Section 2(b) defines a real estate salesman as one employed by a licensed real estate broker.

The last clause in Section 2(a) effects a radical change in the law as it previously stood. It states that one act in consideration of a fee under any of the three divisions in our classification shall constitute

prima facie evidence that the person so acting, or attempting to act, is a real estate broker within the meaning of this Act. During the World War, and in the real estate activity which followed its termination, many persons supplemented their income by a commission realized from a sale of property. Housewives, mail carriers, stenographers, bank clerks, and others were ever watchful for an opportunity to augment their weekly wage. John Kay, a railroad fireman, knew that his neighbor wanted to sell his house. 

He also knew a friend who would be interested in this particular dwelling. He brought the parties together and could invoke the aid of a court of law to recover an agreed-upon commission, even though he had no license. Courts permitted a recovery on the theory that Kay was acting in a single, isolated transaction as agent for the owner and not as a general broker. 

This proposition is discussed more fully in Chapter II.

Section 2(b) of the Act defines a real estate salesman to be one employed by a licensed real estate broker. A check-up of real estate salesmen disclosed many who were in business for themselves and who, through error, applied for salesmen's licenses. It was therefore necessary for these persons to make immediate application for a broker's license in order to continue in business.

The question has been raised whether a salesman employed by a justice of the peace is exempt from the provisions of the Act for the reason that Section 2(c) of the Act specifically exempts justices of the peace from its provisions. The answer must be in the negative. In order that such a person might operate as a real estate broker, it would be necessary for

him to obtain a broker's license. A salesman's license would be issued to such a person only in the event that the justice of the peace obtained a broker's license. The same regulation would apply to persons employed by builders. This has been discussed in a preceding paragraph.

The last group of persons to be discussed in this connection include office building and apartment building managers. If the office or apartment building manager confines his real estate activity to the management of a single building or unit and receives an annual salary for his services from the owner of the building in question, he is considered an employee and does not require a real estate license. However, a person engaged in the business of renting, leasing, or selling space in an office or apartment building would require a broker's license. It is a ruling of the 

Department of Public Instruction that no individual may hold a salesman's and broker's license at the same time. It is impossible for such an individual to represent fairly and equitably the interests of his employer and at the same time to engage in a competing service for himself. The Department therefore requires a salesman to surrender his salesman's license before it will issue a broker's license to him.

Many real estate brokers in the state have raised the question whether a stenographer in an office, or a clerical employee, requires a license. The answer must depend upon the nature of the duties performed by the person in question, and no uniform rule or regulation can be adopted which will be applicable to every case. If the acts performed are of a routine nature, purely ministerial in character, no license is necessary. On the other hand, if such a per-

son is vested with discretionary powers, and has power to negotiate a lease or sale, he must have a license. 

For exemptions under Section 2 (c) see p. 127. 

Section 3 requires the Department of Public In struction to issue licenses, and Section 4 concerns copies of Department records and papers, and fees for such copies.

Section 5-Department May Not Divulge Informa tion Section 5 of the Act provides for penalties to be imposed where any employee of the Department shall divulge any record, document, or paper in the possession of the Department unless required by subpoena issued by special order of the court, or other regular process. At various times individuals write to the Department demanding to know who informed the Department concerning their activities. The Department is prevented by the operation of this Section from divulging the source of its information, and incidentally from betraying a confidence.

Section 6 requires the Department to issue licenses upon the payment of the proper fee.

Section 7-Application for Broker's License 

Section 7 prescribes the procedure to be followed by an applicant for a license. It provides in substance that a person must make application upon a form prepared by the Department, give such information as the Department may require, and obtain the recommendation of at least two citizens not related to the applicant, who are property owners, certifying to the honesty, truthfulness, and good repute of the applicant and recommending that a license be granted to him.

Fourteen questions are asked in the application form relating to the applicant's name, residence, age, business location, and education. Considerable importance is attached to the question—“Give the location, including street number, for which this license is desired," and also to the question-"Have you been convicted of any crime within the five years just closing?" These questions will be considered in detail under Sections 9 and 10 of the Act. The applicant must make an affidavit that the information given in the application is true, and a license once issued by the Department may be revoked by it if it develops that the licensee had given false information in his application.

There is no educational qualification test in the present law as a prerequisite to the issuance of a real estate license. The neighboring states of Ohio, New 

Jersey, and New York require an educational test. 

The State of New Jersey, however, has waived this requirement in favor of Pennsylvania applicants duly licensed in this State, who desire to obtain a broker's license there. Under a Reciprocity Statute recently enacted by the Legislature of New Jersey, a certified copy of the Pennsylvania license and payment of the license fee is sufficient to obtain a New Jersey license. The educational tests prescribed by these bordering states include the knowledge of reading, writing, elementary arithmetic, fundamentals of the law of conveyancing, leases, mortgages, and the provisions of the license law. Applicants for salesmen's licenses must take the test as well as broker applicants. An amendment to the License Law providing for such test will probably be considered by the 

Assembly at its next session. If enacted, its re-

quirements will not apply to brokers and salesmen licensed prior to the date when the amendment becomes effective. For incoming applicants, a primer will be available containing questions and answers so that an applicant can obtain a very good knowledge of the type of questions asked and the answers required. At the present time, an application blank submitted in proper form, accompanied by a fee for the correct amount, makes it practically mandatory upon the Department to issue the license applied for. 

Under this Section a license is good only for the person named in it. It is of a personal nature and is not transferable. Upon the death of the licensee, the license issued is cancelled.

A corporation is a legal entity, separate and apart from the members who compose it; consequently, a license issued to an individual would not be valid for a corporation organized by the licensee and operated by him exclusively. A new license would be necessary for the corporation. All officers of the corporation who are actively engaged in the real estate business require brokers' licenses. The application for a corporation should be made by an officer in his individual name for the corporation. The license would be issued in the name of the corporation and would permit the officer designated in the application to operate as a broker. Inactive officers of the corporation need not apply. In the case of a partnership, the same provisions now apply as in the case of corporations.

Nonresident applicants may be licensed by complying with the requirements relative to resident brokers and salesmen, except that these requirements may be waived where the Department has entered

into inter-comity or reciprocal arrangements with the state in which the applicant is licensed. Such arrangements now exist between Pennsylvania and New 

York and Pennsylvania and New Jersey. Every nonresident applicant, however, must file an irrevocable consent to service by authorizing the Director of the 

Real Estate Brokers' License Law to accept service in such litigation. Suits and actions may then be commenced against such applicant in the proper court of any county of this State in which a cause of action may arise.

Section 8-Fees 

Section 8 specifies the fees to be paid by an applicant. The fee for a broker's license is ten dollars, and for a salesman's license five dollars. In the case of a partnership or corporation, the application for the firm must be accompanied by a ten dollar fee. Other officers of the corporation or additional members of the partnership firm would be required to pay five dollars each. Renewal fees are one half. The fee for each branch office license is two dollars and fifty cents.

The question has been raised whether a person who is an officer in several real estate corporations requires an individual license for each organization, with the accompanying payment of fees.

Under the language of Section 7(d), it is unnecessary for any officer of a real estate corporation to take out an individual broker's license, unless he desires to act as a real estate broker in connection with the business of the corporation "or otherwise."

Clearly, the Legislature contemplated the possibility that an officer of a real estate corporation might also be an officer in other similar corporations, and it obviously in-

tended that, if such an officer took out in his own name a real estate broker's license, he should be free to act as such to the same extent as if he were an individual not connected with the corporation in an official capacity. 

It is, therefore, not necessary for a person who is an officer in several real estate corporations to take out more than one broker's license in his own name, if the several corporations transact business from the same address. If, however, the corporations have separate places of business at different addresses, and the officer desires to engage actively in the business of each of them, he must comply with Section 7(b) of the Act and take out a duplicate license for each office or place of business in excess of one, from which he proposes to carry on his activities. (See page 124.)

Section 9-Place of Business; Change of Employer 

Section 9(a) is a very important provision in the Act that requires, first, that every broker must maintain a definite place of business within the Commonwealth. The purpose of this provision is to eliminate the curbstone operator-a person who engages in real estate business only when an opportunity for a commission presents itself. No particular form is prescribed as to how elaborate or ornate such a place of business shall be. The living room in a residence, equipped and used as an office, is a sufficient compliance with this provision.

The next requirement under this Section is that the place of business must be specified in the application for license. This provision prevents the broker from moving from place to place, so that the Department cannot readily locate him in case he gets into difficulties at a former location. A broker may move from one address to another, provided he notifies the Department within ten days of any change of loca- -K

tion. Where such notice is given to the Department, a license for the new address is issued without any additional charge. Where such notice as required by the Act is not given, the broker's license is automatically cancelled. During the first year of the operation of the Act, many brokers' licenses were cancelled because of non-compliance with this Section of the Act. 

The broker is further required to display prominently both the license issued to him and licenses issued to his salesmen.

Paragraph (b) of this Section requires that salesmen who leave the employ of one broker to enter the employ of another broker notify the Department within ten days of such change, under penalty of automatic cancellation of license. Where proper notice is given, the new license is issued without charge. Before reissuing a license, however, the Department requires the salesman to obtain a release form executed by the broker whose employ the salesman is leaving, recommending that the license be reissued as requested. In case a broker arbitrarily refuses to execute such release form, the Department will reissue the license without it.

Section 10-Complaints 

Section 10 of the Act specifies and provides the grounds upon which a real estate broker's license may be suspended or revoked by the Department. These grounds include substantial misrepresentation, knowingly making any false promise, a continued or flagrant course of misrepresentation or making of false promises through agents or salesmen, or any other conduct whether of the same or of a different character than that hereinbefore specified, which shall

constitute a tort, or when it shall be shown that the licensee has within five years previous been convicted in a court of competent jurisdiction in this State or any other state of the crimes of forgery, embezzlement, obtaining money under false pretenses, extortion, conspiracy to defraud, or other like offense or offenses. It is this Section which gives teeth to the 

Act. The existence of Section 10 of the statute is often a sufficient deterrent to prevent a broker from pursuing a course of unscrupulous dealing, a thing which he might otherwise do.

In a succeeding chapter appear a number of cases held by the Department under this Section of the Act. It is compulsory upon the Department to refuse a license to an applicant where he has been convicted of any of the crimes named in Section 10 during the five years preceding the application. The department has no discretion or alternative in the matter.

The remainder of Section 10 prescribes the procedure to be followed in acting upon the complaint and in appealing from the decision of the Department. À decision by the Department in favor of the applicant or broker is final and may not be appealed from. 

Where the decision of the Department is adverse to the applicant, an appeal may be taken to the court of common pleas of Dauphin County within thirty days of the filing of the Department's opinion. 

Section 11-Procedure in Handling of Complaints 

Section 11 continues the procedure to be followed in the handling of complaints. Under this Section, where a licensee shall be indicted in Pennsylvania or any other state for the crimes specified in Section 10

of the Act, the Department shall have authority, in its discretion, to suspend a license pending trial upon such indictment. Where a licensee has been convicted of any of the crimes mentioned in Section 10, then the Department must revoke a license for a term of at least one year.

Section 12-Penalties 

Section 12 provides that on and after January 1, 1930, it shall be unlawful for any person to engage in the real estate business in Pennsylvania without first obtaining a license from the Department. The penalties provided for non-compliance with the Act are a fine of fifty to one hundred dollars for the first offense, and a fine of five hundred to one thousand dollars, or imprisonment for one year, or both, for a second and any subsequent offense.

Section 13 authorizes the Department, by its representative, to administer oaths and to issue subpoenas for the attendance of witnesses and the production of papers, books, documents, and the like. 

Section 14 of the Act provides that the violation of the Act by a salesman shall not affect the license of the broker employing him unless it shall be shown that the broker had knowledge of such violation, but that a course of dealing shown to have been persistently and consistently followed by a salesman shall constitute prima facie evidence of such knowledge on the part of the broker.

Section 15-Compensation to Others Than Licensees 

Section 15 is a very important provision. It provides in substance that it shall be unlawful for a

broker or salesman to pay compensation in money or any valuable thing to any person not licensed as a real estate broker or salesman for the rendering of any service, or the doing of any of the acts by this act forbidden to be rendered or performed by other than licensees. The operation of this Section is to the advantage of the real estate broker. It is protection against the unreasonable demands of persons who appear at the time of closing and attempt to collect a split on the commission, under threats of having the deal called off. Often such an individual is related to one of the principals in the transaction and consequently possesses considerable influence, with the result that the broker very frequently has to capitulate to the demands of this extortionist. 

Under Section 15 of the Act, it would be unlawful for the broker to pay or share his commission with such a person unless the latter was licensed. If such a person persists in his demands, prosecution can be instituted against him under Section 12. Under the terms of Section 15 (b), it is unlawful for a real estate salesman to accept any compensation or commission from any person other than the broker who employed him. This Section gives to the broker a direct control and supervision over his salesmen which he did not previously possess. Payment of commission by a broker to an unlicensed person, or the acceptance of compensation by a salesman from persons other than his employer shall be sufficient cause for suspension or revocation of his license.

Section 16 provides that a real estate broker shall have no standing in a court of law unless he has complied with the requirements of the Act of 1929, and Section 17 of the Act authorizes the Department to

establish all rules and regulations necessary to carry out the provisions of the Act. 

Section 18-Saving Clause 

The concluding Section 18 is the saving clause of the statute. It provides that if any section, subsection, sentence, clause, phrase, or requirement of the Act is declared unconstitutional, it shall have no bearing and effect upon the remainder of the Act. This is a very important requirement. The Act has been attacked in several cases on the grounds that the exception of attorneys at law, banking institutions, and justices of the peace under Section 2 (c) of the Act is a violation of Article 3, Section 7, of the Constitution of Pennsylvania, which prohibits special or class legislation. Each time the court has sustained the validity of the Act as a proper exercise of the police power and that the exceptions are reasonable. However, should the particular Section in question be declared unconstitutional, it would nevertheless have no effect upon the remainder of the statute. 

An important amendment to the Real Estate Brokers' License Act, approved by the Governor on 

June 26, 1931, provides in substance as follows: 

1. An applicant for a real estate broker's license must be twenty-one years of age and upwards, and a citizen of the United States. 

2. (a) Every real estate broker shall display on the outside of his place of business a sign containing the words "Real Estate" or "Realtor." (b) All licenses must be prominently displayed. (c) Violation shall be sufficient cause for suspension or revocation of license.

3. Additional grounds are provided for suspension or revocation of license, as follows: (a) Any failure to account for or to pay over moneys belonging to others, which has come into his, her, or its possession, arising out of a real estate transaction. (b) Any misleading or untruthful advertising. (c) Any act or conduct in connection with a real estate transaction which demonstrates incompetency, bad faith, or dishonesty. 

4. Renewal fees for a broker's license are reduced from ten dollars ($10.00) to five dollars ($5.00), and renewal fees for a salesman's license are reduced from five dollars ($5.00) to two dollars and fifty cents ($2.50). 

5. Where an appeal is taken from the decision of the Department of Public Instruction, it is necessary for the appellant to file his reasons for such appealed action. 

6. The following provisions have been eliminated from the Act of 1929: (a) The court may, however, in its discretion, receive and consider any additional pertinent evidence, whether oral or written, either in open court or by deposition as the said court shall direct, and the decision of the department shall be reviewed in the light of such evidence, provided the court shall order a trial by jury upon demand of any appellant. (b) The elimination of the provision for payment of one half fees where application for license is made after July 1.

CHAPTER X Formal Opinions of the Department of Justice 

Harrisburg, Pennsylvania

January 2, 1930 

Doctor John A. H. Keith,

Superintendent of Public Instruction, 

Harrisburg, Pennsylvania. Sir:

We have your request to be advised upon a number of questions which have arisen in the administration of the Act of May 1, 1929, P. L. 1216, known as the Real Estate Brokers License Act of 1929.

In view of the large number of your questions, we shall answer them as we state them.

Does an auctioneer, who occasionally sells real estate, as an auctioneer, require a broker's license? 

Auctioneers must be licensed under the Act of May 

5, 1921, P. L. 406. Having been thus licensed, they may, in our opinion, sell at auction property of any character without any further license. This includes real estate as well as personal property. 

The Real Estate Brokers License Act does not specifically mention auctioneers, and, in our judgment, they do not come within the definition of "real estate brokers" contained in Section 2(a) of the Act, if their transactions in connection with real estate are 122

Formal Opinions of Department of Justice 123 

confined to sales at auction. If, however, they sell real estate or offer it for sale otherwise than at auction, they come within the purview of the Act. II Does a person who is an officer in several real estate corporations require a separate broker's license for each organization?

The issuance of brokers' licenses to corporations is covered by Section 7 (d) of the Act, which provides that: 

Where a real estate broker's license shall be issued to a corporation or association, authority to transact business thereunder shall be limited to one officer of such corporation or association, to be designated in the application and named in the license. Each other officer of such association or corporation, desiring to act as a real estate broker in connection with the business of the said association or corporation or otherwise, shall be required to make application for and take out a separate license in his or her own name individually. . . • • Under the language quoted, it is unnecessary for any officer of a real estate corporation to take out an individual broker's license, unless he desires to act as a real estate broker in connection with the business of the corporation "or otherwise."

Clearly, the Legislature contemplated the possibility that an officer of a real estate corporation might also be an officer in other similar corporations, and it obviously intended that, if such an officer took out in his own name a real estate broker's license, he should be free to act as such to the same extent as if he were

Formal Opinions of Department of Justice an individual not connected with the corporation in an official capacity. 

It is, therefore, not necessary for a person who is an officer in several real estate corporations to take out more than one broker's license in his own name, if the several corporations transact business from the same address. If, however, the corporations have separate places of business at different addresses, and the officer desires to engage actively in the business of each of them, he must comply with Section 7(b) of the Act and take out a duplicate license for each office or place of business in excess of one, from which he proposes to carry on his activities. III

Section 9 of the Act states that every broker shall be required to have and maintain a definite place of business within the Commonwealth. Does a broker who transacts his business from a private residence, without any sign indicating that he is engaged in the real estate business, meet the requirements of this provision?

All that Section 9(a) of the Act requires is that each licensed real estate broker shall have and maintain a definite place of business within Pennsylvania, which shall serve as an office for the transaction of business, under the authority of the license, and where the license shall be prominently displayed. 

A place of business where the license shall be prominently displayed must be one which is open to the broker's clients, and must be an office in the usual sense in which that word is used. It may be located in

Formal Opinions of Department of Justice 125 

any type of building, including a private residence, and the law does not require that there must be a sign indicating that the broker is engaged in the real estate business.2

IV 

Referring to Section 2, does the term, "real estate salesman," include an employee or stenographer in an office who, when other members of the firm are out of the office, offers property for sale or rent, or negotiates loans, all in accordance with the established policy of the firm?

Section 2(b) provides that the term "real estate salesman" shall mean and include any person employed by a licensed real estate broker "to sell or offer for sale, to buy or offer to buy, or to negotiate the purchase, sale or exchange of any real estate, or interest in real estate, the property of another, or to negotiate a loan upon real estate, or to lease or rent or offer to lease or rent or place for rent any such real estate."

A stenographer or office boy is not employed for the foregoing purposes; and the mere giving of information in the absence of the licensed broker and the licensed salesmen of the office would not, in our opinion, be such an act as would require the stenographer or office boy to be licensed as a real estate salesman. However, a person employed for general work in a real estate broker's office, including stenography and receiving and waiting upon the public, would be obliged to have a salesman's license. 

1 Changed by amendment approved June 26, 1931.

Formal Opinions of Department of Justice 

V Section 15 of the Act declares that it shall be unlawful for any licensed real estate broker to pay any compensation to any person other than a licensed real estate broker or salesman. May a licensed broker share a commission with one of those persons mentioned in Section 2(c), such as attorneys at law, justices of the peace, etc.?

Section 15(a) of the Act is as follows: 

It shall be unlawful for any licensed real estate broker, or real estate salesman, to pay any compensation, in money or other valuable thing, to any person other than a licensed real estate broker or real estate salesman, for the rendering of any service, or the doing of any of the acts by this act forbidden to be rendered or performed by other than licensees.

This section of the Act does not express what the Legislature had in mind with that precision which is desirable in legislation, and it is difficult to construe it consistently with Section 2 of the Act. 

Section 15 (a) purports to prohibit the payment of compensation by a licensed broker or licensed salesman to any person other than a licensed broker or salesman, for the rendering of any service or the doing of any act "forbidden to be rendered or performed by other than licensees."

In view of the exemptions contained in Section 2(c), there are no services or acts which the Act forbids any person other than licensees to render or perform. Section 2(a) specifies the acts which shall constitute an individual, association, or corporation a

Formal Opinions of Department of Justice 127 

? real estate broker, but Section 2 (c) provides that the term "real estate broker" shall not be held to include "in any way" attorneys at law and justices of the peace, nor shall it include an owner who is selling his own property, a person holding a bona fide letter of attorney from an owner for the disposition of the owner's property, a receiver, a trustee in bankruptcy, an administrator, an executor, or any other person or corporation acting under the appointment or order of any court, a trustee, or a banking institution operating under the banking laws of Pennsylvania.

Accordingly, all of the acts specified in Section 

2(a) may be lawfully performed by real estate brokers, by attorneys, by justices of the peace, or by any other person specifically mentioned in Section 2(c). 

What the Legislature probably intended to provide in Section 15(a) was that it would be unlawful for any licensed broker or licensed salesman to pay compensation to any other than a licensed broker or salesman for rendering any service or doing any act forbidden to be rendered or performed by persons other than licensees or those persons specifically exempted by Section 2(c) from the necessity of obtaining licenses.

Clearly, an attorney or justice of the peace may negotiate the sale of real estate and divide the compensation with a licensed real estate broker or salesman. On the other hand, if Section 15 (a) has any effect whatsoever, it would seem that a licensed broker or a licensed salesman cannot lawfully divide his compensation with an attorney, a justice of the peace, or any of the other persons, associations, or corpora-

Formal Opinions of Department of Justice tions mentioned in Section 2 (c) as exempt from the necessity of taking out brokers' or salesmen's licenses. 

Until this question actually comes before you in the administration of the Act, we shall not answer it decisively.

VI Must each and every one of the officers of a corporation conducting a real estate business secure a license? Must each member of a partnership firm apply for individual license, irrespective of whether such persons are actively engaged in the sale of real estate?

It is not necessary for each officer of a corporation conducting a real estate business to secure a broker's license. Officers are required to take out individual licenses only when "desiring to act as real estate brokers in connection with the business of the said corporation or otherwise." With partnerships the situation is different. The law specifically provides that the broker's license issued to a partnership shall confer authority to act as a broker only upon one member of the partnership, who shall be designated in the application and named in the license. It then provides that, "All the other members of such copartnership shall be required to apply for and take out individual licenses in their own names." This requirement is not qualified as is the similar requirement in the case of a corporation. It is, therefore, necessary for each member of a partnership, other than the one named in the firm's license, to apply for an individual license.2 

1 Changed by amendment approved June 26, 1931.

Formal Opinions of Department of Justice 129 

VII Does a builder who employs a salesman on a salary basis, who sells property which the builder owns, need to be licensed as a broker? Must the salesman of such a builder, on a salary basis, be licensed? If on a commission basis, must the salesman be licensed?

A builder who sells property which he owns is not a real estate broker. Section 2(c) specifically provides that he shall not be so regarded.

Under Section 2 (b) a real estate salesman is to be regarded as such only when he is employed by a licensed real estate broker for the purposes therein specified. As the builder is not a real estate broker, a salesman employed by him is not a real estate salesman.

On the other hand, Section 2(a) defines "real estate broker" to include all persons "who, for another and for a fee, commission, or other valuable consideration, shall sell . . . real estate." This definition clearly brings within its scope a salesman employed by an owner to sell real estate on a salary basis and such salesman must, therefore, take out a broker's license. VIII

Does a justice of the peace who employs a real estate salesman need to be licensed as a broker?

A justice of the peace is not required under any circumstances to take out a real estate broker's license, but the salesman employed by a justice of the peace

Formal Opinions of Department of Justice must take out a real estate broker's license for the reasons stated in answering your previous question. IX

Must the Department of Public Instruction is sue a license to all persons whose applications are properly filled out as indicated in Section 7(b); or can the Department investigate complaints, hold hearings, and if necessary, refuse a license under 

Section 10(b)? In short, in starting this licensing function, may we refuse a license to any person whose application is properly filled out? In still other words, must we, in starting the matter, license everybody and then proceed to the revocation of licenses?

The Legislature clearly contemplated that your 

Department should not grant a license to persons guilty of any of the practices specified in Section 10(a). Section 10 (c) provides that, "The refusal of the department to issue any license, after application properly made and compliance by the applicant with the requirements of this act, shall be subject to review by the court of common pleas of Dauphin County. 

In our judgment, it is your duty to refuse a license if charges are preferred against an applicant and if upon investigation and after notice and hearing your Department believes that the charges have been sustained.

It would be absurd to license all applicants notwithstanding the pendency of serious charges against some of them; and a reading of Section 10 of the Act • •

Formal Opinions of Department of Justice 131 

makes it clear that the Legislature did not intend your Department to pursue this course. 

Very truly yours,

DEPARTMENT OF JUSTICE. 

By William A. Schnader,

Attorney General.

Real Estate Broker- 

Protest Against 

-Wrongful Acts 

-Act of 1929,

A protest against the granting of a real estate broker's license was dismissed and license granted, where the complaint was based entirely upon the transcript of a proceeding in a case in the common pleas court, wherein applicant had been cited for contempt of court. To refuse or revoke a license it would be necessary under the Real Estate 

Brokers' License Act of 1929, P. L. 427, to prove wrongdoing with the public generally, or with those for whose benefit and protection the Act was passed. This complaint did not come within the purview of the legislative intent. 

An Applicant for a real estate broker's license was granted a license, where there was no evidence that he was guilty of any of the acts cited in Section 10 of the Act of 1929, P. L. 427, in his dealings with the public. It is not within the province of the Department of Public Instruction 132

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 133 

to delve into the merits of the relationship between the parties, unless it involves Subsection 4 of Section 10(a), of the Act, which reads, "Of any other conduct, whether of the same or of a different character than that hereinafter specified, which shall constitute a tort." The burden of proof was on the Complainant. 

This is a hearing on the complaint of one Frank A. 

Weinstein, filed with the Department of Public Instruction, against the issuance of a real estate broker's license to C. L. Saxton, under the provisions of the 

Real Estate Brokers' License Act of 1929, P. L. 

427, setting forth and alleging that the latter has been grossly guilty of misrepresentation as set forth in final decree of the Court of Common Pleas of Allegheny County, sitting in Equity at No. 125 April 

Term, 1928.

The application is in correct form and properly submitted. A hearing on the said complaint was held 

January 4, 1930, before Robert W. Semenow, Esquire, of the Department of Public Instruction, Commonwealth of Pennsylvania; and it is for the disposal of said complaint that this opinion is filed.

In this opinion, C. L. Saxton shall be referred to as the Applicant; Frank A. Weinstein as the Complainant; and Robert W. Semenow as the Referee.

As evidence in this case, a transcript of the evidence heard before the Court of Common Pleas sitting in Equity at No. 125 April Term, 1928, is admitted, together with copy of order of court at said term and copy of amended order of court, citing the Applicant for contempt of court for failure to comply with previous order at No. 125 April Term, 1928.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

This testimony, submitted in evidence and marked "Exhibit A," is a voluminous record of 428 pages. 

The testimony is quite involved, and the Referee shall, therefore, attempt to cull out these instances of fraud and misrepresentation, or at least those circumstances which approximate fraud and misrepresentation and which must be relied upon by the Complainant to prove his case.

The facts disclosed at the controversy between the Applicant and Complainant center about the management and sale of certain property in the City of 

Pittsburgh known as the "Penn Dwellings," at the corner of Homewood and Penn Avenues. It appears from the evidence, and the fact is undisputed between the parties, that the Complainant, finding himself in serious financial difficulties in the summer of 1926, transferred and conveyed the aforementioned premises to the Applicant as trustee for the Complainant and his wife on July 6, 1926 (page 204). The

Applicant thereupon took over the active management of the property in question. It appears that he advertised the property for sale, which he admits was done without obtaining the Complainant's consent (page 249). 

On November 26, 1926, the Applicant negotiated with one George C. Smith of New York for the sale of Penn Dwellings at a consideration price of $135,- 000, without disclosing the Complainant's interest in the property to the purchaser (page 243). A sale at the price of $135,000 was subsequently made to 

George C. Smith, which purchase was made sight un-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 135 

seen; and Complainant alleges that it was not made in good faith, especially so since the Applicant's wife is a niece of the purchaser's sister-in-law. 

Complainant called expert witnesses, who testified that the value of the property at the time of the sale was between $185,000 and $200,000. Witnesses for the Applicant refuted this and testified the value of the property to be around the price realized on the sale. It appears that the Complainant purchased the property for $197,000.

It appears from the evidence produced by both sides of the controversy that the actual value of the property in question is seriously disputed. II The next item of alleged fraud or misrepresentation concerns a $10,000 charge made by the Applicant against the Complainant for the payment of a $10,- 000 mortgage to the Merchants' Trust Company on a certain Butler Street property in Pittsburgh, which mortgage was not satisfied as of record. The mortgage was then assigned by Applicant to the Marine Bank as part security for a $67,000 indebtedness. 

The statements submitted to the Complainant by the Applicant from time to time were $10,000 higher than the amount he owed Applicant and also included interest on the $10,000 (pages 98-101).

III 

Next, the facts disclosed that Applicant charged Complainant with interest on money owed the Marine Bank and, at the same time, charged him interest on

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction money paid the bank from time to time, which amounted to double interest in the sum of $697.50.

Applicant admitted this to be correct, but claimed that it was an oversight (page 108). It was specifically admitted to be double interest, and there are similar instances of double interest on other items (page 118). 

IV 

Applicant admits further that he charged Complainant with commission of $4,050 on the sale of the Penn Dwellings to Smith, although the sale was made direct from him as trustee for the Complainant. The evidence shows that a charge of $45,000 was made by the Applicant for the purpose of re-financing Complainant's property, the Penn Dwellings, and that the Applicant admitted that he agreed to make the Marine Bank a present of $10,000 out of said $45,000 commission or bonus (page 186).

VI The Complainant, in his testimony at the hearing on the application before the Referee, disclosed nothing more than is revealed in the testimony submitted as Exhibit A and summarized above. No other witnesses were called in his behalf or to impeach the general reputation or business dealings of the Applicant. 

Four character witnesses testified on behalf of the 

Applicant's good business reputation.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 137  

At a hearing on the complaint, counsel for the Applicant questioned the jurisdiction of the Department of Public Instruction to conduct the hearing, and this question will first be disposed of. Section 

10(c) of the Real Estate Brokers' License Act of 

1929, P. L. 427, reads as follows:

The refusal of the department to issue and license, after application properly made, and compliance by the applicant with the requirements of this act, shall be subject to review by the Court of Common Pleas of Dauphin County, upon petition for writ of mandamus, or other appropriate remedy, with the right of appeal to the applicant as in other and similar cases.

Paragraph (a) of the same section recites the grounds upon which a license may be revoked or suspended; and paragraph (d) of the same section states that, before refusing, suspending, or revoking any license, certain procedure shall be followed in notifying the applicant of the charges against him and for the conduct of hearing, all of which was observed in this case. It is readily apparent, then, that the Department has the power to refuse licenses where the facts warrant, subject to review by the Court of Common Pleas of Dauphin County.

II Section 10 is the only part of the Act involved in this case, and it is well to set down the language of Paragraph (a), since the Complainant's case must

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction stand or fall upon the application of this section to the particular facts in his case. Section 10 (a) reads as 

The department may, upon its own motion, and shall, promptly upon the verified complaint in writing of any person setting forth specifically the wrongful act or acts complained of, investigate any action or business transaction of any licensed real estate broker or real estate salesman; and shall have the power temporarily to suspend or permanently to revoke licenses theretofore issued by the department, under the provisions of this act, at any time when, after due proceedings as hereinafter provided, it shall find the holder thereof to have been guilty, in the performance or attempt to perform any of the acts prohibited to others than licensed real estate brokers or real estate salesmen under the provisions of this act, (1) Of knowingly making any substantial misrepresentation; or (2) Of knowingly making any false promise of a character likely to influence, persuade or induce; or (3) Of a continued or flagrant course of misrepresentation, or making of false promises through agents or salesmen; or (4) Of any other conduct, whether of the same or of a different character than that hereinbefore specified, which shall constitute a tort, or when it shall be shown that the licensee, within five years prior to the issuance of the license then in force, has been convicted in a court of competent jurisdiction of this or any other State of forgery, embezzlement, obtaining money under false pretenses, extortion, conspiracy to defraud, or other like offense or offenses. 

It is the Referee's opinion and belief that the Legislature intended to apply this section only to brokers and salesmen in their dealings with the public generally, in whose interest and for whose benefit this legislation was enacted. A person defrauded by a real

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 139 

estate broker in the purchase of land or property can obtain redress by complaining to the Department of Public Instruction against the issuance of a license to the guilty broker, and the complaint will be given consideration should the broker in question apply for a license. The grounds for complaint, in the main, must concern the commodity of real estate, in which the broker is trafficking. It must be material and must have been uttered for the purpose of misleading the prospective purchaser and as a means of inducing him to make the purchase. Promises of resale at fabulous prices, guarantees of enormous profits, representations of an untrue nature made to purchasers as to the quality and character of the property, are statements which fall under the prohibitions of the provisions of the Act. III In the case at issue, the controversy concerns Applicant and Complainant only, under an agreed-upon written arrangement, in the form of a trusteeship. 

There is no evidence whatsoever that the Applicant was guilty of any of the acts cited in Section 10, in his dealings with the public. The complaint deals exclusively with the Applicant's stewardship as trustee of property belonging to the Complainant. This matter has been litigated in the Court of Common Pleas of Allegheny County for the past two years, and the respective rights and liabilities of the parties to the trustee agreement will be definitely and finally adjudicated by the courts of the Commonwealth. Consequently, it is not within the province of this Department to delve into the merits of the relationship

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction between the parties, unless it involves Subsection 4 of 

Section 10 (a), which reads, "Of any other conduct, whether of the same or of a different character than that hereinbefore specified, which shall constitute a tort."

Certainly there are several fundamental duties which the Applicant owed to the Complainant, the most important of which was loyalty to his trust, and upon which his other duties are based. 

It is charged that the Applicant failed to observe those canons of ethics in the administration of the estate which the law requires. Whether the Applicant was guilty of a dereliction of duty and violated his trust is now being disposed of in the courts of law, and we are concerned with such violations only if such conduct constitutes a tort as stated in the fourth subsection of Section 10(a) of the Act. It is our belief that this provision again refers to a broker's relations to the public and we are not convinced that the acts complained of constitute such a tort action as is contemplated by the Act. We find that no criminal proceedings have been undertaken by the Complainant against the Applicant since their dispute arose.

IV 

No tort action has been brought in this case, and we are convinced that the testimony is insufficient to place the Applicant within the provisions of the Act just cited.

The Complainant has failed to produce a single witness to prove that the Applicant's actions made. him amenable to the penalties of the Act of 1929, or

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 141 

that he had committed any of the acts enumerated in Section 10 (a), in his relations to the public. Complainant has failed to produce any evidence as to the bad reputation of the Applicant in a business way; or that the Applicant is not a satisfactory person to engage in the real estate business; or that the 

Applicant comes under Subsection 4, Section 10(a), of the Act; or that the issuance of a license to the 

Applicant would constitute a danger to the public. 

There was no responsibility upon the Applicant to prove his good reputation. The affidavit of the two property owners in his application, testifying that the Applicant bears a good reputation for honesty, truthfulness and good repute, and recommending that the license be granted to the Applicant, would have been sufficient. It was incumbent upon the Complainant to prove the Applicant otherwise; and this he has failed to do. 

The complaint is, therefore, dismissed. 

McAnulty's Petition 

Real Estate Broker- -Failure to Account for 

Moneys Collected 

-Notice of Sale of Lot- Power of Department of Public Instruction- 

Act of 1929, P. L. 1216.

A real estate broker was refused a license and he was ordered and directed to refrain from engaging in the real estate business, directly or indirectly, and to remove any and all signs and evidences holding himself out to the public to be so engaged, where it was charged that the applicant

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction had collected moneys or rents belonging to the Complainant and had failed and refused to account to the Complainant for the proceeds from such payments. 

The Department of Public Instruction, under the Act of 1929, P. L. 1216, has the power to refuse a license to a real estate broker upon any of the grounds enumerated in Section 10(a) of the Act. The Applicant violated his duties to his principal when he failed to notify the Complainant that the purchaser of a lot had completed his payments on the lot in question and, therefore, that the particular lot in question was no longer available for sale. The Applicant further failed to observe those responsibilities he owed the Complainant in neglecting to send proper financial statements to the Complainant, showing the collection of moneys on the lots and the expenditure of such sums received in the interest of his principal. License refused. NO. 4-1930 

Complaint of R. H. Bellman is made against the issuance of a Real Estate Broker's License by the Department of Public Instruction to R. Milo Mc- 

Anulty, in accordance with the Real Estate Brokers' 

License Act of 1929, P. L. 1216, the complaint setting forth and alleging in substance that the Applicant had collected moneys or rents belonging to the Complainant and had failed and refused to account to the Complainant for the proceeds from such payments. There are two complaints filed with the Department, dated December 17, 1929, and January 10, 1930. In view of the fact that the statements setting forth the fraud or dishonest acts complained of are substantially the same in both complaints, this opinion will treat both complaints as one.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 143 

I The business relations between Complainant and 

Applicant date back to December 1913, when the

Complainant gave to the Applicant authority to sell lots in the Braddock Park Plan in Allegheny County, which lots the Complainant purchased from the Applicant and another. The grantors took back a blanket mortgage for $10,000 upon the premises conveyed with a release clause of $100 per lot. This mortgage was subsequently assigned by the Applicant. 

The sale of lots occurred over a long period of years and the proceeds from such sales, it is alleged by the Applicant, were distributed in the form of taxes, payments on the mortgage, and commissions. II The controversy between the Complainant and the Applicant specifically concerns the sale of a certain lot No. 971 in the Braddock Park Plan to Joseph

Vichinsky. Negotiations for the sale of this lot originated in 1920, and payments were made by the purchaser until 1925. The transaction concerning the purchase of this lot has not yet been concluded between the parties and no deed has yet been delivered to the purchaser, although he has made full payment of the purchase price. It appears that two agreements for Lot No. 971 were signed with the purchaser by the Applicant,-the first was executed by the Applicant in his own name, without revealing to the purchaser the Applicant's representative capacity; the second, executed shortly thereafter, is signed by the Applicant as agent, without disclosing the identity

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction of his principal. It is admitted by the Applicant that he has received payment of the consideration price from the purchaser of Lot No. 971. The Applicant testified (page 29) that the second agreement for 

Lot No. 971 was signed the same night as the first, and that he believed it was acknowledged on the same day. The acknowledgment is dated June 21, 1926six years after the agreements were drawn. The Applicant further admitted that he had no authority from his principal to make the acknowledgment and that he knew the purpose for which the acknowledgment was desired-i. e., to have the instrument recorded.

III 

The facts disclosed that the Applicant sent only sporadic statements of reports of a fragmentary nature to the Complainant. The Applicant's books of account, exhibited at the hearing, failed to reveal the status of any particular lot as regards the payments made upon it and disbursements, and failed to show in any way total amounts received by the Applicant for the Complainant or how such funds were distributed. The Applicant and his son both testified that statements were rendered when necessary at thirtyday or three-month intervals and that the statements were ample, although no supporting evidence, either in a copy of the statements sent or the account books, supported these assertions. Such statements as were occasionally sent were in the form of letters. In one such letter-statement sent by the Applicant to the Complainant, the Applicant wrote, "We persuaded a buyer to accept a deed without getting the title

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 145 

affirmed, as a title search would reveal that all taxes had not been paid."

In June 1926, the Complainant wrote to the Applicant instructing him not to sell Lot No. 971, as he had a prospective purchaser for it. The evidence shows that at that time the Applicant had already received payment in full, or purchase price, for Lot 

No. 971 from the purchaser, Joseph Vichinsky, and the Applicant failed to notify his principal that such was the case (page 23).

This opinion is concerned only with that transaction between the Applicant and Complainant involving the sale of Lot No. 971 in the Braddock Park 

Plan, Allegheny County, to Joseph Vichinsky. While the negotiations were inaugurated as early as 1920 and agreements of sale were executed at that time, payments on account by the purchaser continued until October 1925; and the controversy among all the parties concerning the lot in question continues until the present time. This Department has ruled, in Saxton's Petition, 78 Pittsburgh Legal Journal, page 90, that acts of an applicant for a broker's license, committed prior to January 1, 1930, may be inquired into by the Department in acting upon an application for a license. This Department has the power, as stated in Saxton's Petition, to refuse a license where the facts warrant, subject to review by the
