Court of Common Pleas of Dauphin County.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

II The Department of Public Instruction, under the Act of 1929, has the power to refuse a license upon any of the grounds enumerated in Section 10(a) of the Act. The sole question to be determined here is whether the facts prove the Applicant guilty of any of the acts prohibited in Section 10 of the Act. The application for license reveals the fact that Applicant has been engaged in the real estate business for thirty-five years; and it is presumed, therefore, that he is or should be familiar with those fundamental duties which every broker owes to a principal and to the public generally. Those fundamental duties include the exercise of good faith, a reasonable diligence and ordinary skill, disclosure to his principal of any information he may have relevant to his agency, and an accounting for all moneys and property belonging to his principal. The evidence reveals three specific instances wherein the Applicant failed to observe those principles which his employment demanded.

III 

Without delving into the reasons why the first agreement for the sale of Lot No. 971 was made out by the Applicant as owner instead of as agent, and assuming that it was an honest mistake by the Applicant which he promptly rectified, it is apparent from the testimony that the Applicant acknowledged the agreements of sale without permission of the owner and that such acknowledgment was made for the purpose of having the instrument recorded. It is not within the province of this Department to in-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 147 

quire into any undisclosed motives which the Applicant may have had for the doing of this act, although he testified it was done at the request of the purchaser's attorney. It is the opinion of this Department that the Applicant exceeded his authority in making the acknowledgment without consulting his principal and without even attempting to obtain his approval.

The Applicant violated his duties to his principal when he failed to notify the Complainant that the purchaser of Lot No. 971 had completed his payments on the lot in question and, therefore, that the particular lot in question was no longer available for sale. The Applicant further failed to observe those responsibilities he owed the Complainant in neglecting to send proper financial statements to the Complainant, showing the collection of moneys on the lots and the expenditure of such sums received in the interest of his principal. There is no way of knowing how the payments made to the Applicant were applied. The Complainant could not know from the letters sent him as statements and from the books of account and records submitted by the Applicant at the hearing, the financial history of any particular sale or the status of the entire tract sold by the Applicant for the Complainant. An inspection of the 

Applicant's books fails to reveal whether the total moneys received by the Applicant have been properly expended, or whether there is any balance due the Complainant and, if so, in what amount; or, on the other hand, whether there is any money due the Applicant.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

V While it is true that a broker owes certain duties to his principal, yet there are even more important duties which a broker owes to the public generally; and it was primarily for the protection of the public that this Act was passed. This Department will not countenance such methods of doing business as are evidenced by the testimony of the Applicant on pages 

20 and 21:

Question by Mr. Semenow: What form of statements did you give Mr. Bellman in regard to the status of these lots? 

A. Why just a regular statement. Here is a letter I wrote to Mr. Bellman. I persuaded a buyer to accept a deed without getting it examined. If he got a lawyer they would have found all taxes not paid. 

Q. Do I understand that you persuaded a buyer to accept a deed without having the title examined for the reason that the attorneys' examination would show certain taxes to be unpaid? 

A. Yes, that is what the letter says, doesn't it?

VI This Department holds that such actions fall within the language of Section 10, and constitute grounds for the refusal or revocation of a license. 

It is, therefore, the opinion of this Department that the Applicant has been guilty of acts and conduct prohibited by the Act of 1929, and the applicant's request for a real estate broker's license is hereby refused, with leave, however, given to the Applicant to reapply on or after June 1, 1930.

The Applicant is hereby ordered and directed to refrain from engaging in the real estate business,

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 149 

directly or indirectly, and to remove any and all signs and evidences holding himself out to the public to be so engaged. 

Frey's Petition 

Real Estate Broker- -Commission- -Complaint to Revoke License-Place of Business- 

Act of 1929, P. L. 1216.

A complaint to revoke the license granted a real estate broker because he accepted a commission of less than three per cent was refused. There is no provision in the Act of 

1929, P. L. 1216, which regulates, or attempts to regulate, the rate of commission which a licensed real estate broker may charge in the sale of real estate. That matter is left entirely to the voluntary agreement between the broker and the seller.

NO. 8-1930 

The complaint of Charles H. Hatt, a licensed real estate broker of the city of Reading, Pennsylvania, for the revocation of the real estate broker's license of William Frey of Reading, Pennsylvania, is based on the grounds that the latter has violated Section 

15(a) of the Real Estate Brokers' License Act of 

1929, P. L. 1216, which reads:

It shall be unlawful for any licensed real estate broker, or real estate salesman, to pay any compensation, in money or other valuable thing, to any person other than a licensed real estate broker or real estate salesman Complainant contends that the Department of Public Instruction may revoke William Frey's li-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction cense under paragraph (c) of the same section, which provides: 

The violation of the provisions of this section by any licensee shall be sufficient cause for the suspension or revocation of his license, in the discretion of the department. This action concerns the sale of certain premises, located at 1544 Mulberry Street, in the city of Reading, Pennsylvania, owned by a Mr. Daniel Babb.

The testimony is clear and undenied, that both the Complainant and the Licensee were authorized to negotiate for the sale of the aforementioned premises, and that both brokers produced prospective purchasers. Previously, the Licensee was the broker instrumental in negotiating the sale of the premises to Daniel Babb, for which services he received a commission. It appears that the Complainant produced the first purchaser, Joseph Coyle, and that said purchaser agreed and was willing to pay a price of $5400 for the property. The Licensee, shortly afterward, produced a purchaser, Harry Bryner, who actually purchased the property at a consideration price of $5300. The seller, Daniel Babb, paid to the Licensee the sum of $25 as commission for his services. It appears from the evidence that the seller would have been obliged to pay the Complainant the sum of $162 as commission, which represents the rate of three per cent on the sales price of $5400. The testimony of the seller, Daniel Babb, is to the effect that he accepted the Licensee's prospect instead of the Complainant's because, in that way, he would receive more money from the deal; and it was, therefore, to his pecuniary

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 151 

benefit to convey the property to Harry Bryner rather than to Joseph Coyle, the Complainant's prospect.

II The testimony offered at the hearing reveals that the Licensee is engaged primarily in the grocery business, and that he is conducting a real estate business on the same premises in a part of his grocery store, although he has no particular office equipment for that purpose. The Licensee states that a card, ten inches by twelve inches, bearing the words, "William

H. Frey-Real Estate," has been on display in the window of the Licensee's grocery store since the Act of 1929 went into effect, and that his license has been on display in his place of business. 

There is no provision in the Act of 1929 which regulates, or attempts to regulate, the rate of commission which a licensed real estate broker may charge in the sale of real estate. That matter is left entirely to the voluntary agreement between the broker and the seller. It is generally recognized that the usual, ordinary, and reasonable rate of commission charged for the sale of dwellings is three per cent of the sales price. We are constrained to believe that, in the absence of an express agreement, a court of law ordinarily would permit a broker to recover this amount for negotiating his sale. An owner may, however, agree to pay more than the three per cent; or, on the

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction other hand, a broker may agree to accept less than this customary commission. This represented the law prior to the passage of the Act of 1929, and has not been changed in any way by the provisions of this Act.

II In the case at issue, the complaint is that the Licensee charged the seller a commission less than the usual rate of three per cent. No possible interpretation of Section 15, or any other provision in the Act, would indicate this to be illegal. The purpose of Section 15, as we interpret it, is to prevent a broker or salesman from sharing or splitting a commission with any person other than a licensed real estate broker. 

Under this section, a broker may not legally pay a person compensation for giving to the broker the name of a prospective buyer or for assisting him in closing a deal. The transaction for which the Licensee received a commission concerns only the Licensee and his principal, the grantor. It does not concern such a person as is contemplated by Section 15 of the Act. 

It cannot be argued that, because the Licensee accepted this sum, which was considerably less than the normal commission, this constitutes a sharing of commission with the owner which is contemplated by Section 15. The Licensee's arrangements with the owner were governed entirely by the contract of employment between these two persons. There is no question raised here, and no testimony offered, to prove that Licensee paid or agreed to pay any compensation in money or other valuable thing to any person other than the owner of the premises. The Licensee's agreement with the owner to accept $25 commission instead of

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 153 

the commission rate generally charged does not come within the provisions of Section 15 of the Act and, consequently, is no violation of the Act of 1929. This 

Department will not revoke a broker's license on that account.

III 

The Complainant's case shows that the Licensee is primarily engaged in the grocery business, and that his real estate business is conducted from his grocery store premises. Section 9(a) of the Act provides: 

Every person, co-partnership, association, or corporation, licensed as a real estate broker under the provisions of this Act, shall be required to have and maintain a definite place of business within the Commonwealth, which shall serve as his, their, or its office for the transaction of business under the authority of the said license, and where the license shall be prominently displayed. . . 

There is no provision in the Act which attempts to define what shall constitute a place of business, or how ornate or elaborate such a place of business shall be. 

The Licensee is using his grocery store for that purpose; he is holding himself out to be in the real estate business by displaying a sign to that effect in his store window; and he has complied with the law by having a license on display in the store premises. 

This Department finds, therefore, that the facts set forth in the complaint are not proven; and the Complaint is therefore dismissed. 

Bobbs vs. Hoover & Company

Failure to acquaint principal with instructions given broker by purchaser and failure to turn over hand money

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction after promise to do so held sufficient cause for suspension of license, where it appears that broker did not intend to turn over the down payment on the next day, as promised. Duties of agent to his principal.

NO. 11-1930 

The complaint of Joseph Bobb and Anna Bobb against Hoover & Company and J. L. Jordon, brokers of the city of Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, is substantially as follows: 

1. Said Jordon took away and embezzled both the down money and the contract, without any consideration being paid to complainants. 

2. Said Jordon deceived complainants as to the identity of the buyer by falsely stating the identity of the buyer. 

3. Said Jordon falsely said, as he thus fraudulently absconded and ran away from Complainants' house, that the buyer had instructed him (Jordon) to keep the down money in escrow in his (Jordon's) bank, until the final settlement. 

4. Said brokers fraudulently acted as agents for both the buyer and seller. 

5. The buyer is Complainants' own customer, which said 

Jordon falsely denied, and procured Complainants' signatures by such false statements. 

6. Said Jordon procured Complainants' signatures by exhibiting and handing to them a check for one thousand dollars down money, and after Complainants had signed, forcibly seizing check and contracts and running away with same.

The complaint filed with the Department concerns the sale of certain premises on the south side of the city of Philadelphia, owned by Anna Bobb, one of

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 155 

the Complainants herein. The Licensees were the authorized agents for the sale of the premises in question. Pursuant to such authority, Hoover & Company, through J. L. Jordon, a member of the firm, negotiated the sale of the premises in question to Mrs. 

Alice C. Bassett, and agreements of sale were signed by her for the purchase of the property at a consideration price of $20,000. Upon the signing of the agreements, on or about January 22, 1930, the purchaser gave to Jordon her check for $1000, in part payment of the consideration price, and she stated to 

Jordon at that time that she would prefer that the real estate broker's firm hold the check until the settlement was entirely completed (page 41).

The check, together with the signed agreements, was then taken by Jordon to the Bobbs, and the Bassett check for $1000 was there exhibited to the Bobbs. 

Mrs. Bobb thereupon signed the agreements, and 

Jordon took the check, together with the agreements, and departed. Mr. and Mrs. Bobb testified that Jordon explained that he was taking the check so that it might be properly endorsed, as it was made payable to Hoover & Company, and that the latter would then mail a check to the Complainants the next day. No check was mailed and, upon inquiry, the Complainants were told by Jordon that the $1000 would remain in the possession of Hoover & Company until settlement in accordance with the purchaser's instructions. II The testimony indicates that there was some discussion and controversy concerning the amount of commission to be charged the Complainants by the

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

Licensee in this transaction. Complainant contends that Jordon agreed to accept a commission of $500, which represents 21/2 per cent on the sale price of the property. Licensee, on the other hand, claims that 

Hoover & Company are entitled to $1000 for their services in the deal, which is the usual and ordinary rate of 5 per cent generally charged for the sale of unimproved property in the city of Philadelphia. 

Jordon testified that he refused to agree to a 21/2 per cent cut in the commission rate, but did agree to pay the Complainant, Bobb, $200 out of the commission which he, personally, would receive from the deal. III

In February, 1930, the parties met at the offices of the Real Estate Land Title and Trust Company for the purpose of closing the deal. The deal was not consummated at that time because the records of the Trust Company showed that certain deed descriptions had to be corrected, and that there were a number of judgments entered of record against Anna 

Bobb, the grantor. At the time of settlement, Hoover & Company produced a cashier's check from the Integrity Trust Company to the order of Hoover and

Company for the $1000 in question, but said check was not endorsed by Hoover & Company or turned over to the Trust Company; and the evidence shows that it is still in possession of Hoover & Company. 

The negotiations were then suspended pending the clearing of the title, and it appears that, up until the time of the hearing, nothing further had been done in the matter. It also appears from the evidence that 

Hoover & Company have the said $1000 on deposit

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 157 

in their general business banking account, and that it has not, to date, been placed in a special or trustee account.

The Real Estate Brokers' License Act of 1929, P. L. 1216, specifically provides the grounds upon which a license issued to a real estate broker may be revoked by the Department of Public Instruction. 

It is, therefore, important that the acts and conduct of a licensed broker set forth in the complaint be such as are contemplated and prohibited by Section 10 of the Act of 1929, in order that the Department may take cognizance of the complaint. Section 10(a) of the Act of 1929 reads as follows: 

The department may, upon its own motion, and shall, promptly upon the verified complaint in writing of any person setting forth specifically the wrongful act or acts complained of, investigate any action or business transaction of any licensed real estate broker or real estate salesman; and shall have the power temporarily to suspend or permanently to revoke licenses theretofore issued by the department, under the provisions of this act, at any time when, after due proceedings as hereinafter provided, it shall find the holder thereof to have been guilty, in the performance or attempt to perform any of the acts prohibited to others than licensed real estate brokers or real estate salesmen under the provisions of this act, 1. Of knowingly making any substantial misrepresentation; or 

2. Of knowingly making any false promise of a character likely to influence, persuade or induce; or 

3. Of a continued or flagrant course of misrepresentation, or making of false promises through agents or salesmen; or

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

4. Of any other conduct, whether of the same or of a different character than that hereinbefore specified, which shall constitute a tort, or when it shall be shown that the licensee, within five years prior to the issuance of the license then in force, has been convicted in a court of competent jurisdiction of this or any other State of forgery, embezzlement, obtaining money under false pretenses, extortion, conspiracy to defraud, or other like offense or offenses. II 

There is no evidence here that Jordon "fraudulently absconded, and ran away from the Complainants' house," or "forcibly seized a check or contract and ran away with same," as alleged in the sworn statement of complaint. It does not appear anywhere in the testimony that the Licensee used force or violence in his negotiations with the Complainants. The sole question which is raised by the complaint for determination by this Department concerns the retention of the $1000 hand money-whether the Licensee's conduct in regard to this $1000, under the conditions and circumstances brought out at the hearing, constitutes a violation of Section 10 of the Act and makes the Licensee amenable to discipline by this Department.

III 

It is generally recognized and understood that every broker owes certain fundamental duties to his principal. Those duties are prescribed by the law of agency and include loyalty to his trust, and it is upon this duty of loyalty that his other responsibilities are based. The act of 1929 prescribes additional safeguards for the public which constitutes a broker's

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 159 

clientele. Furthermore, it is well settled in this Commonwealth and elsewhere that a broker ordinarily may not represent both parties to the same transaction at the same time. His employment by one is incompatible with his employment by the other. The interests of buyer and seller are directly opposed to each other, and it is impossible for a broker to represent fairly these conflicting interests at the same time. 

He is employed by the seller, paid by him, and he is therefore duty-bound to disclose to his principal all the information he has which has a bearing on the transaction. The Licensee's endeavor to represent both buyer and seller has brought him into difficulties in this transaction. However, we are concerned with this question only if it concerns Section 10 and constitutes a violation of that section. 

IV 

The check which the purchaser, Mrs. Bassett, gave to Jordon was accompanied by certain instructionsto wit: that she preferred that the check should remain in the possession of Hoover & Company until the settlement was entirely completed. Jordon knew those instructions when he appeared at the Bobbs' house to obtain their signature to the agreements. It does not appear from his testimony or from that of the Bobbs that he acquainted Complainants with the instructions of Mrs. Bassett on that occasion. On the contrary, it appears from the evidence that he said nothing concerning those instructions. The testimony indicates that Jordon stated his reason for taking the check was because it was made payable to Hoover & Company and, therefore, had to be endorsed by the firm. 1.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

The testimony indicates that he promised to mail a check to the Bobbs for the hand money the next day. 

This he failed to do and it appears to the Department that Jordon did not tell the Complainants the purchaser's instructions regarding the hand money until the following day, after the agreements were already signed by both seller and buyer and their respective legal liabilities had already commenced. 

We are constrained to believe from the evidence produced that Jordon purposely failed to tell the Complainants the terms accompanying the check for fear that the deal would fail, and that the Licensee was prompted in the matter by personal and selfish motives to insure the payment of his commission. The purchaser is not to be condemned for giving the check on the conditions which she did; but the Licensee is to be censured for failing to acquaint the grantors with that fact when the agreements were being signed, and for his promise to mail the check the following day when it appears he had no such intention. This conduct of the Licensee, in our opinion, is such as is contemplated by Section 10, and constitutes a violation of such section.

This Department will not consider Jordon's promise to pay Mr. Bobb $200 out of the commission which he was personally to receive from the transaction for the reason that the testimony clearly indicates that no such payment has been made. Such a payment, however, is clearly prohibited by Section 15 of the Act of 1929 and, if made, would constitute sufficient grounds for the revocation of license.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 161 

VI Under the authority given to the Department by 

Section 10 (a) of the Act of 1929, License No. 5668, issued to J. L. Jordon, is hereby suspended for a term of sixty days from the filing of this opinion, and the said Jordon is hereby ordered to refrain from engaging in the real estate business directly or indirectly during this term of suspension; and License 

No. 3913, issued to Hoover & Company, is likewise suspended for a similar term of sixty days, upon similar conditions, from the filing of this opinion, unless the said Jordon severs his connection directly and indirectly with Hoover & Company as is provided for in Section 11 (d) of the act, in which event the license of Hoover & Company shall be suspended only to the date of Jordon's withdrawal from the firm of 

Hoover & Company.

April 3, 1930. 

Berstler vs. Hatt 

Misrepresentation by salesman as to rental of property, ownership of certain property involved in the transaction, and failure to use money entrusted to him for certain purposes, sufficient cause for suspension of license. NO. 14-1930 

The complaint in the case of Mrs. Philip Berstler vs. Charles H. Hatt and James A. McIntyre reads as follows:

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

Mrs. Philip Berstler, being first duly sworn, deposes and says that licensees (Charles H. Hatt and James A. McIntyre) misrepresented the fact in a real estate transaction in a trade of properties in which he said a certain property would bring in a certain rent which was $10 per month less than he stated. He also said that he would need $200 to pay for a cancellation of a contract with the Texas Oil Co. which was never paid.

The complaint concerns the sale of a certain gasoline and oil station which the Complainant owned on the Lancaster Pike, at an intersection of the Sinking Springs Road, during the latter part of 1929 and the early part of 1930.

From the testimony, it appears that James A. Mc- 

Intyre called upon the Complainant and her husband in regard to the purchase of the property, and represented and held himself out to be the purchaser. 

After some preliminary negotiations on a cash basis, agreements of sale were executed between the Complainants and James A. McIntyre for the sale of the premises in question, and Complainants agreed to accept in part payment certain premises located at 1335 Green Street as an inducing cause for the contract.

Said McIntyre represented to the Complainants that the Green Street property was rented at $35 a month. These representations concerning the amount of rent upon the Green Street property were made several times later on by McIntyre and also by Hatt, who accompanied McIntyre on several visits to the Complainants and who was also present at the settlement between the parties.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 163 

II The complaint also concerns the payment of $200 made by the Complainant and her husband to Mc- 

Intyre to be paid by him to the Texas Oil Company for the cancellation of a certain contract which Complainants had with the Texas oil concern. This Texas contract was a five-year agreement for the purchase of gasoline, expiring January 1, 1930; which contract contained a clause providing for a five-year renewal privilege. It is alleged by the Complainants that the $200 in question was paid by the Berstlers to the Texas Oil Company, through McIntyre, for the purpose of cancelling the lease, although a representative of the Texas Oil Company, Hobart C. Hughs, testified that the Oil Company had decided, prior to 

January 1, 1930, not to renew the lease for the additional five-year period.

III 

The testimony of Mr. Horace Fehr, grantor of the Green Street property, was to the effect that there was no deed or even a written contract of sale for the Green Street property between himself and McIntyre, although McIntyre testified (pages 4748) that he told the Berstlers that he owned the 

Green Street property.

After a careful review of the testimony in the case, it is the opinion of this Department that James A. 

McIntyre has been guilty of making certain misrepresentations as follows, to wit:

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

I That the property on Green Street was rented at $35 a month, when as a matter of fact it was renting for only $25 a month, and had been rented at the lower figure for one year or fourteen months preceding the settlement between himself and the Berstlers. 

It is not necessary for this Department to inquire whether or not these misrepresentations were innocently made or whether they were fraudulently made with intent to deceive. The damage and injury sustained by the Complainant is the same in either case. II Said McIntyre testified that he received the $200 in controversy from the Berstlers for the purpose of providing himself with funds for the purchase of gasoline and oil necessary for the operation of the gasoline station which he was purchasing from the Berstlers, and that the said money was not intended for the purpose of payment to the Texas Oil Company for cancellation of the lease. It does not appear that McIntyre was a bona fide purchaser of the gasoline station in question or that he ever contemplated taking over the active operation of the station, since he operated it no longer than six hours.

III 

Furthermore, the Department finds that both Hatt and McIntyre failed to exercise fidelity and good faith by neglecting or refusing to acquaint the Berstlers with the fact that McIntyre was a salesman in the employ of Hatt.

It appears from the evidence that Hatt accom-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 165 

panied McIntyre on several visits to the Berstlers and that he knew or must have known, at all times throughout the proceedings, of all the facts and representations made, and that he must have concurred in such representations and statements by remaining silent. 

Real Estate Salesman's License No. 167, issued by the Department of Public Instruction to James A. 

McIntyre, is hereby suspended for a term of four months. Real Estate Broker's License No. 173, issued to Charles H. Hatt, is hereby suspended for a term of two months. Real Estate Salesman's License No. 

698, issued to B. Frank Hettringer, is likewise suspended during the term of two months for the reason that Section 2(b) of the Real Estate Brokers' Li cense Act of 1929 states that a real estate salesman must be employed by a licensed real estate broker. 

This decision to take effect as of July 9, 1930. 

June 5, 1930. 

Menke and Harris vs. Phillips, et al.

Misrepresentations by salesmen to a purchaser concerning the identity of their principal, and receiving funds through such misrepresentations held sufficient cause for revocation of licenses. 

NO. 15-1930

Anthony Menke, a licensed real estate broker of the State of Pennsylvania, complains to the Department of Public Instruction against the transfer of real estate licenses of Frank M. Phillips and William

J. Maginnis from the employ of the Complainant to

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction the John D. Weeden Company; said complaint being 

Frank M. Phillips and William J. Maginnis formerly represented our firm-Menke Needs Realty Co., 515 Stock 

Exchange Building, Philadelphia, Pennsylvania. On March 

3, 1930, we requested cancellation of their licenses as representatives of our firm. They then opened an office at 806

Denckla Building, Philadelphia, and styled themselves Muscle Shoals Sales Office and issued receipts for property sold under the name of John D. Weeden of Florence, Alabama. When a payment of four hundred and ninety-five dollars became due to our firm March 17 from Miss Maude 

G. Harris, 1222 Locust Street, Philadelphia, this lady was asked to call at the office of Phillips. She offered him the payment of $495 due on the contract with our firm. Phillips accepted the money, she says, and told her he was transferring her purchase to some other property in Florence,

Alabama, and stated he would also credit her account with $300 paid previously on the Menke Needs Realty Company contract. Miss Harris says he discredited the property of our firm (which adjoins the United States Government property at Wilson Dam) and alarmed her by saying it would be twenty years before developments take place in the Menke

Needs properties. She says she had faith in his statements and had no idea he was transferring her to properties far away from the government properties. The statement of 

Miss Harris was made to the writer in the presence of Harvey M. Wilson, our Philadelphia manager, Miss Mae Santarlasci, our office cashier, and the friend of Miss Harris —namely, Mrs. J. P. Oellers, who had previously inspected and purchased some of our firm's property adjoining Wilson Dam, Muscle Shoals, Alabama. Mrs. Oellers expressed amazement at the high pressure tactics of Phillips, and states she subsequently told him he had no right to transfer the payments of Miss Harris to property on the other side of the Tennessee River far away from the government developments. We include the name of John D. Weeden in

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 167 

our complaint because Phillips had Miss Harris make the check payable to Weeden and he gave her a receipt bearing the printed name of John D. Weeden. 

Both Complainant and John D. Weeden Company are engaged in the business of selling real estate located in the Muscle Shoals region of the State of Alabama; and it appears from the testimony in this case that the salesmen in question, Frank M. Phillips and William J. Maginnis, on or about October 29, 1929, sold to Maude G. Harris, of the city of Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, Lots No. 312, 318, and 319 in the Woodrow Heights subdivision. Said sale was made by Phillips and Maginnis when in the employ of the Menke Needs Company. The terms were $300 down and $495 due and payable on March 17, 1930, and additional sums to be paid subsequently. 

On or about the first day of March, 1930, the said 

Phillips and Maginnis left the employ of the Complainant, and on that date, or shortly thereafter, accepted employment with John D. Weeden, or John 

D. Weeden Company. II 

It appears from the testimony that Mr. Phillips wrote the said Maude G. Harris, asking her to come to his office in the Denckla Building for the purpose of transferring lots purchased for other lots in Florence, Alabama. Miss Harris did call at the offices of 

Phillips and Maginnis, and gave a check for $495 at that time.

Although this check was made payable to the order of J. Weeden, it appears clearly from the evidence that said purchaser, Maude G. Harris, believed and

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction understood that the lots were owned by the Menke Needs Company and that the transaction which she was then having in March with Phillips and Maginnis was in their representative capacity as agents and salesmen for the Menke Needs Realty Company.

The payee's name, to whom the check was to be made payable, J. D. Weeden, was written by Phillips on the $495 statement sent by the Menke Needs Realty 

Company with the name of the Menke Needs Realty

Company appearing upon such statement. 

While it is true that the purchaser signed a contract for the purchase of the new lots from the J. D. 

Weeden Company, Miss Harris's testimony disclosed that she was under the impression that it was a transfer of lots with the Menke Needs Realty Company, and that the payment was to that company. 

It was not until the Menke Needs Company called her in regard to the $495 payment that Miss Harris discovered she was dealing wth another concern entirely. She stated in her testimony, page 7: 

A. Now, I trusted Mr. Phillips. I thought if he was going to transfer my property, it was all right. 

Q. (Mr. Semenow) You were under the impression you were paying this to the Menke Needs Realty Company? 

A. Yes.

From the evidence, it is clear to this Department that Phillips and Maginnis have taken an unfair advantage of the said purchaser, Maude G. Harris, and attempted by manipulation in the transfer of the lots to make a new commission in their dealings with her. 

We are clearly of the opinion that these two sales-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 169 

men have not acted in that good faith which their employment and duties to the purchaser demanded. 

They have sought to use this change of employment as a means of promoting their own ends and to further their personal gain by inducing the purchaser to sign agreements for the purchase of entirely new lots with a new company. We are of the opinion that the conduct of Phillips and Maginnis in this transaction is such as is contemplated by the Real Estate Brokers' License Act of 1929 and is prohibited by its provisions. 

Transfer of salesman's license No. 996, issued to 

Frank M. Phillips, and transfer of salesman's license 

No. 995, issued to William J. Maginnis, from the

Menke Needs Realty Company to the John D. 

Weeden Company is hereby refused. Such licenses are hereby suspended for the term of one year, and said Phillips and Maginnis are hereby notified to refrain from engaging in the real estate business in the State of Pennsylvania, directly or indirectly, on and after June 20, 1930. 

May 17, 1930. 

Morch vs. Wendler

Misrepresentations by Licensee to his principal through which he receives money, held a violation of Section 10 of the Act of 1929. 

NO. 17-1930

The complaint in question concerns certain valuable property at the southeast corner of Broad and

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

Oxford Streets, Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, owned by the Complainant, Thomas Morch, which property the Applicant, Paul Wendler, Jr., was endeavoring to sell on behalf of the Complainant during the summer of 1928. 

The property in question was listed with the Applicant for sale, and Applicant notified the Complainant that he had a prospective purchaser for the property in the person of Mrs. Emma Bergdoll, whom he said he represented and from whom he held a power of attorney, and whose secretary he was or had been. 

It appears from the evidence that, after Applicant informed Complainant of the interest of the said 

Mrs. Bergdoll in Complainant's property, the Applicant obtained a loan from the Complainant in the sum of $275, and that in November, 1928, he obtained a further loan from the Complainant of $320 because of certain statements and representations made by the Applicant to the Complainant to the effect that Mrs. Bergdoll had seen the property and liked it, but that she thought $325,000 was too much and would give $300,000 for it upon terms which the 

Applicant had submitted.

II Complainant testified that it was upon the strength of these statements and to assist Applicant in furthering the negotiations with Mrs. Bergdoll that the two loans in question were made by him to the Applicant. 

It appears that Wendler was in severe financial straits, and that the loans were made by the Complainant so that the Applicant could be of free mind in pursuing the negotiations with Mrs. Bergdoll in

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 171 

the interests of his principal. Complainant further states in his testimony that Applicant subsequently informed him again of the interest of Mrs. Bergdoll in the property, and that she would go through with the transaction as soon as she received certain monies which she was expecting.

The Complainant, later on, called upon Mrs. Bergdoll for the purpose of facilitating the transaction; and Complainant testified she informed him, at that time, that she never told Mr. Wendler she was interested in the property, and didn't want to have anything to do with him.

This Department has already ruled in other cases that it will not permit any person to use the Act of 1929 or the agency of this Department as a means of collecting money. However, the Department is interested in, and will take cognizance of, representations and conduct of a broker upon which he receives money, which constitutes a violation of Section 10 (a) of the Real Estate Brokers' License Act of 1929.

The Applicant here has failed to produce a single witness or any supporting data to corroborate his allegation that Mrs. Bergdoll was a bona fide prospective purchaser of the property in question. II

This Department is of the opinion that the statements made to the Complainant by the Applicant

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction were for the purpose of procuring certain loans, were untrue, and were such misrepresentations as are contemplated by, and constitute a violation of, Section 

10(a) of the Act of 1929. 

Application of Paul Wendler, Jr., for a real estate broker's license is hereby refused, with leave to reapply for a license on or after January 1, 1931. 

May 22, 1930.

Greiner vs. Reilly Incorporated 

Failure to keep promises made to prospective purchaser which induced the contract of purchase, sufficient cause for suspension of license.

NO. 25-1930 

 Complaint of Ernest H. Greiner vs. Reilly Incor porated reads as follows:

On the sixteenth day of November, 1929, a man representing Reilly Incorporated, a real estate firm at 716 Pennsylvania Building, 15th and Chestnut Streets, Philadelphia, stopped in to see me to sell me a store and fixtures for seven hundred and fifty dollars ($750). I agreed to take the same, but before we could make any agreement he offered me the property 4058 North 5th Street, in which the store was located, for the sum of twelve thousand dollars ($12,000). He stated that I would only need eight hundred dollars ($800) to buy the property. He did on the same day make an agreement of sale, of which you will find a copy inclosed, agreeing to finance the property to eleven thousand, two hundred dollars ($11,200), free of cost to me. I agreed to buy this property under the above-mentioned conditions, and gave Reilly Incorporated the sum of five hun-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 173 

dred dollars ($500) on account of the purchase. On February 8, 1930, or eight days before the settlement, I received a letter from Reilly Incorporated stating that "we have secured first and second mortgages aggregating eighty-five hundred dollars ($8500) on property 4058 North 5th

Street, which is the largest amount possible for us to obtain." I immediately got in touch with Reilly Incorporated, and stated that I could not go through with the settlement if that was the highest mortgage I could get. As he took my five hundred dollars ($500) agreeing to place eleven thousand, two hundred dollars ($11,200) of mortgages for me, I then got in touch with Reilly Incorporated and J. W.

Borowski, owner, and demanded settlement as per agreement, and J. W. Borowski told me that he did not agree to place any mortgages on this property and that he signed the agreement on the advice of Reilly Incorporated, who was the agent for J. W. Borowski, the owner. I then went to 

George Keehfuss, real estate agent, and explained the situation. He immediately got in touch with Borowski and demanded settlement, offering the three hundred dollars ($300) balance, and Borowski would not take or listen to the same. On or about March 28, 1930, I went down to see 

Reilly and demanded my five hundred dollars ($500) back, and Reilly told me that he had nothing more to do with the settlement or the sale. I then had George Keehfuss, my agent, write Mr. Reilly a letter, and the advice given to Mr. 

Keehfuss, my agent, was that he, Reilly, did not feel as if he should return my money, as the five hundred dollars ($500) was his commission for selling the property. I feel that I have been unjustly attacked in this matter, as Reilly 

Incorporated have not delivered to me what they sold me, and only on their advice that they would get me the eleven thousand, two hundred dollars ($11,200) in mortgages I agreed to buy the property. I therefore make this plea to you and your committee for an investigation in this matter, so that I may have my five hundred dollars ($500) returned to me or the property. Trusting that you will take this matter under consideration and advise me at your earliest convenience • • • 717

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

I The controversy between the parties involves the purchase of a cigar store and premises at 4058 North 

5th Street, Philadelphia, from one Borowski, who was represented by Reilly Incorporated. It appears from the evidence that the original negotiations conducted between the parties concerned the purchase of the cigar store only, at a price of $750. Shortly thereafter, the Licensee started negotiations for the sale of the premises in question at a consideration price of $12,000, upon terms of $800 in cash, the balance to be taken care of by mortgages. This is not denied by the licensee.

The Complainant thereupon gave to the Licensee the sum of $500 as part payment. This money the 

Licensee admits he retained as commission for negotiating the deal. II Letters were submitted by the Complainant to the effect that the Licensee could procure first and second mortgages only in the amount of $8500, and it is the contention of the Licensee that the balance of $2700 was to be taken by the seller, Borowski, as a third mortgage. This is denied by the Complainant.

In the evidence produced at the hearing on this complaint, the Licensee failed to produce the seller, Borowski, to substantiate his statement that a third mortgage for the difference between $8500 and the amount agreed upon was to be taken back by the seller.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 175  

This case raises two questions of misrepresentation for consideration by this Department: 

1. Whether the Licensee failed to procure mortgages in the amount of $11,200, as agreed upon by the terms of the written agreement; and 

2. The status and effect of a certain second agreement, in which the consideration price for the premises in question was specified at $15,000 instead of $12,000, the real consideration.

It is the opinion of this Department, from the evidence produced by both sides on this question, that the Licensee was unable to procure mortgages in the amount of $11,200, as he had agreed to do, and as he had represented to the Complainant as an inducing cause for his entering the contract.

From the testimony, it appears that the Licensee executed the second agreement for $15,000 purely for the purpose of obtaining larger mortgages. A clearer or more striking example of misrepresentation or unscrupulous dealing can scarcely be imagined. It appears from the evidence that the Licensee was willing deliberately to perpetrate a fraud upon a would-be mortgagee by this action. II It appears from the evidence that the only person in the transaction who profited was the Licensee, and his gain was to the extent of $500. The Licensee here failed to exercise those principles of loyalty and fair

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction dealing which his employment demanded. Licensee failed and neglected to take any steps or make any effort to consummate the deal. It appears that he was satisfied to retain the $500 as his commission and let the parties look out for themselves.

III 

It is the opinion of this Department that Licensee has committed such acts of fraud and misrepresentation in this transaction as are included in Section 

10(a) of the Real Estate Brokers' License Act of 1929, P. L. 1216, and which constitute a material violation thereof. Wherefore it is the opinion of this 

Department that the following licenses issued to 

Reilly Incorporated on February 25, 1930, be suspended for a period of four months, which suspension is to take effect on July 17, 1930:

Real Estate Brokers' License No. 5945-Reilly Incorporated (B. J. Reilly). 

Real Estate Broker's License No. 5947-A. Joseph Kormann.

Real Estate Broker's License No. 5946-John A. 

Mitchell.

Real Estate Salesmen's License No. 1308Charles Christiansen. 

June 13, 1930. 

Better Business Bureau vs. Bridgeway Realty Company 

Where a "free lot" is given away and the recipient of such "free lot" is required to pay a sum of money to cover conveyancing charges, and such sum is in excess of the actual cost of conveyancing, held to be a violation of Section 10 of the Act of 1929.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 177 

NO. 31-1930 

The complaint of George W. Connor, representing the Better Business Bureau of Philadelphia, against the Bridgeway Realty Company and Ira Copeland, alias Ira Cope, et al., reads as follows:

George W. Connor, being first duly sworn, deposes and says that he is an employee of the Better Business Bureau of Philadelphia. On or about April 14 or 15, 1930, his brother-in-law, George F. Fennel, received a telephone call from a man purporting to represent the Bridgeway Realty Company, and stating that he had been selected to receive one of their free advertising lots. George S. Fennel not being at home to accept the telephone call, and said George 

W. Connor knowing that the Better Business Bureau was interested in this type of activity on the part of the Bridgeway Realty Company, called said company on the telephone posing as Mr. Fennel, stating that he was desirous of seeing the lot and was advised by the Bridgeway Realty Company that they would communicate with him at a later date, at which time they wanted him to bring his wife to the subdivision in Moorestown, N. J., for the purpose of examining the lot. Due to an unavoidable occurrence, said George W. 

Connor was not able to keep the appointment, but prior to 

May 17, 1930, made an appointment with the company to view the property with his alleged wife. On May 17th, 1930, said George W. Connor went to the officer of the 

Bridgeway Realty Company with Miss Elizabeth Mahoney, also an employee of the Better Business Bureau, posing as 

Mr. and Mrs. George Fennel, 553 Mayland Street, Philadelphia, where they were interviewed by one Green, who informed them that they had been selected as the recipients of one of their advertising lots, and that the lot would be pre-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction sented to them free, but the cost of transferring the deed would be $50, which they would have to pay, and that the lot was $450. Said Green stated that this method of advertising was cheaper than using newspapers and that, after title to the property had been acquired, the company would canvass the neighborhood and sell lots to other people by using the names of the recipients, offering to said George W. 

Connor and Elizabeth Mahoney a commission of 2½ per cent of the sale prices of any lots so sold, and when this commission amounted to a total of $450, the company would present them with another lot. 

Said George W. Connor and Elizabeth Mahoney were then introduced to one Cope, who conducted them to the property. He showed them a certain piece of ground which was purported to be 20 ft. x 100 ft., which was apparently unimproved farm land staked out and bearing street signs. 

Said Cope made every effort to secure the signature of said 

George W. Connor and Elizabeth Mahoney on a contract of purchase and to secure a deposit, first setting the amount at $25, and successively reducing this to $2.00. 

Said Cope further stated that at any time said George W. 

Connor decided to build in the development, he would be allowed the full purchase price of $450 on the advertising lot to be applied toward the purchase of any other site in the development. 

At the hearing on said complaint held in Philadelphia on June 19, counsel for Licensees raised objections against the form of the complaint, for the reason that the defendants named in the complaint were Mr. Green and Mr. Cope, and that the officers of the corporation were not named therein; and, consequently, no action could be taken against them. Counsel for Complainant thereupon asked permission to amend his statement of complaint. This motion was granted, and the hearing was continued to July 16, 1930. The amended statement of complaint reads as follows:

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 179 

George W. Connor, being first duly sworn, deposes and says that this affidavit amends and supplements his statement of complaint of June 5, 1930, and is relative to the same transaction, which is complained of in the former affidavit.

Said George W. Connor states that to the best of his knowledge and belief, the officers of the Bridgeway Realty 

Company mentioned in his affidavit of June 5, 1930, are: 

President, D. Merrill Rosman; Vice President, Edward Copeland; Secretary-Treasurer, F. J. Frantz; General Manager,

Ira Copeland, alias Ira Cope; and further says that the acts complained of in his affidavit of complaint of June 5, 1930, were committed by Ira Copeland, alias Ira Cope, and one Green in their capacity as agents and/or salesmen for the Bridgeway Realty Company.

II The complaint in the main concerns certain free lot operations of the Licensees in the city of Phila delphia. The Better Business Bureau of Philadelphia produced several witnesses, all of whom testified substantially to the same effect-to wit, that the witness was called on the telephone by a representative of the Bridgeway Realty Company and told that he was the winner of a free lot in the Company's subdivision plan in Moorestown, New Jersey. The witness was then requested to call at the Philadelphia offices of the Bridgeway Realty Company. The representative of the Company informed the prospect that it was the Company's plan to single out a representative person in a certain district in the city, give that person a lot, then canvass the neighborhood, and, on the strength of the lot given to the person selected in the neighborhood, sell lots to other persons. It was also represented to the prospect that, if other sales were

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction made in the neighborhood, a commission of 21/2 per cent would be credited to the recipient of the free lot until such fund reached $450—the value of the free lot. Then a new and more valuable lot would be offered the holder of the free lot, and the free lot accepted in part payment at a valuation of $450. In one case a witness (Basedow) testified that Cope notified him that a commission of 21/2 per cent on the sale of a lot for $1090 would be credited to his account or would be paid to him in cash should he subsequently desire it. 

George W. Connor and Elizabeth Mahoney, employees of the Better Business Bureau; Gertrude 

Saybolt, a housewife; and Wilhelm Basedow, a draftsman employed by the General Electric Company, gave similar testimony.

III 

The evidence disclosed that, in every case where a free lot was awarded, the prospect was asked to pay $50 to cover the cost of conveyancing. Mr. J. Carr 

Jessupp, an experienced real estate dealer and a past president of the Camden Real Estate Board, testified that the conveyance charges in Burlington County, New Jersey, in a transaction similar to the one between the parties in this action, would be $3 and possibly $10 more for preparing the deed, although the deed is usually supplied by the grantor, and no charge made. This witness testified further that a lot 20 ft. x 100 ft., which are the dimensions of the free lot, was worth considerably less than $450, which the Company represented its value to be. Mr. 

Jessupp also stated that taxes of $2.50 (amount of

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 181 

tax on Basedow's lot) would be the rate charged on farm or unimproved property.

IV 

It appears from the evidence that certain representations were made by the officers and representatives of the Bridgeway Realty Company to the various prospects, to the effect that most of the lots in the plan were sold; that the building lots were valued at from $900 to $1500; that gas, water, light connections, and street improvements were to be made; and that a large bus terminal was to be erected there. It appears further from the testimony that the free lot size was 20 ft. x 100 ft., and that the agreement of sale for the lot contained a provision prohibiting the grantee from erecting a dwelling house on a lot less than 40 ft. x 100 ft. in size.

At the hearing on the complaint, counsel for Licensees offered voluntarily to return the licenses in question to the Department for cancellation, so that the hearing would not take place and the complaint be dismissed. The Department at that time refused counsel's offer and heard the case. In our opinion, there is a marked difference between voluntarily surrendering a license and having such license revoked or suspended by the Department for cause. We were of the opinion then, and we are of the same mind now, that if a Licensee has been guilty of a violation of the Act of 1929, such violation merits discipline by

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction this Department. To permit the Licensee to return the licenses without giving the Complainant an opportunity to be heard would, in our opinion, be contrary to public policy and to the purposes and provisions of the Act.

II 

Section 10 (a) of the Real Estate Brokers' License 

Act of 1929 specifies and defines the grounds upon which this Department may suspend or revoke a real estate broker's license.

The sole question for determination raised by the complaint is whether the Licensees' conduct and representations made in connection with the free lot distribution constitutes a violation of Section 10 and makes the Licensees amenable to discipline by this Department. The representations made in this case were that the prospect would obtain a lot free, and that $50 had to be paid to cover the cost of conveyancing. This we find to be untrue, because the conveyancing charge was only $3 for recording, or at most $13, which includes the cost for preparing the deed. This constitutes such material misrepresentation as is contemplated by the Act and is sufficient grounds for suspension or revocation of a license.

III 

The Licensees have selected winners of free lots on a large scale, playing upon the vanity of gullible prospects by telling them how important they are and what standing they have in the community in which they live. Such questionable methods of doing business are looked upon with disfavor by legitimate

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 183 

brokers, and justly so. In all cases the $50 is alleged to be a charge for conveyancing only. In the light of the tax rate per lot and the agreement of sale offered the prospect, which contains a clause prohibiting the erection of a dwelling house upon lots less than 40 ft. x 100 ft. in size, and on considering that the dimensions of the free lots are 20 ft. x 100 ft., it may well be argued that the $50 is more properly a purchase price than a conveyancing charge. We are firmly convinced that it is more the former. IV 

Section 15 (a) of the Act of 1929 reads as follows: 

It shall be unlawful for any licensed real estate broker, or real estate salesman, to pay any compensation, in money or other valuable thing, to any person other than a licensed real estate broker or real estate salesman, for the rendering of any service, or the doing of any of the acts by this Act forbidden to be rendered or performed by other than licensees. 

We find further that the Licensees have violated this section by informing Basedow, a free lot recipient, that he (Basedow) was to receive 21/2 per cent commission on the sale of a $1090 lot which was made in his neighborhood. It therefore appears that the 

Bridgeway Realty Company was willing to pay commissions to any persons in order that the sale of lots in their Moorestown plan might be stimulated, although this practice is a clear violation of the Act. It is not denied that the officers of the Company were familiar with the representations made by their

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction agents and salesmen. Many of the negotiations were conducted in the offices of the Bridgeway Realty 

Company in Philadelphia; and Ira Copeland, also known as Ira Cope, the general manager, was present, took an active part, and receipted for monies paid to the Company on account of the alleged conveyance charges.

The purpose of the license law, primarily, is to protect the public in its dealings with real estate brokers. 

This Department would indeed be remiss in its administration and enforcement of the Act were it to permit the Licensees or any other real estate brokers to continue such a flagrant course of misrepresentation as is evidenced by the free lot method. It is a misnomer and grossly misleading. Real estate broker's license No. 2014 issued to Edward Copeland, real estate broker's license No. 2015 issued to Samuel J.

Frantz, and real estate salesman's license No. 1797 issued to Ira Cope are hereby revoked; said revocations to be effective as of September 15, 1930. 

August 11, 1930. 

Krentzman et al. vs. Keystone Developing Co., Inc.

Fake representations made in advertising circulars concerning lots, sufficient cause for discipline by the Department. 

NOS. 52, 54, 62, 65-1930

The complaint of Harry Krentzman, Harrisburg, against the Keystone Developing Company, Incorporated, is as follows:

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 185 

I 

On Labor Day, September 1, 1930, a representative, Joseph H. Cohen, of the Keystone Developing Company called at my home, 1509 North 6th Street, Harrisburg, Pennsylvania, and informed me that my son, Nathan Krentzman, was the winner of a lot in New South Wales Development, and that the lot would be given to him, my son, absolutely free of cost, but that it would be necessary to pay $59 to cover the costs of conveyancing, but it was to be distinctly understood that the lot was free and that fee was not in payment of the lot. I then told him that I did not have $59, and he informed me that he would accept a partial payment of $5 on account. I paid him the $5 and received the receipt from him. He then informed me that a bus would call for my son, my wife and I on Sunday morning, September 7, and would transport us to and from the lot in question free of charge. On September 7, a private fivepassenger automobile, which at the time contained six people, called at my home and the representative (MacHale) asked for an additional $10 before he would take us along. I refused to make the trip and have made demand for the return of my $5 deposit, which I have not yet received.

In August, 1930, a traffic puzzle was submitted by the Complainant to the licensee. Complainant was then told by a representative of the licensee that he (the Complainant) had solved the puzzle correctly and was the winner of a free lot, but he would have to pay $59 conveyancing charges.

On October 8, 1930, Complainant called upon Sarah

It was further represented that the lot in question, located in the North Wales Development, was nine miles from the center of the city of Philadelphia, which Complainant alleges and believes to be untrue. 

The complaint of Mary A. Fritz, Philadelphia, against the Keystone Developing Company, Incorporated, is as follows:

On October 3, 1930, an automobile puzzle was left on our porch. My daughter, Mrs. F. D. Teickman, solved the puzzle, sent it to the Keystone Developing Company, In-

corporated, on October 4. Mr. Miller came to our home and informed me that Mrs. Teickman won a lot, and that we should go to North Wales Park and look it over. We did, and there we met Mr. Davis, a licensed real estate dealer, and Mr. Miller. They showed us the lot, which was 

20 ft. by 100 ft., and was to be free. But here Mr. Davis informed us we would have to pay the transfer charges, which were $59. II In view of the fact that the grievances set forth in the first three complaints are substantially similar and that hearings on the complaints were held at the same time, we shall dispose of all three complaints in this opinion. The grievances set forth in the Fritz complaint are analogous to the others and for that reason this opinion will likewise dispose of that complaint.

The testimony discloses that the Keystone Developing Company, Incorporated, has been engaged in marketing a subdivision at North Wales, Pennsylvania, and in the course of its operations has used the following method of approach and sale. A puzzle consisting of six cars in a traffic tie-up has been distributed on a wholesale scale in several cities and towns in the eastern part of the state. If the puzzle is solved correctly so as to relieve the traffic conges-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

#11 tion, the person solving the puzzle is then notified by a representative of the Company that he is the winner of a $500 free lot. However, there is a proviso or condition attached, to the effect that the sum of $59 must be paid to the Company to cover certain charges. 

In connection with the representations made by the representatives of the Company concerning the free lot, we refer to excerpts taken from the testimony at the hearing on the complaints. 

Referring to page 9 of the testimony: 

MR. SEMENOW: Mr. Ward, please tell us in your own words briefly just what dealings or negotiations you have had with the Keystone Developing Company.

MR. SEMENOW: Was there anything said about the $59 charge on this lot that was awarded?

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction the advertising expenses connected with it, meaning the conveyancing, drawing of the deed—all those charges—were all included in addition to the taxes for the current year-that would cost her $59.

Ordinarily the seller furnishes the deed to the property he is selling; there is no cost as far as the purchaser is concerned for the deed, no charge for notary fee because the notary fee is paid by the seller who makes the acknowledgment; the only charge is recording fee, and that is about $3; isn't that right?

A. Well, that may be; it depends upon the amount involved.

A. That portion of it.

You made the extravagant statement that they totaled $40

to $60. Isn't it true that they never total more than $10 as far as purchasing is concerned? 

A. I do not know.

A supplementary hearing on the Callaghan complaint was held on December 3, 1930, for the purpose of ascertaining the truth or falsity of certain statements and representations set forth in the advertising matter distributed by the Keystone Developing Company, Incorporated, that "North Wales

Park is easily accessible to the business and shopping districts of Philadelphia, a great metropolis with a population close to 3,000,000. The Philadelphia & 

Reading Railway Company recently appropriated $20,000,000 to electrify its lines as far as Lansdale, Pennsylvania, which will assure even quicker and better service to Philadelphia." The testimony at this hearing indicated that the 1930 population for the city of Philadelphia is 1,964,430, and that the Reading Railroad has appropriated at various times a total sum of approximately $20,000,000 for the electrification of its lines from Philadelphia to Trenton, which is some considerable distance north of Lansdale, and that approximately $6,500,000 covers the line from Philadelphia to Lansdale.

In the case of Miss Callaghan, it appears further that Sarah Poynton, a representative of the Keystone Company, represented the North Wales Development to be located nine miles from Philadelphia, and it appears from the evidence that the subdivision development is more than twenty miles distant from that city. The evidence further reveals that the Licensee by its agents and representatives agreed

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction to pay Mr. Ward the sum of $25 for each lot that the Company sold in Carlisle, Pennsylvania, and that similar representations and promises were made to Mr. Krentzman for the sale of any lot to a resident of 

6th Street, Harrisburg, Pennsylvania.

There is some conflict in the testimony as to whether a recipient of a free lot could build upon such a lot, or whether it would be necessary to purchase an adjoining lot, the size of the free lot being 20 ft. x 100 ft. 

It appears that the Licensee has attempted to exploit the vanity and culpability of gullible prospects through the medium of a very simple puzzle device. 

While such questionable methods are not regarded as consonant with sound business practices and ethics and are looked upon with disfavor by legitimate brokers, this Department is concerned only with these sales methods employed where they constitute a violation of the Real Estate Brokers' License Act of 

May 1, 1929, P. L. 1216, and in what particular respect there is such a violation: that is the sole question raised by the complaints for determination by this Department.

II Section 10 (a) of the Act specifies and provides the grounds upon which a real estate broker's license may be suspended or revoked by the Department of Public Instruction, which agency of the Commonwealth is charged with the administration and enforcement of the Act.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 193 

III This Department finds, as a matter of fact, that no lots were given away free as was represented by the Licensee and its representatives. Instead, a payment of $59 was demanded in each instance as a condition precedent to the transfer of title. No free lot could be obtained by any recipient unless payment of the $59 was first made. The traffic puzzle method of operation is but a variation of the free lot method of doing business.

The representations made in the cases at issue here are not essentially different from those made in the case of George W. Connor vs. Bridgeway Realty 

Company in No. 31-1930. We ruled in the latter case that the action of the Licensee constituted a flagrant violation of the Act of 1929, and we disciplined the brokers accordingly. We stated in that opinion, and we reiterate here, that the term "free lot" is a misnomer and grossly misleading. 

In the complaint of Pauline W. Callaghan against the Keystone Developing Company and Sarah Poynton, we find the representation made that the North Wales Development is located nine miles from Philadel to be false and to be such a material misrepresentation as is contemplated by Section 10 of the Act, and we consider that it constitutes a violation of said Section for which Licensees may be disciplined.

IV The Licensees through their agents and representatives have agreed to pay commission or compensation to persons solving the puzzle where sales of lots

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction are made to other persons in the prize-winner's neighborhood and vicinity. While there is no evidence present of any payments actually made, the testimony is clear as to the Licensee's intent to violate Section 

15(a) of the Act which reads as follows:

It shall be unlawful for any licensed real estate broker, or real estate salesman, to pay any compensation, in money or other valuable thing, to any person other than a licensed real estate broker or real estate salesman, for the rendering of any service, or the doing of any of the acts by this act forbidden to be rendered or performed by other than licensees. 

This Department is of the further opinion that the officers of the Company were familiar with the representations made by their agents and salesmen. Many of the negotiations were conducted personally by the officers of the Keystone Developing Company, Incorporated, and they knew, or should have known, of the representations made by their representatives; and consequently the Department may hold said Company and its officers responsible under subsection 3 

of Section 10 (a) of the Act. 

The Department finds that the representations made in the advertising circulars concerning the population of the city of Philadelphia and the amount of money to be expended by the Reading Railway Company for electrification and improvement of its lines from Philadelphia to Lansdale, Pennsylvania, to be false, and to be such material misrepresentations as to constitute a violation of Section 10 of the Act, previously cited.

This Department is of the opinion that, for reasons previously stated, the Licensees have been guilty of

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 195 

violating the Act of 1929, and we would indeed be lax in our enforcement of the Act and negligent in our duties to the public were we to permit such violation to pass undisciplined.
