Dixon vs. J. A. Young & Co. 

False statements made by broker regarding the existence of a second broker in order to obtain a commission in excess of the agreed-upon commission, is such a violation of the Act of 1929 as is contemplated by Section 10. The return of such excess commission prior to hearing-immaterial. NO. 64-1930 

The complaint of Russell A. Dixon, Pittsburgh, against J. A. Young and Company, Incorporated, and J. C. Young, Pittsburgh, is as follows:

On or about September 1, 1930, I gave J. A. Young and 

Company my property at 6045 St. Marie Street, Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, to sell for $6,750 with 3 per cent commission. About September 5, J. C. Young claimed another realtor had customer for my property, but that deal could be made only by paying each his full commission or $400. I refused, and suggested that if each would cut his share $50, I would pay $300 to be split between them. Upon closing of deal on October 2, Guaranty Title Company paid J. C. Young $190.20 (in addition to hand money of $100 which he already had). I asked name of other realtor, insting that I was liable for not more than 3 per cent unless a second realtor be named. J. C. Young refused to disclose his name. Believing the "other realtor" to be fictitious, I

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 197 

asked purchaser, Mrs. Stewart, who said she dealt directly with Young. I therefore contend that Young has falsely obtained at least $87.70 more than the agreed 3 per cent commission.

II It appears from the evidence that the Complainant listed certain property that he owned at 6045 St. 

Marie Street, Pittsburgh, with the Licensee for sale on or about September 1, 1930. He signed an exclusive listing contract at that time for a three-month period, and agreed to pay a commission of three per cent on the consideration price if a sale were effected. 

About one week later, J. C. Young, of the Licensee firm, called the Complainant and informed him that a prospective purchaser had been obtained for the Complainant's property through a second broker, and that it would be necessary for the Complainant to pay the second broker a full commission of three per cent. After some controversy, the Complainant agreed to pay total commissions of $300, although the Licensee refused to divulge the identity of the second broker. Shortly afterward, agreements of sale were signed by the purchaser, one Stewart, and by Dixon, the Complainant. The parties met on October 2, 1930, at the offices of the Guaranty Title Company for the purpose of consummating the transaction. The Complainant again demanded to know the identity of the second broker, but the Licensee refused to comply with his request. The Complainant then paid the $300 commission under protest. This amount was $87.70 more than the three per cent agreed to be paid by the Complainant at the time he listed the property for sale with the Licensee. Mr. J. C. Young admitted

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction in his testimony that there was no second broker involved in the transaction and that the name of the second broker which he gave to the Director of the 

Real Estate Brokers' License Law prior to the hearing was false. After the complaint was filed with the 

Department, but before the hearing was held, the Licensee returned the $87.70 excess commission to the 

Complainant. 

The sole question raised for determination by this Department is whether the action of J. C. Young, a licensed real estate broker, constitutes a violation of the Real Estate Brokers' License Act of 1929,

P. L. 1216, and particularly Section 10 (a) of the Act.

Counsel for Licensee submitted a very able brief in which he contends that the conduct of the Licensee is not embraced within the terms of Section 10, inasmuch as no material misrepresentation was made by the Licensee in his negotiation of the sale of the Complainant's property to Stewart. It is contended further that the controversy between the parties was entirely one of commission, concerning which the Department has no jurisdiction and can take no cognizance. II

It is true that there is no provision in the Act of 1929 regulating or attempting to regulate the rate of commission to be charged in connection with sale of real estate. It is purely one of voluntary agreement

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 199 

between the parties. The contract executed by the 

Complainant stipulated a three per cent commission to be paid, but J. C. Young, through misrepresentations made to the Complainant, induced him to agree to pay more than the originally agreed-upon amount. 

We believe that the purpose and intent of the Act of 1929 is to regulate and control the activities of a real estate broker towards his principal. Such a relationship exists here between the Complainant and the Licensee. There are certain fundamental duties which every agent owes to his principal, the most elementary of which, and the one upon which the others are based, is loyalty to his trust. The Licensee has in this case seriously violated this fundamental duty and attempted, through material misrepresentations, to make an additional profit at the expense of his principal. We are of the opinion that such action is contemplated by Section 10 (a) of the Act and makes a broker amenable to discipline by the Department for violation thereof.

III Evidence was produced to show that restitution has been made, and it is urged that therefore the complaint against the Licensee should be dismissed. We are not of this opinion. To permit a real estate broker to practice misrepresentation, and then avoid the responsibilities of this Act by making restitution whenever a Complainant aggressively asserts his rights. in the matter, would be to defeat the intent and purpose of the Act, and we would indeed be remiss in our responsibilities to the public, for whose benefit the Act was passed, were we to permit such a violation to pass undisciplined. Accordingly, this Depart-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction ment holds that J. C. Young has been guilty of violating the Act of 1929 and that Real Estate Broker's 

License No. 2398 issued to J. A. Young & Co., Inc., of which J. C. Young is president; Real Estate 

Broker's License No. 2399 issued to J. A. Young; and Real Estate Broker's License No. 2400 issued to

Walter D. Young, are hereby suspended for a term of four months from December 25, 1930, unless an appeal is taken from this decision, in which event the term of suspension shall begin as of the date of the final determination of such appealed action. It is further provided that should J. C. Young sever his relationship and connection with J. A. Young & Co.,

Inc., directly or indirectly during the term of this suspension, then and in that case Real Estate Broker's 

License No. 2399 issued to J. A. Young and Real 

Estate Broker's License No. 2400 issued to Walter 

D. Young shall not be suspended. 

November 20, 1930. (Appeal now pending before Pennsylvania 

Superior Court)

Banks vs. Homestead Development Company, Inc. 

Promises and representations made to a purchaser concerning the construction of dwelling, obtaining money and failing to use it for such purpose, held sufficient cause for suspension of license.

NO. 68-1930 

The complaint of Oscar Banks, Camden, New Jersey, against the Homestead Development Company, Incorporated (Elias A, Kalcheim), Philadelphia, is as follows:

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 201 

On March 27, 1930, I purchased from the Homestead 

Development Company, 143 North 13th Street, Philadelphia, two lots at their development in Atco Park Estates, Camden County, New Jersey, for a total price of $319. 

I paid $28 down, and agreed to pay $10 monthly thereafter until total amount was paid in full. Mr. H. B. Evans, who represented the Homestead Development Company, handled this transaction. On May 1, 1930, Mr. E. A. Kalcheim of the Homestead Development Company interviewed me at my home, and informed me that for $995 he would build me a bungalow on my lots. He asked me to pay $300 down and $15 monthly thereafter until payment was made in full. 

This was to be in addition to the $10 monthly paid for the lots. He promised to have the house built on or before 

July 15. I signed an agreement with him, in which the 

Homestead Development Company was listed as "Agents for Louis Kanning, Builder." I gave him the $300, receipt for which I hold. No work was ever started on the house and on July 14, when I was to make one of my regular payments on the lots, I asked for my money back, and Mr. Kalcheim refused to give it to me. He stated that my agreement was with Louis Kanning, the builder, and that Mr. Kanning had done nothing about building the house and that he (Kalcheim) could not make him do anything. He said the only thing he could do to help me out would be to apply the $300 cash which I had given him against the money I owed on the lots. Inasmuch as I had paid $10 in May, $10 in June, and $10 in July, in addition to the $28 cash down, my total payments on the lots amounted to $58, leaving a balance to be paid thereon of only $261. Because Mr. Kalcheim was attempting to use my $300 to pay a $261 balance, I refused to accept his suggestion. I also discontinued making my monthly payments on the lots. Mr. Kalcheim has consistently refused to give me my $300, and is now threatening to sue me to get the balance due on the lots. Since I have brought this matter to public attention, I am informed by those who know the true values of real estate that the price

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction asked for the lots at Atco Park Estates is absolutely exorbitant. Mr. Kalcheim also tricked a Mr. Bynum of 2412 

Mole Street, Philadelphia, out of $300 just as he tricked me. II The complaint concerns a certain transaction which the Complainant had with the Licensee for the purchase of lots and erection of a house thereon in a development owned by the Licensee at Atco, New Jersey, near Camden. We find that the Complainant paid $3 under protest on his initial trip of inspection to the development, and that he later agreed to purchase three lots and paid $25 on account, receiving credit for the $3 previously paid. These payments were made to one Kalcheim, president of the Homestead Development Company, Incorporated, and

Complainant's subsequent negotiations were conducted with the same person. We find that Kalcheim subsequently negotiated with banks for the construction of a dwelling upon the lots purchased, and he represented to the Complainant that the cost would be $995 and that the house would be completed by July 15, 1930. The building contract was written by the Licensee and was executed by the Complainant and Licensee as agent for one Kanning, a builder. 

The Complainant never saw nor had any personal dealings with Kanning. We find that the Complainant relied upon these representations and paid $300 to Kalcheim on account on May 1, and made some additional payments as well. We also find that no building was erected or construction started by July 15, and that Kalcheim refused to refund the $300 payment, but agreed to apply that amount to the payment on the purchase of two other lots, which ar-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 203 

rangement was unsatisfactory to the Complainant. 

The Licensee tendered deed for lots numbered 78-80 Block "F" in the Atco plan, which Complainant refused to accept.

The sole question for determination by this Department is whether the Licensee has been guilty of a violation of the provisions of the Real Estate 

Brokers' , P. L. 1216, and particularly Section 10 (a) of the Act, which specifies and provides the grounds upon which a real estate broker's license may be suspended or revoked by the Department of Public Instruction, which agency of the Commonwealth is charged with the administration and enforcement of the Act.

We must accordingly determine whether the Licensee's conduct in this transaction constitutes a violation of the Act of 1929 which makes the Licensee amenable to discipline. Having found that Kalcheim, the president of the defendant Company, had represented that a dwelling would be erected for the Complainant by July 15, 1930, and that it would be properly financed by the Licensee for the Complainant, which promises and representations induced the Complainant to pay the Licensee $300-that the building was not even started, let alone completed, by the date specified-we believe this to be such a material violation of Section 10 (a) as is contemplated by the Act and for which violation the Licensee may be disciplined by this Department. The Complainant had a right to rely upon the representations and promises

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction made to him by Kalcheim, and when those promises were found to be false, whether proceeding from fraud or innocence, the resulting loss was the same to the Complainant. The Licensee in this case has failed to discharge his duties as a real estate broker with that degree of fidelity and trustworthiness which his calling merits and demands.

II 

Since it appears from the records of the Department that to date no application for a real estate broker's license has been made by the Licensee for the year 1931, there is no license to suspend or revoke, and the Department will not impose any penalty unless and until the said Homestead Development Company, Incorporated, or Elias A. Kalcheim make application for license. This Department will refuse to issue a real estate license to the Homestead Development Company, Incorporated, and to Elias A. 

Kalcheim, for a term of six months after application is made, and the same penalty applies to Lewis E. 

Harman, unless the said Harman severs his connections directly or indirectly with the Homestead Development Company, Incorporated.

Collecting rents and failure to account for the proceeds may be inquired into by the Department.

The complaint of Robert Gerhart, Robesonia, against N. E. Sutphen, Drexel Hill, is as follows:

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 205 

I I, Robert Gerhart, first duly sworn, deposes and says that rents collected have not been paid over to me. I own properties 7026, 7028, 7030, 7102, 7104, 7106, 7130 Emerson Avenue, and 7115 Atlantic Avenue, Stonehurst Hills, Delaware

County, Pennsylvania, which were left in Mr. Sutphen's hands for rental collections from April to September inclusive, 1930. That the rentals of said houses aggregated the sum of $262.50 per month and I am informed that he has made full collections, but he has made no remittance to me whatever, and refuses to account or answer communications satisfactorily.

County, Pennsylvania. We find that the Licensee collected rents for the properties in question from April to September, 1930, inclusive, aggregating a total rental of approximately $1600, and that said Licensee has refused and failed to remit or account for such rentals. The Licensee did not appear at the hearing on the complaint, nor was he represented by counsel, nor did he give the Department any notice or information concerning his failure to appear, although due and proper notice was given to him as to the time and place of hearing, as required by Section 10 of the 

Act of May 1, 1929.

Section 10 (a) of the Real Estate Brokers' License 

Act of May 1, 1929, P. L. 1216, specifies and provides the grounds upon which a real estate broker's

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction license may be suspended or revoked by the Department of Public Instruction, which agency of the Commonwealth is charged with the administration and enforcement of the Act. 

Having found that N. E. Sutphen collected rents for property belonging to the Complainant in Stonehurst Hills, Delaware County, and failed to account or remit the proceeds of such collection to the Complainant, we find that such conduct is a violation of the Act of 1929 as is contemplated by Section 10(a), and for which the said Sutphen may be disciplined by this Department. N. E. Sutphen makes application for license.

Restenberger vs. Conroy

Misrepresentations that properties are free and clear of encumbrances held sufficient grounds for revocation of license. Broker's profit out of transaction-evidence of bad faith.

The complaint of Elizabeth Restenberger, Allentown, against Edward J. Conroy and Albert J. Kline, Allentown, is as follows:

Elizabeth Restenberger, being first duly sworn, deposes and says that Edward J. Conroy and Albert J. Kline offered

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 207 

her premises #2203 Gordon St., Allentown, Pa., in exchange for her property in Freeland, Pa., the trade-in value fixed by Kline and Conroy being $17,000 and $4,000 in cash, stating that "Then you'll have a nice clear home." The price mentioned for the property on Gordon Street was $21,- 500, but Kline and Conroy stated that $500 would be deducted to allow her to make necessary improvements in decorations. As a consequence, she deeded the Freeland property to Kline and Conroy and gave them a check for $4,000 and she received the Gordon St. property. She was told that she had to sign the deed to the Gordon St. property and also a paper to move into the house, both of which she signed and which now appear to be a bond and mortgage in the extent of $10,000. In addition to the said mortgage of $10,000 there is a first mortgage against the premises of $15,000. Her first notice of any encumbrance occurred in 

September when the Lehigh Valley Trust Co. sent her a bill for $450 interest. She notified Conroy and he said that it must be a mistake and called her later in the day and said he had taken it up with the Trust Co. and that it was all a mistake. Thereafter she heard nothing until January 

20, 1931, when she was again notified regarding interest due. 

Since that time an offer has been made by Kline and Conroy to pay the interest for the next six months. In addition to the above transaction the said Kline and Conroy sold her six homes on Hanover Ave., Allentown, Pa., receiving therefor two properties located at #948-950 West Fourth St.,

Hazelton, Pa., the trade-in value of which was $12,000, and in addition thereto $12,000 in cash. At the time of this transaction the daughter, Daisy Restenberger, said, "Are they clear?" and Conroy replied, "Yes, don't worry about that." After the Hazelton properties had been transferred and the $12,000 check delivered, Daisy said, "That was a good buy at $4,000 each” and Conroy replied, "Oh no, there is $5,000 against each one." As of record there was a mortgage of $5,000 against each of five of these properties and one of $5,500 against the sixth one. Elizabeth Restenberger holds the cancelled checks above referred to.

An examination of the title to the property disclosed judgments and liens of such a large amount that the Complainant was advised against proceeding with the purchase of the property in question. J. made by the Licensee to obtain this money for the Complainant. Upon the failure of the Adams deal to materialize, the Licensee negotiated the exchange deal of said houses on Hanover Street for the two properties owned by the Complainant in Hazelton, and $12,000 in cash.

III We find from the evidence that the Complainant is a person of little business skill, experience, or sagacity, and that she relied implicitly upon the honesty and integrity of the Licensee. We also find that the

Street property, which was acquired by the Complainant as a residence for her own use. the Licensee to one Silvassi, a sister-in-law of Kline, at a consideration price of $9,000 in spite of the fact that these premises were taken in exchange at $17,000 (p. 52) and that the Hazelton properties, formerly owned by the Complainant, were deeded to a Miss Gabuta, similarly related to Kline, at a stated consideration price of $4,800. The deeds to these Hanover apartments went direct from Cassone to the Complainant, although Conroy had an agreement to purchase from Cassone, under the terms of which he was to give title to the Hazleton properties owned by the Complainant, and which of course the Licensee did not own.

VI We also find that in February or March of this year the Licensee attempted to obtain from the Com-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction plainant an instrument releasing himself and Kline from any and all claims that the Complainant might have against the Licensee and Kline in regard to any transactions that she may have had with them up to that time, in consideration of an advance of $500 to $600 to pay the interest on the mortgages on two of the properties on Hanover Avenue. Upon advice of counsel, the Complainant refused to sign the release. 

The Complainant in the above entitled case sets forth certain allegations concerning misconduct and fraud on the part of the Licensee. Consequently, the sole question for determination by this Department is whether such allegations have been proved to the satisfaction of the Department and whether they are of such a material nature as to constitute a vio lation of Section 10 (a) of the Real Estate Brokers' 

 (P. L. 1216), which

Section sets forth the grounds upon which a real estate broker's license may be suspended or revoked. 

It is not within the province of the Department to pass upon or criticise the business methods employed by a licensee, nor to dictate canons of business ethics; but a licensee must be ever mindful that the methods employed by him do not transgress the provisions of law. The evidence is clear and indubitable that the 

Licensee has, from the very beginning of his negotiations with the Complainant, embarked upon a course of action which had for its sole end and objec-

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 213 

tive the selfish pecuniary gain of the Licensee. In this he has succeeded all too well. He has enriched himself at the expense of the Complainant, who is now penniless. The methods employed by him were unethical, unscrupulous, and contrary to the provisions of the Real Estate Brokers' License Law. He has taken advantage of the culpability and gullibility of the Complainant to the extent of considerable money and property. The properties conveyed by the Complainant are now in the hands or control of the Licensee.

II There are certain duties which every agent owes to his principal, and the most important of these is loyalty to his trust. It is upon this duty that his other responsibilities rest. To be loyal to his trust, an agent must not sell to himself, purchase for himself, or purchase from himself. He should at all times diligently assert the rights of the principal whom he represents. 

The Licensee has demonstrated a woeful lack of knowledge of and utter disregard for these duties and responsibilities. He has violated all the duties of loyalty. Having found that the statements which the 

Licensee made concerning the Allentown properties were untrue that they were free and clear of encumbrances—such misrepresentations constitute a sufficient and material violation of the Act of May 1, 1929; and we would indeed be remiss in the administration of this Act and in our duties and responsibilities to the public, for whose benefit and protection this Act was passed, were we to permit such unethical and unscrupulous conduct to pass undisciplined.

Decisions of Department of Public Instruction 

III The evidence of wrongdoing and misconduct on the part of the Licensee is voluminous. This condition of misconduct pertained from the very first, and we are indeed convinced that the Licensee was of mal intent ab initio. He has attempted to fortify his position legally by a testimonial letter sent to himself at his request by the Complainant. Legitimate real estate brokers, subscribing to accepted standards of business, do not solicit letters of commendation from widow clients. This letter mitigates against the Licensee's case rather than in favor of it. Words cannot adequately censure the Licensee for his conduct in this transaction.

IV 

Real Estate Broker's License No. 4817 issued to

Edward J. Conroy is hereby revoked, this revocation to take effect July 16, 1931, unless an appeal is taken from this opinion. Then and in such case, revocation of license shall take effect thirty days from the final determination of such appealed action. 

June 12, 1931.

CHAPTER XII  Introduction 

WH HILE it is true that there have been a great many statutes enacted regulating the relation of landlord and tenant, nevertheless it must be remembered at the outset that the lease contract voluntarily entered into makes, to a large extent, the law by which the parties are governed. The parties are free to enter into a lease or not as they see fit, but once the lease contract is signed, neither party may repudiate it if subsequent conditions turn out to be different from what either party anticipated.

Form: Written and Verbal 

The lease contract may be verbal or in writing. 

The verbal contract is just as binding and as of great legal efficacy as the written contract, providing that the term is for three years or less. This is so under the old Act of March 21, 1772, known as the Statute of 

Frauds. It provides as follows:

Leases for over three years from the making thereof, and all estates or interests in lands or buildings erected thereon shall have the force and effect of leases or estates at will only, unless put in writing and signed by the parties making or creating the same, or by their agents authorized in writing. The act provides that these formalities shall be observed not only in the making or creation of the leases and estates to which it refers, but also in an assignment of them 215

to a third party and in release or surrender of them, unless the assignment, release or surrender is brought about by operation of law. 

For obvious reasons, a written contract is preferable to a verbal one. The tenure of human life is at best uncertain, and one or both of the parties to the contract may die during its term. In case of controversy or litigation, the surviving partner would not be permitted to testify in regard to the terms of the verbal agreement. This is so under the laws of evidence which prohibit the surviving party from testifying in a matter the other party to which is deceased. The reason for the rule is readily apparent: the person testifying could not if allowed to testify be contradicted by the contract itself or by the other party thereto.

When the lease contract is in writing, such instrument can then be introduced as evidence, and it speaks for itself in regard to the respective rights and liabilities of the contracting parties. Again, under the verbal lease, so long as the memories and recollections of the contracting parties coincide as to what was originally agreed upon, everything progresses smoothly, but as soon as their memories differ, then, in the absence of corroborating witnesses, there is no way of conclusively proving what was agreed upon.

Both parties therefore generally prefer a written lease. It may be a very simple instrument, but usually it is a lengthy contract with many finely printed clauses and covenants specifying and defining the respective rights and liabilities of the parties to the contract.

Parties to a Lease

The owner of the premises is referred to as the lessor or landlord, and the person having possession of the premises is called the tenant or lessee. The lease should be executed by the owner himself. Where the execution is delegated to an agent acting in behalf of the owner, it is important that the representative capacity in which the agent acts appears in the instrument. Agents

Where an agent executes a lease without disclosing his representative capacity, such lease would be open to attack by either lessor or tenant, and also could be disaffirmed by either party. Where the lease is executed by the agent as agent without disclosing the identity of his principal, the agent thereby assumes certain important personal responsibilities in connection with the contract. The tenant may hold the agent responsible for any breach of the lease, or the tenant may elect to proceed against the lessor for such breach when he ascertains the identity of such lessor. For the protection of the agent, it is preferable that the name of the lessor appear in the instrument so that in the event it is attacked, suit will be brought against the principal and not the agent -for example, John Steele (lessor), by his agent

Adam Brown. In this way, the agent who acts within the scope of the authority conferred upon him by his principal would be relieved of any and all liability in case a controversy should arise between lessor and lessee.

Minors

A lease executed by a minor, a person under twenty-one years of age, may be avoided by him unless it can be proved that such contract is a necessity. Only those contracts of a minor which are for necessaries are legally binding upon him. They include contracts for food, clothing, and shelter. Practically all other contracts are voidable by him at his option. He may disaffirm such voidable contracts at any time before arriving at his majority or within a reasonable time thereafter. The minor who owns property and leases it consequently could repudiate this contract at any time during the life of the lease, if the term of the lease did not extend beyond his minority. It must be remembered, however, that infancy is personal to the minor and can be asserted by him only. In other words, the other party to the contract may not disaffirm it on that account. Parties should deal with the guardian of the minor and not with the minor himself. Trustee

A trustee of land may grant leases which are unimpeachable as long as the trustee has remained within the power granted to him by the deed of trust. It is not necessary to secure an acknowledgment of the cestui que trust (beneficiary), and the beneficiary may not attack the instrument if the trustee has not exceeded the power conferred upon him.

Administrator An administrator, on the other hand, cannot execute a lease. He has been appointed by the court

for the purpose of winding up an estate, and has nothing to do with the renting of property or the collection of the rents. Similarly, an executor cannot lease property covered by the will unless he has been made a trustee under it. 

Distinction between Lease and License

Before proceeding to a detailed study of the law of landlord and tenant, it is necessary that a clear understanding be secured of the differences between a lease and one or two other legal relationships which closely resemble a lease. The first distinction to be noted is that between a lease and a license. The mere permission to occupy certain premises without pay on condition that the person living on the premises will leave whenever requested to do so by the owner does not create a tenancy. The reason for this brings out a very important distinction. The lease presupposes a payment or remuneration for its use to the owner of the property. If this payment is not provided for, or if no compensation is to be given to the landlord, it is as a general rule impossible to establish a contractual relationship of landlord and tenant. 

Lease Defined

The essence of a lease is the payment of rent, and we may define a lease as the contract, written or oral, for the possession of lands and tenements, on the one hand, in return for recompense of rent or other income, on the other hand. Suppose, for example, that

A arranges with B that if B would take care of A's property during his absence, he would allow B to occupy it free of cost, and use coal and other supplies which might be on the premises, with the understand-

ing that B would vacate at any time when A might desire to reoccupy the property. Such agreement between A and B is a mere license and vests no enforceable rights in B.

Distinction Between Lease and Agreement to Lease 

The next distinction to note is between a lease and an agreement to lease. It sometimes happens in practice that the parties enter into a preliminary agreement respecting property and postpone the execution of the lease proper until the time when the tenant takes possession. Suppose, for example, that in February A agrees to lease certain premises to B, beginning May first. No formal lease is executed in February. B merely pays the May rent and receives a receipt from A for such payment. In the event that the lessor fails or refuses to give possession of the premises to B upon the specified date, May first, an important question arises as to whether B can compel specific performance of the agreement to lease and compel A to give possession of the premises in question to B. The failure of A, the lessor, to perform his contract gives B a right of action against him to recover monetary damages for such breach. But it may very well be that monetary damages are not adequate compensation to him for the loss which he has sustained. He may have had important business reasons for desiring the property in question. For example, let us suppose that B is the present owner of the property at 100 and 104 Fifth Avenue, Erie, Pennsylvania, and that A, the owner of 102 Fifth 

Avenue, Erie, Pennsylvania, agreed in February to lease his premises to B, and on May first fails to do so. It can be readily seen that B has certain strategic

reasons for wishing to consolidate his business holdings, and monetary damages will not fully compensate him for A's default. Will the court decree specific performance under an agreement to lease in this case? While it is true that courts of equity will aid persons in interpreting the contract already entered into, it is likewise true that the courts will not create a contract for the parties. A person goes into a court of equity when he desires some special redress or remedy such as an injunction or, in this case, specific performance of the contract. To grant a decree of specific performance of the agreement to lease and to permit B to obtain possession of the premises under the facts stated would be to create a contract for the parties. For example, who would determine when the rent was to be paid; how long the lease was to run; the status of repairs; the question of assignability and the other numerous questions which ordinarily arise in connection with the interpretation of the contract? A court of equity therefore would not decree specific performance of the agreement to lease in this case because the agreement has not fully set forth the respective rights and liabilities of the contracting parties. In order to have an enforceable agreement to lease, it would be necessary that the main provisions of the lease proper be embodied in the agreement. It is preferable to have the lease proper executed at the time when the contract is consummated, even though possession is to be deferred until a later date. Under a loosely drawn agreement to lease, the prospective tenant's failure to obtain specific performance would not prevent him from obtaining monetary damages in an action at law. In a certain case, the defendant agreed to lease to the

plaintiff a hotel for three years from a succeeding date in April. Before that date the tenant invested a lot of money in furniture for the hotel, storing the goods until he could secure possession. He also sold his residence and much of his household furniture, since he intended to live in the hotel from then on. 

When the time for the lease arrived, the lessor refused to sign the contract, attempting to repudiate the agreement to lease. The result was, that the tenant, besides being deprived of whatever profit he might have made out of the hotel, was compelled to pay storage on his furniture and board himself and his family. He accordingly brought suit for damages under each of these heads, and since he was able to prove the agreement, secured an award covering the amounts involved. Description

In making a lease, it is not necessary to insert a minute description of the premises leased. The reason for this is apparent. In conveying properties by deed or in executing a mortgage, it is imperative that the premises be minutely described so that the purchaser or mortgagee may know accurately the limits of the property he is acquiring. In a lease, mere reference to street number and ward is sufficient. In the leasing of commercial property, it is advisable to incorporate a reference to the deed book in which is recorded the deed to the lessor. By reference to the deed volume the description contained in the deed is automatically incorporated in the lease. For example -"premises situate at 100 Fifth Avenue in the City of Pittsburgh, County of Allegheny and State of

Pennsylvania, and being more fully described in the

Recorder's Office for Allegheny County in Deed 

Book Volume 1987, Page 14." In renting a furnished property it is important to have a list of the furniture or chattels which are attached to the house made a part of the description. It is also advisable to insert a provision giving the landlord the right to inspect from time to time the furniture which the tenant is required to preserve in good condition. Term

Most leases of small resident properties are for the term of one year beginning May 1 and expiring 

April 30 of the following year. Where a lease is for a definite period, and the tenant remains in possession after the expiration of the designated period, he then becomes what is known as a tenant from year to year, and all the terms and provisions of the original lease continue to apply, with the exception of the term of the lease. If, for example, the original term of the lease was five years and the tenant remained in possession after the expiration of the five-year period, he could not contend that the lease was thereby continued for an additional five-year period. A tenant from year to year is entitled to three months' notice to vacate where the term of the lease is for the period of one year or longer. This is required under the Act of December 14, 1863. Where the lease is for less than one year, the tenant is entitled to thirty days' notice to vacate, under the Statute of March 31, 1905. 

Many leases contain a provision whereby the tenant waives this statutory notice. Another clause in the lease provides that if the tenant lawfully occupies the premises at the expiration of the term, then and in that case the lease shall be in effect for another

year, and so on from year to year. From this, the important and practical question frequently arises whether the lessor or the lessee may terminate the lease contract by notifying the other party on any day prior to the expiration date of his intention to cancel, or must notice be given three months' prior to the expiration date? Such a clause without modification would be interpreted to give the tenant the right of an option, and it would be for this party to the contract to determine whether a renewal of lease is desired, and such decision could be made at any time prior to the expiration date after the lessor had failed to notify the tenant of his intention to terminate. 

Tenant's Liability for Rent 

It is most advisable for the landlord to incorporate a provision in the lease whereby the tenant expressly covenants to pay rent. If this clause be omitted, an implied covenant would nevertheless ensue whereby the tenant would be required to pay rent for the use and occupancy of the premises. The advantage of an expressed covenant to pay rent can be readily seen where a tenant with or without the consent of the landlord assigns the lease to a newcomer to take possession. Should the new occupant fail or refuse to pay rent during the term of the lease, the landlord would have the right of holding the original tenant responsible for the fulfilment of the rent payments during the entire term. This is so under the expressed covenant clause in the lease. The only way in which the original tenant could be relieved from this continuing responsibility would be to have the original lease surrendered and destroyed and a new lease entered into by and between the lessor and the new tenant.

Assignment

Many leases contain a provision prohibiting a tenant from subletting or from assigning the lease without the written consent of the landlord endorsed upon the lease. All contracts fall into one of two classes: they are assignable or non-assignable. Those contracts involving personal skill and personal service are non-assignable; all other contracts are assignable. 

Consequently, in the absence of a restriction to the contrary, a lease is an assignable contract. A clause against assignment is strictly construed and will not be extended to include a prohibition against subletting; and vice versa, a covenant against subletting could not be enlarged to include a prohibition against assignment. A further distinction must be drawn between a voluntary assignment and an involuntary assignment, which takes place, for instance, through operation of law in bankruptcy proceedings. For example, A, the lessor, leases certain premises to B for ten years at a rental of $100 per month. At the expiration of five years, B becomes bankrupt and a trustee is appointed to liquidate his business. It is found at that time that the leasehold has a value of $200 per month. Consequently the lease represents a tangible asset of the bankrupt's estate and can be realized upon for the benefit of the tenant's creditors by the trustee in bankruptcy through an assignment at the increased rental for the balance of the term. 

The courts have held that such an assignment by an officer of the law is not a violation of the clause in the lease prohibiting an assignment, the theory of the law being that such action is involuntary as far as the tenant is concerned, and frequently is a matter over

which he has no control, and which may be arranged in opposition to his wishes. In order to guard against such a situation, a clause is frequently inserted to cover involuntary assignments as well as voluntary assignments. Such clause reads as follows: 

Nor assign this lease voluntarily or involuntarily, by judicial sale or otherwise, without the written consent of the lessor.

Repairs 

One of the important clauses in a lease which causes the most frequent controversy between the parties is that concerning repairs. It is imperative that a tenant when leasing property should satisfy himself that it is not only suitable and satisfactory for the purpose which he has in mind at the time that he enters into the lease contract, but also that the premises will continue in that condition during the term of the lease.

Under the law in Pennsylvania, there is no responsibility upon the landlord to make repairs, and should he make occasional repairs, such action may not be pleaded by the tenant as grounds for holding the landlord responsible for making subsequent repairs.

The rule of caveat emptor ("let the purchaser beware") holds good here with the same rigidity as is found in the case of all other relations between the landlord and the tenant. The tenant's eyes are his bargain, and he is in duty bound to inspect the premises in order to determine whether they are fit and suitable for the purposes he has in mind. The tenant, on the other hand, is not free from the responsibility of making repairs unless he has entered into a special

covenant with the landlord releasing him from the necessity of maintaining the property. A tenant holding for years is bound to make what are known as tenantable repairs, such as, for example, putting in windows and doors that have been broken by him or, if the property is a farm, repairing fences and private roads which are on the premises. The tenant is also required to keep the premises wind and water tight so as to prevent obvious waste and decay, but he cannot be forced to make general or lasting repairs which would put the property in a better condition than it was when he took possession. Where the landlord has agreed to make repairs and failed to do so, the tenant may not refuse to pay rent on that account. 

His obligation to pay rent is paramount to that of the landlord to make repairs. The extent to which a tenant is bound to repair has not received a very clear definition. It seems to be generally decided by the courts that the tenant cannot be bound to make good such deterioration as arises from necessary wear and tear incidental to the proper and ordinary use of the property, nor is he responsible for injuries resulting from inevitable accidents unless they can be remedied at slight expense, and unless to allow them to continue would result in serious damage to the property. The law seems to be well settled that the tenant is bound to repair all injuries which are occasioned by his negligence. A tenant cannot, however, be presumed to be under obligation to repair property unless it can be shown that he has been negligent in caring for the premises. 

In determining the liability of a landlord to his tenant for injuries sustained by the latter by reason

of the defective condition of the demised premises, there is a material distinction between an omission on the part of the landlord to perform a general covenant to make repairs and the negligent performance by him of repairs actually made or attempted. The former is a case of nonfeasance-the failure to perform an act which should be performed. The latter is a case of misfeasance-the performance of an act in an unlawful, injurious, or negligent manner. In the former case an action may be brought by the tenant on the covenant to recover the cost of making the repairs himself; but it is frequently, though not universally, held that no action will ordinarily lie against the landlord for injuries suffered by the tenant, or members of his family, because of the defective condition of the premises resulting from the failure of the landlord to make repairs in accordance with a general covenant to keep the premises in repair, as distinguished from a covenant to make a specific repair or to keep the premises safe, unless the injuries were caused by latent defects, known to the landlord, and fraudulently concealed by him at the time of the letting. The authorities are almost unanimous that it is the duty of the landlord, when he attempts in fact to make repairs or improvements upon the demised premises, whether the work be done gratuitously or under a covenant to repair, to exercise reasonable care that no injury results to the occupying tenant. 

If, therefore, repairs are made by the landlord in a careless and negligent manner, the tenant may recover for resulting injuries of which the negligence of the landlord in making the repairs was the proximate cause. (Kimmons vs. Crawford, 109 So. 585.)

Fire Clause 

In summing up the law in Pennsylvania, it may be said that the tenant is bound to return the premises in the same condition as they were when he took possession-reasonable wear and tear alone excepted. In this connection, it should be noted that the destruction of the leased premises by fire is a risk assumed by the tenant unless there is a clause to the contrary. 

Under the common law, if a fire occurred, the tenant was bound not only to continue the payment of rent during the term of the lease, but in addition he was legally obligated to rebuild the premises. Under present conditions, the premises are usually insured against fire by the lessor, and consequently the status of rent is the only one which arises in case that a fire occurs. The law in Pennsylvania is to the effect that the tenant must continue the payment of rent during the term of the lease even though he has lost the use of the premises which he rented. The clause frequently inserted in tight form leases to the effect"... to surrender the same in as good order as they now are, and accident by fire alone excepted" does not relieve the tenant from liability for the payment of rent. The clause merely relieves the tenant from the rebuilding of the premises. Where a fire occurs, the landlord is under no obligation to rebuild the property in the absence of an expressed agreement on his part to do so. Moreover, such an agreement is unusual. Not only is the reconstruction of property optional with the landlord, but the time at which he can begin operation and the speed with which the work is carried on lie wholly within his discretion. In order to guard against such a situation, which is manifestly unfair to

the tenant, a clause should be inserted in the lease providing for this contingency. A comprehensive form clause which may be used to good advantage reads 

In the event of the whole or partial destruction of the leasehold property by fire, the lessee may, at his option, terminate the lease and be relieved from further obligation thereunder, unless within thirty days after such fire the lessor shall by written notice to the lessee elect to rebuild or make good injuries caused by such fire within a reasonable time after the service of such notice, and which shall not be longer than six months from the happening of the fire, that is to say that the option of the lessors to continue the obligations of the lease are conditioned upon the service of such notice and on the ability of such lessors to complete and restore the property to its original condition within six months of the happening of the fire, and that in the meantime, payment of the rent hereby reserved shall cease except to the extent above specified.

It must be remembered, of course, that the clause just cited is one of voluntary agreement and is not a stipulation set forth by law. A tenant, however, is not obliged to repair any injury occasioned by an act of God or a public enemy. For example, if an earthquake occurred, the tenant would not be bound to rebuild because the act of God would have relieved him of the obligations imposed upon him by his contract. The same rule would apply in the case of an act of a public enemy. A tenant who had suffered at the hands of an invading army would be, for example, relieved of responsibility to his landlord because of the nature of the injury.

Additions, Alterations, and Improvements 

An important covenant contained in leases of business property is that which provides that all alterations, additions, and improvements made by the lessee upon the property shall remain until the end of the lease, at the option of the lessor. It is frequently further provided that the lessor shall have the option of requiring the tenant to restore the premises to their original condition. The meaning of these three words-alterations, additions, and improvements-has been the source of much friction, and it is practically impossible to lay down a general rule which would be applicable in all cases. The conflicts usually arise between the parties over machinery and other fixtures annexed to the leasehold. The tenant claims that such equipment are trade fixtures, and as such, personal property, which may be removed by him at the expiration of the lease. The landlord on the other hand contends that such property is included within the phrase "alterations, additions, and improvements." For example, if a tenant leased a store and installed shelving at considerable expense, which shelving enhanced the value of the building for renting purposes, the landlord would be within his rights in maintaining that such shelving constituted additions, alterations, and improvements, and require that it be left upon the premises at the expiration of the lease. On the other hand, if the shelving installed were of little value and would cost more to remove than it was worth, the landlord could insist that the tenant remove such fixtures and restore the premises to their original condition in accordance with the terms of the covenant in the lease. In insert-

ing a clause in connection with additions, alterations, and improvements, great care should be taken to define exactly what shall be understood by this term. 

If this is done, the possibility of future controversy will be largely eliminated. Termination

There are various ways by which a lease may be terminated. The usual method is, of course, by performance. Surrender

Where the tenant remains in possession until the expiration date and then removes from the premises, his rights and liabilities naturally cease. The parties, however, may enter into a mutual agreement to cancel the lease before the expiration date. This is called a surrender. It is a specific agreement, presumably mutually satisfactory to both, and believed to be to the advantage of both parties. Because of this fact, the courts have given wide range to the parties in making out such an agreement, and essentially it is not necessary that such an agreement be in writing or in any particular form, and no consideration need be included in order to make the contract binding upon the parties, the presumption being that the advantage accruing to both parties is sufficient to give it full force and effect. In order, however, to make a surrender complete, it is necessary that it be specifically accepted by the landlord. It is not sufficient that the tenant desires to relieve himself of the obligations under the lease, but there must be some positive act to show that the landlord coincided in this intention. It is important to note that a surrender destroys any

right which the landlord possesses to rent which has accrued but is not yet due. In other words, if the rent under a lease is paid quarterly, January 1 and April 1, and a surrender agreement is entered into on February 15, in the absence of an agreement to the contrary the landlord would forfeit his right to the six weeks' rent which has accrued but which is not due and payable until the following April 1. The landlord should insert a provision in the lease that he agrees to accept the surrender upon the payment of a fixed sum of money representing the amount of rent which has accrued up to the date of the surrender. 

Breach and Eviction

A lease may be terminated by breach of condition. 

Where one of the parties violates some important covenant in the lease, the other party may plead such act as grounds for termination of the contract.

Where the landlord is guilty of a violation, such breach is termed an eviction. It is a violent assertion of a right by the landlord as opposed to the rights which the tenant possesses. In other words, it is an unwarrantable ousting of the tenant by the landlord. 

The distinction between an eviction and an ejectment rests primarily upon this point. An eviction is a wrongful dispossession of the tenant. Ejectment is secured under some right which the landlord possesses either by implication or as the result of an expressed covenant. The action of ejectment is usually used to test the title. An eviction has a meaning somewhat different from that generally attributed to it by the layman. It need not necessarily be a forceable ousting or removal of the tenant by the landlord from possession of the premises. As used here in connection with

the law of landlord and tenant, it means the violation of any material covenant by the landlord which interferes with the tenant's quiet and peaceful enjoyment of the premises. Anything that would interfere with a tenant's beneficial and peaceful enjoyment of the leased premises would be, in the eyes of the law, an eviction. Suppose, for example, a landlord should go upon the premises for the purpose of making repairs, and there was no provision in the lease giving the landlord such right; the tenant could plead such action as sufficient grounds for cancelling the lease agreement. It is immaterial that the repairs to be made would benefit the tenant. So long as the work would be detrimental to the tenant's quiet and enjoyment, or would seriously interrupt his use of the property, he could plead it as grounds for terminating the agreement. Such action on the part of the landlord constitutes a question of fact which would have to be determined by a jury in an action at law. Suppose a landlord should enter the property which he has leased to a tenant for the purpose of obtaining lumber, or that he should take away something which has been part of the property at the time he delivered possession. In either event, he would be guilty of a technical eviction and the tenant could either overlook the act, in which case he would waive his right to complain, or he could plead that the act terminated the agreement, remove from the premises and be relieved of the liabilities under the lease. The same thing would be true were the landlord to secure an injunction restraining the tenant from using a portion of the premises or preventing him from subletting to another, nothing being stipulated in

the contract to warrant such action by the landlord. 

It must be remembered that the act complained of must be committed by the landlord or an agent representing the landlord or acting for him. The only ground upon which a tenant can plead eviction by a third party is in case the one whose act he complains of was exercising a right which he secured under a paramount title to that of the landlord. For example, if A leased to B and afterward it turned out that the title was vested not in A but in C, and C should proceed to dispossess B, the action of C would amount to an eviction. If, however, the party whose acts were complained of proceeded under a questionable or defective title, the tenant could not plead immunity on the grounds that an eviction occurred. An overt act committed by a third party is not an eviction in contemplation of law. The owner of a building, A, had leased the upper wall of the building to B for a term of three years for advertising purposes. B had erected an advertising sign upon the wall which could be seen by people passing in the vicinity. At the expiration of one year, C, the owner of the adjoining property, a one story building, erected an addition on the building in such a way that the view of B's wall was completely hidden from the view of passers-by, and its value for advertising purposes was therefore worthless. Consequently, B refused to pay rent on the grounds that an eviction had occurred, but the court disallowed the claim on the grounds that the injury had been inflicted neither by the owner nor by one having paramount title to the wall upon which the advertising was displayed, but by a third person, over whose actions A had no control.

1 

Tenant's Remedy 

If an actual or constructive eviction occurs, and the tenant chooses to terminate the agreement, all of the rent which is past due and payable becomes an obligation which must be settled by the tenant upon demand. This action on the part of the tenant terminates any right which the landlord has to demand rent after the date of the eviction. It is important to note that if the landlord should evict a tenant from a portion of the property, the tenant could on this ground evade the duty of paying rent on the portion which still remains to him. An eviction by landlord from part of the premises is, in the eyes of the law, an eviction from all of the property.

An important modification of this rule, however, exists in the case where a tenant is evicted from part of the premises by a paramount title invoked by a third party. In this case, the rent would be apportioned so that he would have to pay rent for that portion of the premises which still remained to him. If, however, the lessee takes the property with knowledge of the defective title of the lessor, it is then impossible for him to plead eviction as a defense against the payment of rent. After an eviction has occurred and the tenant remains in possession of the premises and takes no action which would indicate that he intends to hold the landlord responsible for the overt act, his continued occupation would constitute a waiver of the injury. When an eviction occurs, the tenant should promptly assert his rights in the matter. There are two courses which he may pursue: first, he may terminate the contractual relation between himself and the landlord by moving out and bringing suit for

damages which he has sustained; or, secondly, he may remain in possession of the premises and after promptly notifying the landlord of the injury suffered and stating that he will hold the landlord responsible for the damage, he may bring suit for the amount he claims to have lost. In this event, however, it is necessary for him to continue to pay rent in exactly the same manner as though no breach had ever occurred. Forfeiture

Corresponding to the tenant's right to terminate the lease by reason of breach of covenant by the landlord, is the landlord's right to terminate the lease where the tenant is guilty of a violation of a material covenant. This is known as a forfeiture. Under the common law, if a tenant should disclaim, disaffirm, or impugn the landlord's title by some positive act, he thereby forfeited all rights under the contract. The reason for this is obvious. If the landlord could not terminate the contractual relation, it might be possible for the tenant to work great harm to the property, not only by violating the spirit and letter of the agreement, but he might go even so far as to claim title to the property by adverse possession after continuous occupation for twenty-one years. The law, therefore, provides that the landlord has the option of declaring the lease forfeited upon the breach of any material covenant by the tenant. The landlord then has the right to enter and take possession of the property unless it can be shown that he has by some act waived the breach which has occurred. Suppose, for example, that a landlord should accept rent from a tenant for a period subsequent to the commission of the acts in

controversy. In this case, he would have waived his right to declare the contract forfeited by permitting the tenant to continue in possession.

Landlord's Levy 

It is important to note in this connection that distraint instituted for rent in default constitutes a technical waiver of the forfeiture which has occurred and permits the tenant successfully to maintain his right to the property under the terms of the lease. A landlord's levy is one of statutory enactment entirely. 

Under the common law, the landlord had no lien to the chattels of the tenant on leasing the premises or to the crops raised thereon. They were the absolute property of the tenant. He could do with them as he saw fit. It was not necessary for him to secure the consent of the landlord before selling them, nor could he be held accountable for the proceeds of the sale. In other words, the chattels and the crops of the tenant were as much his property as though he owned the property free and clear of all debt. It is by statutory law that the landlord has a method by which he can secure a lien upon the chattels and goods of the tenant. The right to distrain, however, exists in favor of the landlord only upon a claim for rent due and accrued. 

Distraint Defined

Distraint may therefore be defined as the right of a landlord to levy upon a tenant's goods and chattels for rent in arrears. The right does not reside in the landlord until there has been a default in rent, but he can bring this action the day after the rent is due. 

If the rent is due on the first day of the month, as is

generally true, and is not paid on that date, distraint proceedings may be instituted against the tenant on the second day of the month. 

Two Due Dates for Rent

In practice, however, the situation is often complicated by having conflicting due dates for the rent, the lease providing that the rent of $110 per month, for instance, shall be due and payable on the first day of the month, but if paid by the tenth of the month, there shall be a rebate of $10.00 per month, or a net rental of $100. Other leases provide that the rent shall be $100 per month, due and payable on the first day of the month, and if not paid by the tenth of the month, there shall be a penalty of $10.00 per month and the rent in that case shall be $110 per month. 

Since the rent is not in arrears until after the due date, the important practical question then arises as to when the rent is in default; is it on the second day of the month or must the landlord wait until the eleventh day before instituting his right to distrain, or is the rent not in arrears until the first day of the following month? It may very well be argued that even though the lease provides for the payment of rent on the first of the month that the effect of the second due date is to avoid the responsibility of payment on the first date, and the tenant is therefore within his rights in tendering the rent for the current month at any time during the month, and no lawful levy may be made until the succeeding month. Although a rebate clause is preferable to a penalty clause, there is no great difference in the legal rights resulting from the two clauses. In the absence of a supreme court decision defining the tenant's rights under a

lease with two due dates, it would appear that the rent is not in arrears until the following month. There may be a definite covenant in the lease to the effect that the rent shall be due and payable on the first day of the month irrespective of the second due date. The use of such a clause should be inserted for the benefit of the landlord.

Where a tenant in possession under a lease signs a new lease for the same premises before the expiration date of the old lease and then removes from the premises before the new lease becomes effective, it is not lawful for a landlord to distrain under the terms of the new instrument. For example, A leased certain premises from B under a lease expiring April 30, 1931. On April 6, 1931, a new lease for the same premises was entered into by the parties for the term beginning May 1, 1931, and ending April 31, 1932. 

The April 1931 rent was paid. On April 28, 1931, the tenant removed from the premises. It is not possible for the landlord to exercise his right of levy upon the tenant's goods and chattels for the reason that there is no rent due under the terms of the lease under which the tenant is in possession and the new lease has no legal status until May 1, 1931. The only remedy available to the disappointed landlord under such circumstances would be to wait until May 2, 1931, and then obtain judgment against the tenant for the term of the lease. The only possible way under which the landlord could proceed by landlord's levy under the terms of the new lease would be to have the old lease cancelled and surrendered as of April 6, 1931, and the new lease dated to take effect from 

April 6 to May 31, 1932.

Right to Levy for Rent not in Arrears

There are certain circumstances under which a landlord may levy when rent is not in arrears. Where the tenant has expressed or declared his intention to remove from the premises, the landlord generally is given the right by the terms of the lease to distrain. 

It is necessary for the landlord to prove intent on the part of the tenant, which is extremely difficult to do unless the tenant is found in the act of moving from the premises or has his goods packed preparatory to moving. Where the landlord relies merely upon the information given to him by neighbors or friends, he may find himself the defendant in an action for damages on account of an illegal distraint. 

At the time of the trial, he may not be able to produce the witnesses who have informed him concerning the tenant's intentions, or the memories of the informants may become very faulty. Consequently, it is a right which is not frequently invoked, as the law provides a severe penalty to the extent of double damages where the landlord levies when no rent is due (Section 3, Act of March 21, 1772).

Writ of Distress The first step in a distraint proceedings is the execution by the landlord of a writ of distress, or, as it is called, a landlord's warrant. The distraint proceedings may be initiated by the landlord himself or by a constable acting as his agent and bailiff. The constable will insist upon a written statement of the exact amount of rent due and will not proceed without such written statement, inasmuch as he is personally liable for damages in an illegal levy. A writ of distress is as follows:

To John Hays, 316 Bowman Street, Harrisburg, Pa.

Take notice that by the authority and on behalf of your landlord, Henry Steele, I have this day distrained the several goods and chattels specified in the schedule hereunto annexed in the house at 316 Bowman Street, Harrisburg,

Pa., County of Dauphin, for the sum of sixty dollars and no cents arrearage of rent due and in arrears, or replevy the aforesaid goods and chattels, according to law, within five days hereafter exclusive of this day I shall, after the expiration of the said time, cause the said goods and chattels to be appraised and sold according to law in such case provided. 

Given under my hand the twenty-eighth day of April, 1931. .. Constable.

If the services of a constable cannot be obtained for the service of this writ, the landlord may serve it himself or it may be served by his agent. To this notice is attached a form or schedule containing an inventory of the goods and chattels in the house which are distrained for the rent. In other words, the constable goes from room to room making an itemized list of the articles of furniture found there. A copy of this schedule of the goods must be posted or left with the tenant at the time of the service of the writ. Distraint, in other words, merely constitutes an impounding of the tenant's goods and chattels by the landlord, together with the serving of notice upon the tenant that unless the amount of rent which is due and payable is satisfied within five days, the landlord will take steps to enforce its collection and for that purpose will cause the sale of the goods upon which he has made levy. This right to distrain exists not only in favor of the landlord, but also can be claimed by an

assignee of the lease. Either class can exercise this right with equal facility. The law requires a landlord or his bailiff, the constable, to make a levy between sunrise and sunset. However, where a tenant clandestinely attempts to remove from the premises after dark, the landlord may himself serve a warrant of distress or have his agent, the constable, do so at that time. In the case of Pickering vs. Greene, 31 Superior Court 280, where a tenant bought furniture from a dealer and then removed from the house leaving the furniture, which was seized in payment of the rent, the court held that the levy on such furniture by the landlord after sunset for rent in arrears when the dealer was removing it from the premises was valid, as evidence showed the constable had made several ineffectual attempts to make a levy between sunrise and sunset, but was prevented from so doing because the house was locked. 

Right to Force an Entry 

The presence of a constable is necessary when the appraisement and sale take place, so that it is preferable to have this officer take charge of the proceedings from the very beginning. In making a distress, neither the landlord nor his bailiff, the constable, may break open an outer door or break open or tear down gates or enclosures for that purpose. Either the landlord or the constable may, however, enter an open door or window for the purpose of making levy, and inner doors may be broken if an entrance has been lawfully effected. If distraint has once been made, and either the landlord or constable is refused admittance afterwards for the purpose of proceeding with the levy, he may effect an entrance by force if necessary.

A breach of the peace, however, should be avoided. 

There is a question as to whether or not the landlord or constable may lift a latch of a door to open it in order to make distraint. Some decisions hold that when a man merely latches his door, he may be regarded as inviting persons to enter his house who have business therein. In New York, however, it has been held that opening a door constitutes a breaking within the meaning of the law. Lifting a latch is as much a breaking as the forcing of a door locked by a bolt; the fastening is enough. In the case of a place of business when every one having business to transact with the proprietor has an invitation to enter by lifting the latch during business hours, it has been held that a constable can enter to distrain; but in the case of a dwelling house, the usual procedure is to knock or ring, and if there is no response, the conclusion will be that the proprietor is not at home. To allow a constable to lift a latch and to enter a dwelling house with no one about would be a dangerous extension of his powers and one not contemplated by law. As long as the rule is to be made for all kinds of buildings, the New York rule seems to be the proper one to adopt.

Posts Inventory 

When the constable lawfully enters the premises, he gives notice of his claim for rent and declares that the goods which he names shall not be removed from the premises until the rent be paid, and he makes an inventory of the goods levied upon, leaving a copy of the inventory, as well as the writ with the tenant or some adult member of the tenant's family, or, if no

one is there, he posts a copy of the notice and inventory in a conspicuous place. If the goods are removed after levy, treble damages and costs of suit may be recovered. The landlord or constable may, if he desires, place a watchman in charge of the goods to prevent a sale or removal, but the landlord must pay the watchman. The goods may be removed, but the tenant should give notice of the place to which the goods are removed. It is usual to leave the goods on the premises, the landlord taking the risk of removal. The law gives the landlord considerable latitude in making a levy for overdue rent. Suppose, for example, that a tenant who had punctually met his obligations to the landlord should assign his lease, with the latter's consent, to another and should leave a portion of his chattels on the premises with the consent of the new tenant. If the second tenant should default in the payment of rent, the landlord could proceed to levy upon the goods of the first tenant as well as upon the goods of the party then in possession for satisfaction of his claim. The fact that the first tenant was under no obligations to the landlord would have no bearing upon the case.

All Goods May Be Levied Upon 

The general rule is that all goods found on the premises, irrespective of ownership, may be distrained upon. It is important to note how far-reaching this rule is. It is unnecessary to inquire whether the title of the goods upon the premises is vested in the tenant. 

As long as they are found upon the premises at the time that the levy is instituted, they are subject to sale by the landlord in satisfaction of his claim.

Statutory Exceptions Goods of a Warehouseman 

To this general far-reaching principle of law, there are certain statutory exceptions or exemptions. The first of these includes chattels found upon the premises by reason of the tenant's trade or business. Where a tenant has in his possession goods belonging to other people which have been given to him in the ordinary course of his business, such cannot as a rule be levied upon. This rule, for example, includes goods which are deposited in a warehouse or which are entrusted to a commission merchant for sale. For example, A placed his household goods in storage with the B Company, tenants of C. The B Company defaulted in the payment of rent, and C issued a landlord's warrant. C could not sell the goods of A because they were upon B's premises by reason of the latter's trade or business. Where the goods of a stranger are in the possession of a tenant, not as a necessity of the latter's business or trade, but as a matter of favor, they are not within the principle exempting them from distress for rent as are the goods of a stranger which are necessarily in the demised premises in the way of trade or business. For example, A, a neighbor of

B, requested permission from B to store a very valuable grand piano with B temporarily. A was to pay no charge for this service, and B was to have the use of the piano during the time that it was in his possession. B defaulted in the payment of rent to C, his landlord, and C levied on A's piano as well as upon other goods belonging to B. The levy was legal and it was therefore necessary for A, the owner of the

piano, to pay B's rent in order to release the piano from the levy. In another case, A consigned a machine to B, a tenant of C, for trial, with a view to a subsequent sale. B sold out his business to D and stored the machine with E, who occupied another story in the same building. E was not a warehouseman, but permitted the machine to be placed on his premises as an accommodation to B. C distrained upon the machine in E's custody for rent due from D. Since E was merely a subtenant, all of his goods were liable for the rent. A brought replevin, however, maintaining that the machine was exempt from distress as property delivered to B in the necessary way of trade, or, in all events, as property deposited with E on storage merely. The judgment was for C, the landlord, the court holding that the machine was liable for the rent under the general rule of law. 

Goods of a Boarder 

The second exemption covers goods of a boarder who takes his meals at the house of the tenant. If, however, the boarder merely lodges at the tenant's house, he is not protected by the rule and his property, except such goods as he actually uses, is subject to distraint for rent due by the tenant to the landlord. 

If a tenant takes in boarders who are admitted into the family circle and get meals from the boarding housekeeper, such of their goods as they actually use will not be distrainable. A leading case on the subject is Riddle vs. Weldon, 5 Wharton 9. This was a case of replevin by A, the boarder, against B, the landlord, founded on distress for rent due from C. 

C kept a boarding house. A boarded with C, and the goods levied upon were the goods of A and were in

the two rooms of A, which he occupied as a boarder. 

The court held that they were exempt from distress. 

It must be remembered, however, that goods of a boarder are exempt only to the extent such articles are actually used by him. For instance, D leased certain premises from B. A went to live with 

D and took with him sufficient furniture, not only to furnish his private room, but also to furnish the balance of the house. The furniture was used by D and his boarders in common. As D was in arrears for rent, B distrained on A's furniture so used in common, whereupon A brought suit against B for an illegal distress. A contended that, since he was merely a boarder, all of his goods on the premises were exempt from levy. The court held that all of the furniture on the premises not in actual use by A, and necessary for his boarding and lodging as a boarder in the house, was liable to distress for rent due by D. 

In another case, C arranged with B to live in the house and pay fifty cents for each meal he might take, and twenty dollars a month for a room to be furnished by himself. C ate very few meals at B's table but continued to rent the room, getting his meals elsewhere. A distrained on and sold C's goods for rent, notwithstanding notice of C's ownership and his arrangement with B.
