In an action by C, the court held that to constitute one a boarder, he must constitute one of the family of the boarding housekeeper. Consequently, the levy was proper, as the goods were not exempt.

Goods in Custody of Law; Bankruptcy or Death 

Goods in the custody of the law are exempt from levy. Where bankruptcy proceedings have been in-

stituted against the tenant, no distraint proceedings may be brought for rent in arrears. The landlord then becomes a preferred creditor to the extent of one year's rent, and it is immaterial to determine whether such rent is in arrears or in the future. If the lease contract contains a provision whereby the tenant waives the benefit of the exemption laws, it is advisable for the landlord in filing his claim for one year's rent with the referee in bankruptcy to file a stipulation setting forth the waiver by the tenant of his rights under the exemption laws. If this is not done, the referee may permit a tenant his statutory exemptions and where this is allowed, may operate to prevent the landlord from any recovery on account of insufficient funds remaining for distribution. 

In event of the death of a tenant, a landlord is prevented from pursuing his remedy of distress for rent due him. Here again he becomes a preferred creditor to the extent of one year's rent. His claim should be filed with the executor or administrator of the tenant's estate. The law has provided that funeral expenses, medical attendance, servant's wages for a period not to exceed one year shall have precedence over other claims in the order stated. The landlord's claim for rent for a period not to exceed one year comes next on the list, being followed by all other debts which may be chargeable against the estate. Fixtures 

The fourth exemption is very obvious. Fixtures which cannot be removed are not distrainable because they are as a rule more nearly the property of the landlord than of the tenant. Such fixtures which are annexed or affixed to the realty in such a manner as

to constitute a part of the freehold would inevitably remain part of the premises at the expiration of the lease and consequently belong to the landlord. Fixtures which can be removed, and are therefore considered as personal property, are subject to levy. 

Personal Belongings and Implements of Trade 

The fifth exemption includes bibles, school books, and wearing apparel from liability to be seized in satisfaction of a claim for rent. Implements of trade which are used by the tenant in prosecuting his trade, such as carpenter tools and plumbing tools, are exempt from this liability. It is not the policy of the law to take away from a tenant his last means of eking out a livelihood.

Perishable Goods 

The next exemption covers perishable goods, the impounding of which would result in their destruction. When it is realized that landlord's levy proceedings take at least thirteen days from the beginning to the end, it can be readily seen that if foodstuffs were impounded, they would have no value at the time of the sale. $300 Exemptions

The law has also provided that household goods of a tenant to the amount of $300 are free from liability unless this exemption is waived in the lease, which is generally done. While the Act of April 10, 1849, was passed for the protection of the tenant, the courts have uniformly held that the tenant may waive the benefits guaranteed to him by this statute. Frequently the goods found upon the premises do not

have a value equivalent to $300, so that the landlord's right to prosecute his levy would be useless unless the tenant agreed to a waiver of the benefits of this Act.

Sewing Machines; Typewriters 

The list of exemptions also includes sewing machines and typewriters in use.

Leased Pianos, etc. 

Frequently articles of furniture and equipment are purchased on credit under what is called a bailment lease or installment contract. The buyer obtains possession of the article purchased but title remains in the seller until the last installment is paid towards the purchase price.

The Act of May 13, 1876, exempted leased pianos, melodeons and organs from distress for rent. The act required the owner of such an instrument to notify the landlord of the lease on the article. The notice required should be given to the landlord when the leased instrument is placed on the premises or at least before the right to distrain accrues. The owner of a leased piano is liable for damages in removing it from the premises of the lessee after the landlord has levied upon it.

Soda Water Apparatus 

The Act of April 28, 1899, exempts soda water apparatus and the appurtenances thereto when levied upon under a lease or installment contract where notice of such lease is brought to the attention of the landlord or prominently displayed upon the article itself.

Electrical Equipment 

Certain leased electrical apparatus (dynamos, motors) have been similarly exempted by statute where a notice of the lease is brought to the attention of the landlord or prominently displayed upon the article itself.

Release by Landlord 

The furniture dealers in Pennsylvania have for a long time been endeavoring to obtain the passage of an act exempting from a landlord's levy furniture which has been sold under a lease or bailment contract to a tenant, but they have so far been unsuccessful in their attempts. The only way by which the leased furniture may be exempted from a landlord's levy is to obtain from the landlord a written release wherein he agrees not to proceed against such leased furniture for rent. Mere notice that the furniture in question is leased, that the title does not belong to the tenant, is insufficient to exempt the furniture. 

Where notice of the leased furniture is given to the landlord and a release obtained from him, such a release is a covenant between the landlord and the furniture company, and would not bind a subsequent purchaser of the property. In other words, it is not such a covenant as runs with the land, and the purchaser could levy upon the furniture. 

On the other hand, where furniture or equipment have been purchased by the tenant on an installment lease and a release is obtained from the landlord exempting such leased articles in event of a levy, the landlord may be bound by this release in case the lease is assigned subsequently to a new tenant who takes

over the contract of purchase on the leased equipment.

Tenant's Right to Replevy The writ of distress provides that the tenant shall have five full days within which to replevy the goods and chattels levied upon. In other words, if the tenant has any valid reason for not paying the rent or claims that no rent is in arrears, he may proceed to release his property from the landlord's levy by posting bond for double value of the goods seized. This must be done wthin five days of the service of the writ of distress. Cash, personal, or a real property bond may be posted for the necessary amount. Where real estate is offered as security for the replevin bond, the value of such real estate is figured at the difference between the assessed valuation and the encumbrances. Suppose, for example, that a bond in the sum of $2,000 must be posted and real estate worth $10,000 is offered for the replevin bond. If the assessed valuation of such real estate is $7,000, and there is a $6,500 mortgage on record against such property, the real estate would not be satisfactory for the bond. When the bond is posted, the property is released from levy and the tenant is free to dispose of such personal property in any manner that he sees fit. The case then comes up for trial in the ordinary manner, and if the landlord is successful in his claim, he realizes satisfaction on the bond which has been posted as security. Appraisement

The law provides that after a lapse of five full days succeeding the service of notice of distress, the con-

stable shall cause the chattels to be appraised by two reputable citizens. If the last day falls on Sunday or a holiday, no lawful appraisement can be made until the succeeding day. If the notice of distress is served on February 1, the appraisement, together with notice of sale would be made on February 7, provided that February 7 is not Sunday, in which case the appraisement would be made and sale advertised on February 8. In calculating the six days which must intervene between the appraisement and sale, the first day is excluded and the last day included. In other words, if the appraisement were made February 8, the sale could not take place until February 14, and if this day were Sunday or a holiday, not until the fifteenth. Sale

It is very important that the constable carefully observe the requirements of the law in this procedure because if he takes a wrong step at any time in the proceedings, he becomes a trespasser ab initio (from the beginning) and is then amenable in damages to the injured tenant. A postponement of the sale is permissible. The consent of the tenant to a postponement should first be obtained. The goods must be sold separately or in parcels calculated to bring the highest price. The entire stock of goods must not be sold in mass. If sold in too large parcels, the injured parties can recover damages. $300 Exemption 

If the tenant has not waived his $300 exemption, he must request the constable to summon three distinguished and competent persons who are sworn or

affirmed to appraise such property as the tenant may elect to retain to the value of $300 as determined by such appraisement. It must be remembered that this is a separate and distinct appraisement from that by two reputable freeholders. Where the tenant waives the $300 exemption in the lease, no such appraisement is, of course, made.

War Measure-Act of May 3, 1917 

Section 50 of the Act of May 3, 1917, provided that no civil process should be issued against any officer or enlisted man of the Pennsylvania National Guard in the active service of the Commonwealth or of the United States during so much of the term as he shall be engaged in active service under orders nor until thirty days after he shall have been relieved therefrom. The purpose of this provision was to protect tenants engaged in military or naval service of the United States government. New forms of lease printed since the passage of this Act had a printed clause inserted to the effect that the lessee expressly waives and renounces the benefit of any and all stays of execution and exemption from civil process under any law of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania or of the United States now in force or hereafter to be passed, and particularly, under the acts of General 

Assembly of Pennsylvania approved April 18, 1861, (P. L. 408) and May 3, 1917, exempting from civil process persons in the military service of the State or of the United States. It is a question whether the benefits of these acts can be waived by the lessee. In view of the fact that the benefits of the Act of 1849 can be waived by the lessee, as has been held in numerous cases, it is not unreasonable to believe that

the courts would take the same attitude upon the acts in question. With the termination of the 

World War, the question ceased to become important, and the waiver clause is not now inserted in leases.

May Follow Goods for 30 Days 

Where a tenant has violated the terms of the lease by moving out before the expiration date without paying rent for the balance of the term, the landlord is permitted by law to follow and distrain upon the tenant's goods and chattels for a period of thirty days. This right to follow the tenant's goods for the space of thirty days is also generally provided for in the lease agreement. It is a remedy, however, which, for two reasons, is not generally resorted to in practice. In the first place, it is necessary for the landlord to prove that the goods which are followed and distrained upon are the identical goods which were on the leased premises during the term of the lease. It happens very infrequently that a landlord is sufficiently familiar with the household goods and chattels of the tenant to be in a position to prove this condition. In the second place, it is necessary for the landlord to prove that the goods followed are the property of the tenant. Usually the tenant claims that title and ownership to the articles in question are in the name of his wife, and it is extremely difficult for the landlord to disprove this claim. In a certain case, the husband alone signed the lease and subsequently defaulted in payment of rent. The goods were being removed from the leased premises when levied upon by the landlord. Most of the goods were on a truck in front of the premises preparatory to being moved

1 7 1 1, 1 1 or e, on -k away. The constable executing the warrant levied upon the goods and chattels in the truck, which were property of the wife. The wife then executed a sixtyday promissory note for $140, the rent in question, whereupon the constable permitted the goods to be moved, and the levy was withdrawn. When the note matured, the wife refused payment on the note for the reason that the goods levied upon were her property and were off the premises at the time of the levy and therefore not subject to the distraint proceedings, so that there was no valid consideration to support her promise to pay the $140 in question. She contended further that as a married woman she was exempt by law from executing an instrument for the accommodation of another.

There are two types of defenses which may be advanced against payment on a negotiable instrument at maturity. The one type is known as personal defenses, which are good as between the immediate parties to the instrument, and the other is known as real or absolute defenses, which may be asserted by the maker against any person-a holder in due course as well as the original payee. Absence or want of consideration is a personal defense only, and so long as the note was in the possession of the payee, the landlord, at maturity, the wife was within her rights in refusing to pay the instrument on that account. The constable exceeded his authority when attempting to levy on goods which were off the premises at the time of distraint. Since there was no legal consideration for the note, the wife was not liable. Therefore when a tenant removes from the leased premises before the expiration of the lease, the landlord will usually follow his right to confess judgment under the terms

of the contract, and seek to obtain satisfaction by issuing execution against the tenant. 

Confession of Judgment 

Most leases provide for a confession of judgment clause which reads as follows: 

And in case of violation of any of the covenants or agreements in this lease by tenant, the said tenant further at the option of the said lessor authorizes and empowers any such attorney either in addition to or without such judgment for the amount due according to the terms of this lease to appear and confess judgment forthwith against tenant and in favor of lessor in an amicable action of ejectment from the premises above described with all the fees, release, waivers of stay of execution and exemption to accompany said confession of judgment in ejectment as are set forth in said confession of judgment for said sums due. 

The difference between an ordinary lease and a "tight" form lease rests primarily on this point. Under an ordinary lease contract, where the tenant violates a covenant by removing from the premises, it would be necessary for the landlord to institute suit before reducing his claim to judgment. The expenses of an attorney and litigation are thus incurred. Under the "tight" form lease, judgment may be had without the lawsuit or even personal service upon the tenant. In other words, the same distinction which exists between action on a promissory note and a judgment note applies here. Where a judgment has been entered against a tenant and the tenant believes that such action is unwarranted, he may proceed by petition to show cause why the judgment should not be opened up to air the entire matter. Where a landlord deals with a tenant who is comparatively unknown

to him, it is advisable to have the tenant's wife, if he is married, join in the execution of the lease. 

The Superior Court of Pennsylvania has held that household goods of a stranger which are not in the house distrained by the landlord, but are temporarily on the sidewalk in the course of removal, are not liable to distress by the lessor. The wife occupies the position of a stranger in this connection. 

It is often the case that the funds of the family are kept in the name of the wife and that issuing execution on a judgment against the husband would be futile. Also, where the landlord desires to exercise his right to pursue a tenant's goods which have been removed from the premises, his right to proceed against goods of the wife would be guaranteed to him where she joins in the execution of the lease. This affords him additional protection and security. Surety

Where the tenant is a person not financially responsible, it is well for the landlord to insist upon some person of means and financial responsibility to enter upon the lease as surety for the faithful performance of the conditions and covenants contained therein. Such a clause reads as follows:

For value received, I agree to be bound absolutely to the lessor in the foregoing lease as long as the liability of the tenant continues under said lease or the renewal thereof; that the covenant of the tenant will be properly kept and that any default therein as to payment of rent or otherwise, immediate recourse may be had to Albert Harris by suit or otherwise, and I agree to pay to the lessor such sum or sums of money as will be sufficient to make up any deficiency and fully satisfy the conditions of this agreement without requiring any notice of non-payment or proof of demand

being made.

All exemption laws as to property are hereby waived. 

Witness my hand and seal this first day of May, A. D., 1931. 

Albert Harris (seal)

Demand must first be made upon the tenant, who is primarily responsible on the lease, and if the landlord fails to receive satisfaction from him, the surety, who is secondarily liable, may then be held liable for the fulfillment of the terms and conditions contained therein. If the lease contract is renewed for a new term, it is important that the same terms and covenants prevail because a change in the original conditions would terminate the surety's responsibility. 

Renewal Clause 

Most forms of leases contain a provision regulating the continuance of the tenant upon the contract after termination of the term fixed. Such a clause reads as 

If the tenant lawfully occupies the premises after the end of the term, this lease shall be in force for another and so on from to relation of landlord and tenant continues. as long as the 

This provision therefore recites that unless the tenant or the landlord gives certain notice of his intention to terminate the lease, it shall be extended for the further named period. If there is no such clause, the landlord ordinarily may treat a tenant who holds over as a trespasser or as a tenant from year to year. If the landlord does not proceed to remove the tenant after the expiration of the term and accepts rent from him, it is taken for granted that the provisions

of the old lease apply so far as they are consistent with a lease from year to year. When a lease is for a certain definite term and no provision has been inserted giving the tenant the right to hold over, the tenancy expires upon the expiration of the time for which it was granted, and no notice to quit is necessary to enable the landlord to recover possession in ejectment or to enable the tenant to quit without incurring liability for subsequent rent. If the tenancy is for an indefinite period, either at sufferance or at will, it is necessary for the landlord to give the tenant formal notice of his intention to terminate the contract, in accordance with the Act of March 31, 1905, and the Act of December 14, 1863, requiring thirty days' notice and three months' notice to vacate respectively, depending upon whether the lease is for one year or less. Some landlords and real estate brokers take the extra precaution of serving quit notices upon the tenant three months before the expiration date, even where the lease provides for waiver of such notice. A common form of such notice to quit reads as follows: 

John Steele, being in possession of certain premises with the appurtenances situate at 100 Fifth Street, Harrisburg,

Pa., which you now hold as a tenant under John Roland, I hereby give you notice that it is my desire to have again and possess the same at the expiration of your current term, to wit, on the 30th day of April, 1931.

It is necessary for an agent giving notice to quit to a tenant to have the authority granted to him by the landlord, and if he should give such notice without possessing the authority, it would be impossible for the landlord to validate the agent's action after

the date on which a valid notice could have been given. 

For example, if an agent without authority served a notice to quit on January 25, 1931, it would be impossible for the landlord to ratify such unauthorized act in February, 1931, where the expiration date of the lease was April 30, 1931. The landlord, in other words, must ratify his agent's acts prior to the time upon which he would have been precluded from serving the notice on the tenant himself. It is also important to note that a notice to quit should be given by the landlord to the lessee and not to the subtenant holding under the lessee. If the tenant is not in possession, the landlord has a right to enter for the purpose of securing possession of the property and may remove any goods either to the sidewalk or to the highway, or he may have them stored at the expense of the tenant. If a tenant however is in possession, it is usually necessary for him to be careful not to use more force than is actually necessary to accomplish the object which he has set forth to perform. 

If he uses unnecessary roughness or violence, he throws himself open to the possibility of a damage suit being brought by the tenant. It should be noted in this connection that the right of forceable entry sometimes exists in favor of the tenant, as in a case where he has been wrongfully evicted and desires to repossess himself of the property.

In addition to this common law remedy of forceable entry, certain statutory rights providing for summary proceedings have been given to the landlord which do not supersede, but merely assist the landlord in securing possession of the property. The right of ejectment is the most important of these legal remedies which have been provided for him. In

the event of a tenant's failing or refusing to give possession of the premises to the landlord upon the expiration of his term, the latter may appeal to the court for assistance in securing possession of the property. 

The court will fix a day for the hearing of the matter and if the tenant fails to appear, or is not able to establish a satisfactory defense, the court will issue what is known as a writ of ejectment which directs the tenant to surrender possession of the property upon a given day. If the tenant should fail to obey this order, he would put himself in contempt of court, which is one of the most serious offenses which a man can commit. Ejectment, therefore, instead of being a violent physical encounter, as the word would imply, is nothing more or less than a legal proceeding by which the landlord, through the instrumentality of the court, again secures possession of that which lawfully belongs to him. This writ is mostly used in case the parties to the controversy bear to each other the relation of landlord and tenant. If this relation does not exist or cannot be easily implied, the landlord must take some other method of securing possession of the premises. However, he has the right to eject a sublessee or an assignee of the original tenant or one holding under him. 

In Pennsylvania, summary proceedings of ouster are limited to tenancies for years or at will, and only lie in cases where a certain definite rent was reserved by the lease. If the amount of the rent is indefinite or indeterminable, summary proceedings is not the proper remedy to pursue. Thus summary proceedings in like manner cannot be brought against a tenant holding over after a forfeiture of his term because of the fact that the law presumes that the original agree-

ment which would have been the basis of the action no longer exists. However, where the lessor may, according to the terms of his lease, terminate the contract at his option, after exercising such option he may maintain summary proceedings and eject his tenant. In the same manner, he may also oust a tenant who has failed to pay his rent for the specified time, but if he should be foolish enough to take rent for subsequent months when there is rent in arrears, he waives his right to bring summary proceedings for the rent past due and in arrears. 

The tenant has only two defenses to an action of ejectment. In the first place, he may introduce any evidence which may show that his possession of the property was lawful. He might claim, for example, that he had a paramount title to that of the person claiming himself to be his landlord, or that he was holding under rights secured to him by adverse possession; or secondly, he may introduce any evidence tending to show that the relationship of landlord and tenant does not exist between him and the person bringing the action. If he succeeds in establishing either point, he defeats the action of the landlord.
